Logarithmic motivic homotopy theory
Federico Binda, Doosung Park, Paul Arne Østvær

Abstract
. This work is dedicated to the construction of a new motivic homotopy theory for (log) schemes, generalizing Morel-Voevodsky’s (un)stable 𝔸 1 \mathbb{A}^{1} -homotopy category. Our setting can be used to represent log topological Hochschild and cyclic homology, as well as algebraic K K -theory of regular schemes, and to realize the cyclotomic trace as a morphism between motivic spectra. Among the applications, a generalized framework of oriented cohomology theories allows us to produce new residue sequences for (topological) Hochschild, periodic and cyclic homology of classical schemes, to compute THH \mathrm{THH} and variants of Grassmannians, and to define a new version of algebraic cobordism. Finally, we give a construction of a log étale stable realization functor, as well as a Kato-Nakayama realization functor, which are of independent interest for applications in log geometry.



[name=notation,title=Index of Notation]

1.
Introduction

The term “motivic homotopy theory” was coined around twenty years ago to denote a unified framework for using methods borrowed from algebraic topology to treat objects that traditionally existed in the realm of algebraic and arithmetic geometry
[
70
]
,
[
98
]
.
An aspect that guided this construction is the idea that the affine line
\mathbb{A}^{1}
plays the same role in algebraic geometry as the unit interval
[0,1]
in algebraic topology.

The main purpose of this work is to change this perspective and develop a homotopy theory for algebraic varieties without imposing the axiom of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy invariance. This is a follow-up of our previous work on the subject
[
22
]
, where we developed a cycle-theoretic perspective through log correspondences over a field.
In this paper, we construct analogues of the Morel-Voevodsky categories
\mathcal{H}(S)
and
\mathcal{SH}(S)
, over a general base, and prove that many generalized cohomology theories of arithmetic interest (such as topological Hochschild homology
\mathrm{THH}
) can be represented in our setting, while being not representable in the original motivic framework.

We point out that this paper has a “companion” in the form of an expository article
[
21
]
,
where we motivate and summarize some basic results.

\mathbb{A}^{1}
\square
\mathbb{P}^{1}

Figure 1.
The logarithmic interval
\square
is contractible as
\mathbb{A}^{1}
and compact as
\mathbb{P}^{1}

1.1.
Why avoid
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance?

One of the most successful tools in the study of the algebraic
K
-theory of a ring is the theory of trace methods, that is, the study of trace maps from
K
-theory to much more computable invariants derived from Hochschild homology, such as
\mathrm{THH}
and
\mathrm{TC}
, and in the analysis of their (homotopy) fibers.

Unfortunately, it is not so hard to show (going back to the work of Weibel-Geller
[
43
]
) that if we apply Voevodsky’s
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-localization functor
L_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
we have
L_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\mathrm{THH}\simeq L_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\mathrm{HH}\simeq 0
. Therefore, regardless of any assumption of regularity, it is impossible to extract information about (topological) Hochschild homology from Voevodsky’s framework. The situation for
\mathrm{TC}
does not improve, since a result of Elmanto
[
38
]
shows that
L_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\mathrm{TC}
vanishes after profinite completion. These kind of no-go results clearly show that, at a very fundamental level,
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-motivic homotopy theory is incompatible with trace methods.

1.2.
Why log geometry?

Our setting for overcoming this problem is to work in the context of logarithmic geometry
in the sense of Deligne, Faltings, Fontaine, Illusie, Kato, Tsuji and many others
[
78
]
. Porting over some of the ideas developed in our previous work
[
22
]
, our insight is to use the
1
-point compactification of the affine line
\square=(\mathbb{P}^{1},\infty)
equipped with its canonical logarithmic structure, as a contracting object, instead of the affine line
\mathbb{A}^{1}
.
The resulting condition is intermediate between
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance and
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-invariance (considered, for example, by Ayoub
[
10
]
).
Figure
1
gives a geometric picture of this situation.

There are several reasons for embracing log geometry as a natural framework for studying non
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant cohomology theories, and for the choice of our contracting object, as discussed in
[
22
]
. We limit ourselves to expose a point of view that was not highlighted in our previous work on the subject, that is, the connection between logarithmic geometry and (topological) Hochschild and cyclic homology. This idea dates back to the work of Hesselholt-Madsen on the
K
-theory of local fields. Together with the fact that Hochschild homology and variants do not enjoy a satisfactory localization property (unlike
K
-theory), it motivated Rognes’ definition of (topological) Hochschild homology for a logarithmic ring (spectrum)
[
88
]
. Representing the extension of
\mathrm{THH}
in a motivic category that shares some properties with Morel-Voevodsky’s construction
[
70
]
will make it possible to obtain new localization sequences for
\mathrm{logTHH}
and variants, generalizing the work in
[
89
]
.
It turns out that
\mathrm{logTHH}
and their relatives are indeed representable in our framework, giving rise to motivic
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-spectra. This fact has many consequences, as we shall see.

1.3.
Outline and summary of main results

We now give an overview of the content of this work.
We devote §
2
and
3
to the basic constructions.
Our story begins with a quasi-compact and quasi-separated, integral quasi-coherent log scheme
S

(basically,
any
log scheme one would encounter in real life),
and the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{lSm}/S)
of presheaves of spaces on
\mathbf{lSm}/S

— log smooth saturated log schemes of finite type over
S
.

A (pointed) log motivic space
\mathcal{F}
is a
\square
-local (in the obvious sense) and

dividing
Nisnevich local presheaf of (pointed) spaces on
\mathbf{lSm}/S
;
that is,
\mathcal{F}
turns every strict Nisnevich distinguished square
into a cartesian square, and every dividing cover (a class of admissible blow-ups,
see §
2.3
) into an equivalence.
We introduced this topology in our previous work
[
22
]
.

The resulting
\infty
-category is denoted
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{(\ast)}(S)
:
it underlies a presentable symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category (often denoted with the same symbols).
With these definitions,
we deduce an equivalence

(1.3.1)

\mathbb{P}^{1}\simeq S^{1}\otimes\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}.

Let us clarify the notation. The scheme
\mathbb{P}^{1}=(\mathbb{P}^{1},\emptyset)
is seen as a log scheme with trivial log structure,
the simplicial circle
S^{1}
is seen as a constant log motivic space,

\otimes
is the symmetric monoidal product on pointed log motivic spaces (also denoted
\wedge
),
and finally the log multiplicative group scheme
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}:=(\mathbb{P}^{1},0+\infty)
is specified
by the divisor
0+\infty
on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
(and pointed at
1
).
We view (
1.3.1
) as the log motivic analogue of the
fundamental equivalence
\mathbb{P}^{1}\cong S^{1}\otimes\mathbb{G}_{m}
in
[
70
]
.

When
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
is a scheme, we can consider the localization

\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
of
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}(S)
with respect to the class of admissible blow-ups along smooth centers in the sense of Definition
2.6.1
. The category
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
is our preferred setting for
unstable
computations, and we see it as a non
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant analogue of
\mathcal{H}(S)
.

Using
(\mathbb{P}^{1},\emptyset)
,
we define the
stable
log motivic
\infty
-category by setting

(1.3.2)

\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(S):=\textup{lim}\left(\cdots\xrightarrow{\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}}\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\ast}(S)\xrightarrow{\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}}\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\ast}(S)\right).

Although the basic definitions are essentially formal, it is a nontrivial fact that
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(S)
can be presented as a category of
symmetric spectra
, and it agrees with the formal inversion of the log Tate sphere
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}
. See Lemma
3.2.13
. The final outcome is a stable presentable symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category.

The construction of
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}
satisfies the following universal property, as explained in
§
3.4
:
For
S\in\mathbf{lSch}
and any pointed presentable symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathcal{D}^{\otimes}
,
there exists a naturally induced fully faithful functor

\operatorname{Fun}^{\otimes,\mathrm{L}}(\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}^{\otimes}(S),\mathcal{D}^{\otimes})\to\operatorname{Fun}^{\otimes}(\mathbf{lSm}/S)^{\times},\mathcal{D}^{\otimes}).

Its essential image consists of all
\mathcal{F}
that satisfies
\square
-invariance and
dNis
-descent such that the
cofiber of

\mathcal{F}(i_{1})\colon\mathcal{F}(S)\rightarrow\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{P}^{1})

is an invertible object in
\mathcal{D}^{\otimes}
for
the one section
i_{1}
.
We refer to Robalo
[
87
]
for a similar universal property in stable motivic homotopy theory.

Although the basic axioms are those presented above, in §
3.5
we discuss an alternative model when
S
is a quasi-compact and quasi-separated scheme, considered with a trivial log structure. This is the context in which we can compare our theory with the classical
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant one.
Instead of starting with the category of log smooth log schemes over
S
, one can consider the category
\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
of those log smooth log schemes whose underlying scheme is itself smooth over
S
. Then, we consider the
\infty
-categories of
strict
Nisnevich sheaves of spaces
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{sNis}(\mathbf{SmlSm}/S)
, and the full subcategory spanned by the objects
\mathcal{F}
that are local with respect to the maps
\mathcal{F}(X)\to\mathcal{F}(X\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1}))
: we say that such
\mathcal{F}
is
(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})
-invariant. After that, we stabilize with respect to the log Tate circle.
The condition of being
(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})
-invariant is a priori stronger than
\square
-invariance, but it turns out that the resulting category is indeed equivalent to
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(S)
.

In fact, the following more precise statement holds:

Theorem 1.3.1

(Corollary
3.5.4
, Corollary
3.5.8
)
.

There are equivalences of compactly generated stable
\mathbf{Sm}
-premotivic
\infty
-categories

\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{dNis}^{\rm eff}(-,\Lambda)^{\otimes}\simeq\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(-,\Lambda)^{\otimes}\simeq\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{sNis,(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})}(\mathbf{SmlSm}/-,\Lambda)^{\otimes}

\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(-,\Lambda)^{\otimes}\simeq\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(-,\Lambda)^{\otimes}\simeq\mathcal{S}\mathrm{pt}^{\Sigma}_{T}(\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{sNis,(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})}(\mathbf{SmlSm}/-,\Lambda))^{\otimes}.

for any commutative connective ring spectrum
\Lambda
.

This is one of the strongest properties of our construction: it implies that one gets
a posteriori
dividing descent and invariance under admissible blow ups as a consequence of a very simple axiom: the sheaf property plus the invariance with respect to the compactified affine spaces. In practice, this latter condition is very easy to check and typically follows from the projective bundle formula (note that there are examples of theories satisfying
(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})
-invariance, but not the projective bundle formula, like real topological Hochschild homology
[
82
]
). See §
2.7
for a discussion on compact generators.

The inevitable question now is whether our logarithmic motivic theory is at all understandable.
Happily, the short answer is yes at this stage, mainly due to the following reasons discussed in
the main body of the paper.

1.3.1.
Comparison with
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-motivic homotopy theory

In §
4
we compare our construction with
Morel-Voevodsky’s
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant motivic homotopy theory of schemes.
If
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
is a scheme,
we have natural transformations of premotivic
\infty
-categories

\omega_{\sharp}\colon\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}\text{ and }\omega_{\sharp}\colon\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\ast}\to\mathcal{H}_{\ast},

\omega_{\sharp}\colon\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}\to\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}\text{ and }\omega_{\sharp}\colon\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}\to\mathcal{SH}.

It turns out that their right adjoints, denoted by
\omega^{*}
,
are fully faithful functors.
One can view this as providing an embedding of homotopy theories:

(\mathbb{A}^{1}\text{-local motivic homotopy theory})\subset(\square\text{-local motivic homotopy theory})

In particular,
the endomorphism ring of the motivic sphere
\mathbf{1}

— the unit for the tensor product —
is isomorphic to the endomorphism ring of
\omega^{*}(\mathbf{1})
in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}
.
Over fields,
this is the Grothendieck-Witt ring of quadratic forms via Morel’s theorem
on the motivic
\pi_{0}
of the sphere spectrum
[
69
]

(
[
13
]
shows a similar result for rings of integers in certain number fields).
We expect that
\omega^{*}(\mathbf{1})
is equivalent to
\mathbf{1}
in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}
on a general base. For the moment, we have the following results. In
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{DM}
,
the equivalence
\omega^{*}(\mathbf{1})\simeq\mathbf{1}
shown in

[
22
, Theorem 8.2.11]
uses resolution of singularities, while in
[
85
, Theorem 1.1]
it is shown that
\omega^{*}(\mathbf{1})\simeq\mathbf{1}
in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(k)
, again for a field admitting resolution of singularities. In fact, more generally,
\omega^{*}
is shown to be fully faithful, monoidal and colimits-preserving for
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(k)
.

1.3.2.
Logarithmic Picard groups and
K
-theory

Logarithmic Picard groups
\operatorname{logPic}
of log schemes are part of our setup,
see §
5
.
We show
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
-invariance for
\operatorname{logPic}
and for the log multiplicative group scheme, we have the following Theorem.

Theorem 1.3.2

(Theorem
5.2.10
, Corollary
5.2.11
)
.

For every
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
there is an equivalence

\mathrm{B}\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}\simeq\mathbb{P}^{\infty}

in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
,
which is natural in
S
. When
S\in\mathbf{lSch}
, the same equivalence holds in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}^{\rm eff}(S,\Lambda)
, for every commutative ring spectrum
\Lambda
.

This is the analogue of the equivalence
\mathbb{P}^{\infty}\simeq\mathrm{B}\mathbb{G}_{m}
proved by Morel and Voevodsky using
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy invariance.

In §
6
we construct a logarithmic
K
-theory of log schemes.
While a notion of perfect complexes on log schemes remains elusive (but see
[
18
]
for an approach to solve this problem),
we employ the infinite Grassmannian to geometrically represent
K
-theory of log schemes
defined over a regular base scheme. This gives us a motivic spectrum
\mathbf{logKGL}
, with the structure of homotopy commutative monoid, that satisfies the folllowing:

Theorem 1.3.3

(Theorem
6.5.7
)
.

Suppose
S
is a regular scheme. For every
X\in\mathbf{lSm}/S
with compactifying log structure given by the embedding
X-\partial X\hookrightarrow X
, where
\partial X
is a Cartier divisor on
X
, there are canonical equivalences of spectra

\mathrm{K}(X-\partial X)\simeq\operatorname{map}_{\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(S)}(\Sigma_{S^{1}}^{\infty}X_{+},\Omega_{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}}^{\infty}\mathbf{logKGL})

and

\mathrm{K}(X-\partial X)\simeq\operatorname{map}_{\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(S)}(\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{d}\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{\infty}X_{+},\mathbf{logKGL}).

Similar results hold for
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}(S)
and
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(S)
as well.

Note that over a base
S
with a trivial log structure, the fact that “log
K
-theory” agrees with the
K
-theory of the open complement of the support of the log structure was one of the expected properties. For a general log scheme
S
, we obtain a motivic spectrum simply by pullback of
\mathbf{logKGL}
defined over the integers.

1.3.3.
Oriented cohomology theories

We develop in §
7
a theory of Chern orientations and Thom classes in our setting. This allows us to prove a projective bundle formula
7.2.6
, a blow-up formula, a Thom isomorphism theorem
7.3.8
, a Whitney sum formula
7.3.16
, an excess intersection formula
7.3.18
, a self intersection formula
7.3.19
for any oriented cohomology theory represented in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(S)
. All the above results apply in particular for cohomology theories on classical schemes that admit a natural extension to log schemes, like Hodge, crystalline, prismatic, syntomic cohomology and so on, that are
not

\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy invariant. One of the key inputs in the proofs is the following fundamental computation, obtained by adapting our argument in
\mathbf{logDM}
.

Theorem 1.3.4

(Proposition
7.2.1
)
.

For every
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
, there is an equivalence

\mathbb{P}^{n}/\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\cong(\mathbb{P}^{1}/\operatorname{pt})^{\wedge n}

in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}(S)
.

One of the main applications of our formalism is the construction of Gysin or
residue
sequences for non
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant cohomology theories: we adopted the expression “residue sequence” in our joint work with Lundemo
[
19
]
, since the graded pieces of the Gysin sequence for
\mathrm{logHH}
yield the classical residue sequence for Hodge cohomology. The source of them is the following Theorem.

Theorem 1.3.5

(Theorem
3.2.21
, Remark
3.2.22
)
.

Let
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
. Let
X\in\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
and let
Z\in\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
be a strict closed subscheme in
X
that has strict normal crossing with
\partial X
over
S
. Then there is a cofiber sequence of pointed motivic spaces

({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}X,E)_{+}\to X_{+}\to\mathrm{Th}({\mathrm{N}}_{Z}X)

in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\ast}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
, functorial in
(X,Z)
, where
\mathrm{Th}({\mathrm{N}}_{Z}(X))
is the Thom space of the normal bundle of
Z
in
X
, appropriately defined.

In the presence of an orientation and in the stable setting, the
Thom space term can be trivialized, thus assuming a familiar form. This applies, for example, to prismatic cohomology, as discussed in
[
18
]
, which can be represented by an oriented
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-spectrum (see §
8.7
).
In §
7.4
we also present a complete computation of the cohomology the Grassmannians
{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)
.

Theorem 1.3.6

(Theorem
7.4.6
)
.

Let
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
and let
\mathbf{E}
be an oriented homotopy commutative monoid in
\mathbf{logSH}(S)
.
There is a naturally induced ring isomorphism

\varphi_{r,n}\colon\mathbf{E}^{**}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R_{r,n}\to\mathbf{E}^{**}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)),

where
R_{r,n}
is the
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra defined in (
7.4.1
).

The argument requires new ingredients, since we cannot apply the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopies used, for example, in
[
75
]
. Finally, we introduce a logarithmic algebraic cobordism spectrum
{\mathbf{logMGL}}
using our definition of Thom spaces. This is the universally oriented homotopy commutative monoid in
\mathbf{logSH}
.

1.3.4.
Logarithmic trace methods

In §
8
we construct a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-spectrum
\mathbf{logTHH}\in\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(S)

for all
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
. The circle action on
\mathbf{logTHH}
allows us to consider the variants
\mathbf{logTP}
and, of course,
\mathbf{logTC}^{-}
, representing, respectively, periodic and negative topological cyclic homology. As seen in
[
20
]
, the cyclotomic structure makes it possible to obtain from
\mathbf{logTP}
and
\mathbf{logTC}^{-}
yet another spectrum, that is
\mathbf{logTC}
, representing
log topological cyclic homology. More precisely, we have the following:

Theorem 1.3.7

(Theorem
8.4.4
, Theorem
8.5.1
, Theorem
8.6.7
)
.

Let
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
. Then there are
oriented
homotopy commutative monoids in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(S)

\mathbf{logTHH},\;\mathbf{logTC}^{-},\;\mathbf{logTP},\;\mathbf{logTC}\in\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(S),

and there is an equivalence of spectra

\mathrm{logTHH}(X)\simeq\operatorname{map}_{\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}(S)}(\Sigma_{S^{1}}^{\infty}X_{+},\Omega_{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}}^{\infty}\mathbf{logTHH})\simeq\operatorname{map}_{\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(S)}(\Sigma_{T}^{\infty}X_{+},\mathbf{logTHH})

for all
X\in\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
. The spectrum
\mathbf{logTHH}
is
(2,1)
-periodic, in the sense that
we have an equivalence of spectra

\mathbf{logTHH}\simeq\Sigma^{2,1}\mathbf{logTHH}.

Similar results hold for
\mathbf{logTC}^{-}
,
\mathbf{logTP}
, and
\mathbf{logTC}
also.

In particular, when
X=\operatorname{Spec}(R)
is just a ring with a trivial log structure, we find that
\mathrm{THH}(R)
can be described as a mapping spectrum in our motivic category. Two main ingredients go into this: a proof that a suitably globalized version of
\mathrm{logTHH}
satisfies
(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})
-invariance and a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle formula for
\mathrm{logTHH}
. See §
8.5
.

Since the above spectra are, in fact, oriented cohomology theories in the sense of §
7
, all the results mentioned above apply. For example, we immediately get a full computation of
\mathrm{THH}
of the Grassmannians, new Gysin sequences, and so on.

When
k
is a field that admits resolution of singularities, we can put together the results of §
6
and
8
to obtain the following result.

Theorem 1.3.8

(Theorem
8.6.5
)
.

There is a homomorphism

(1.3.3)

\mathbf{logTr}\colon\mathbf{logKGL}\to\mathbf{logTC}

of homotopy commutative monoids in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(k)
such that

\lambda^{*}\Omega_{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}}^{\infty}\mathbf{logTr}\colon\lambda^{*}\Omega_{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}}^{\infty}\mathbf{logKGL}\to\lambda^{*}\Omega_{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}}^{\infty}\mathbf{logTC}

is equivalent to the cyclotomic trace
\mathrm{Tr}\colon\mathrm{K}\to\mathrm{TC}
in
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{Nis}(\mathbf{Sm}/k,\mathcal{S}\mathrm{pt})
.

The assumption on resolution of singularities here comes from the equivalence between
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(k)
and
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(k)
, but we do not consider it to be crucial.
We see (
1.3.3
) as a first important step in studying
motivic filtrations
on
\mathrm{TC}
and variants by using the geometric methods given by motivic homotopy theory, such as the slice filtration. See §
1.5
below.

1.3.5.
Kato-Nakayama spaces, étale realization

In §
9
we construct log étale and Kato-Nakayama realization functors.
Over a field
k
with an embedding
k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
into the field of complex numbers, we can consider the functor that sends
X\in\mathbf{lSch}/k
its Kato-Nakayama space
(X)^{\log}
. It is a manifold with boundary, considered here as an object of the category of topological spaces. Since the Kato-Nakayama realization of
\square
is nothing but the closed unit disk (seen as a manifold with boundary), this can be promoted to an
\infty
-functor

\mathrm{Re}_{\mathrm{KN}}
, satisfying the following property.

Theorem 1.3.9

(Proposition
9.3.6
)
.

There is a commutative triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Re}_{\mathrm{KN}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Re}_{\mathrm{Betti}}}

where
\mathrm{Re}_{\mathrm{Betti}}
denotes the Betti realization functor

\mathcal{H}(k)\to\mathcal{S}
.
There is a similar commutative triangle for
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(k)
,
\mathcal{SH}(k)
, and
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{pt}
.

In light of the work of Vistoli and Talpo
[
94
]
, one could consider more refined Betti realization functors, with value in a category of log analytic spaces. We leave this task to future work.

When
k
is an arbitrary field and
\ell
is invertible in
k
, we construct in §
9.2
a log étale
\ell
-adic realization functor

\mathrm{Re}^{l{\acute{e}t}}_{\ell}\colon\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{l{\acute{e}t}}(k)\to\mathcal{P}\mathrm{ro}(\mathcal{S}\mathrm{pt})^{\mathbb{Z}/\ell}

(as well as its unstable cousin). This is obtained by considering the log étale homotopy type or, more precisely, the
shape
of
X\in\mathbf{lSch}
, defined as
\Pi(\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}(X_{l{\acute{e}t}},\mathcal{S}))
(see
[
63
, Chapter 7]
and the work of Hoyois
[
47
]
and Zargar
[
105
]
). The basic input is given by the
\square
-invariance of Kummer étale cohomology with locally constant coefficients, proved in Theorem
9.1.5
.

In summary,
the fundamental theories considered in motivic homotopy theory are available in our logarithmic setting, but, in addition, important examples from
p
-adic geometry come into focus.

Remark 1.3.10
.

Let us offer a stacky perspective on our set-up,
cf.
[
51
]
,
[
79
]
.
A rank one Deligne-Faltings log structure on a scheme
X
is equivalent to a section

X\to[\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]
;
the quotient stack is formed with respect to the canonical
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action by scaling.
Now, a morphism
f\colon X\to[\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]
is the datum of a

\mathbb{G}_{m}
-torsor on
X
, a.k.a., a line bundle, together with a section.
For
X
regular and noetherian,
this is exactly the datum of a generalized Cartier divisor
D
on
X
.
In concrete terms,
the closed subscheme
D
can be recovered as
f
’s fiber over

\mathrm{B}\mathbb{G}_{m}\subset[\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]
.
In particular,
for the log scheme
\square
and the divisor
\infty\in\mathbb{P}^{1}

we obtain the cartesian square

(1.3.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\infty\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}\mathbb{G}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}].}}

This provides a kind of analogy between the geometry of
\square
-homotopy invariance and
Annala-Hoyois-Iwasa’s work on weighted
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy invariance in

\mathbb{P}^{1}
-inverted stable homotopy theory for schemes —
they show that the bottom horizontal map in (
1.3.4
) is an equivalence assuming
elementary blow-up excision and
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-stability, see
[
1
]
.
Both of these properties hold by construction of our logarithmic stable homotopy theory
and Theorem
3.3.5
. See §
4
for the construction of a comparison functor.

1.4.
Discussions about non
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant homotopy theory

There are at least four approaches to non
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant motivic homotopy theory.
Let us review their advantages and disadvantages as follows.

(1)
The interval
\square
in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}
allows us to adapt various arguments in
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy theory to the non
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant setting,
which is one of the main contents of this paper.
Representing a cohomology theory of schemes in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(k)
demands, of course, its extension to log schemes.
This is often possible, as we have discussed, but requires extra effort, see, e.g.,
[
19
]
,
[
20
]
, and
[
18
]
.
The unstable categories
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}(S)
and
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
can be used as a replacement for
\mathcal{H}(S)
if one removes the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant axiom. To our knowledge this is completely new.

(2)
The slight variant
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}^{\mathbf{Adm}}
of
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}
allows non-toroidal boundaries.
This is useful to remove the assumption of resolution of singularities,
but it is harder to represent a cohomology theory.
If we knew that
\mathrm{TC}
is representable in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(k)
for a perfect field
k
,
we would obtain a cyclotomic trace in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(k)
and hence in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(k)
without assuming resolution of singularities. We plan to pursue this approach in future work.

(3)
The theory of motives with modulus due to Kahn-Miyazaki-Saito-Yamazaki
[
54
]
is specialized to understand the ramification behavior at the boundary,
which is not observable in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}
or
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{DM}
.
For the sake of this,
representing cohomology theories would require even more effort. Of course, the representability in motives with modulus is a priori a stronger statement.

(4)
A different perspective is given by the

\mathbb{P}^{1}
-inverted homotopy theory
[
1
]
.
To represent a cohomology theory in this setting,
one needs to know a priori elementary blow-up excision,
which can be obtained by another input (e.g., blow-up formula for
K
-theory) or deduced from
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
-invariance if an extension to log schemes is known.
This theory is stable by construction,
while unstable computations are possible in our log setting.

1.5.
Outlook and future developments

We quickly review some of the consequences of the results presented here that we have either developed or will develop in upcoming works.

1.5.1.
Residue sequences for
\mathrm{logHH}

The Hochschild-Rosenberg-Kostant filtration on logarithmic
\mathrm{HH}
was constructed in our joint work with Lundemo
[
19
]
.
The theory of orientations in §
7
produces the Gysin sequences for
\mathrm{logHH}
.
Even when
X=\operatorname{Spec}(R)
and
Z=\operatorname{Spec}(R/I)
are smooth affine schemes, the Gysin sequence relating
\mathrm{HH}(R)
and
\mathrm{HH}(R/I)
is completely new if
\mathrm{codim}_{Z}(X)\geq 2
since non-affine log schemes are involved.

The variantions
\mathrm{logHC}^{-}
and
\mathrm{logHP}
are studied in
[
20
]
,
where logarithmic refinements of the Beilinson filtrations
[
3
]
are also constructed.

1.5.2.
Blow-up excision for
\mathrm{THR}

Real topological Hochschild homology
\mathrm{THR}
is another example of non
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant cohomology theories.
The purpose of
[
82
]
is to extend
\mathrm{THR}
to log schemes and to show that it is
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
-invariant.
Together with Theorem
3.3.5
,
this implies that
\mathrm{THR}
satisfies blow-up excision for closed immersions of smooth schemes.
This statement does not involve log schemes,
but the proof needs log schemes.

1.5.3.
Logarithmic prismatic cohomology via
\mathrm{logTHH}

The purpose of
[
20
]
with Lundemo is to study logarithmic refinements of the Bhatt-Morrow-Scholze filtrations
[
14
]
on
\mathrm{THH}
and its variants.
In particular,
the definition of Nygaard completed logarithmic prismatic cohomology is obtained.
This study is further carried out in
[
18
]
,
where the first and second authors, together with Lundemo and Merici, construct a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-spectrum version of Nygaard completed logarithmic prismatic cohomology.
The theory of orientations in §
7
again produces the Gysin maps for Nygaard completed logarithmic prismatic cohomology, and over a perfectoid base also for the non-Nygaard completed version.

1.5.4.
Filtrations on
\mathrm{logK}
and
\mathrm{logTC}

We can define the slice filtration on
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}
that is directly analogous to Voevodsky’s slice filtration
[
99
]
in
\mathcal{SH}
.
For a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p
admitting resolution of singularities,

[
23
]
shows that the logarithmic cyclotomic trace morphisms

\mathbf{logKGL}\to\mathbf{logHC}^{-}\text{ and }\mathbf{logKGL}\to\mathbf{logTC}(-;\mathbb{Z}_{p})\text{ ($\mathrm{char}$ $p>0$)}

are compatible with the slice filtration on the left-hand side and the Beilinson filtration on
\mathbf{logHC}^{-}

[
20
]
and the Bhatt-Morrow-Scholze filtration on
\mathbf{logTC}(-;\mathbb{Z}_{p})
.
This argument breaks down in
\mathcal{SH}(k)
since
\mathrm{HC}^{-}
and
\mathrm{TC}
are not
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant.

1.6.
Changes from the previous ArXiv version

This paper first appeared on the ArXiv in March 2023, after having circulated informally since 2021. This is a rewriting of the previously available ArXiv version:
for the readers’ convenience, we list here some of the most important changes.

First, we minimized the usage of model categories in this updated version,
while the previous version used model categories for constructions.
As a consequence,
we streamlined many arguments in §
2
.

We removed the noetherian assumption on
S
for
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(S)
.
To do this,
we considered a logarithmic version of the noetherian approximation in §
3.1
.
Such a generalization is often desired since many schemes in
p
-adic geometry are not noetherian. In the same spirit, some arguments are now available without the traditional “fs” assumption on the log scheme. We have not investigated this systematically in this work, but for example in
[
17
]
one needs to deal with non-fine log structures on integral perfectoid rings.

In the same spirit, in the previous version

\mathrm{logTHH}
was only defined for noetherian fs log schemes.
We also removed this noetherian assumption by introducing another equivalent definition,
see Definition
8.3.7
.

1.7.
Notation

We will use the following notation (see also the Index for a more comprehensive list).

\mathbf{Sch}

quasi-compact and quasi-separated schemes

\mathbf{Sch}_{noeth}

noetherian schemes of finite Krull dimension

\mathbf{lSch}

quasi-compact and quasi-separated integral, saturated

quasi-coherent log schemes

\mathbf{lSch}_{noeth}

noetherian fs log schemes of finite Krull dimension

\mathbf{Sm}/S

smooth schemes of finite type over a scheme
S

\mathbf{lSm}/S

log smooth fs log schemes of finite type over an fs log scheme
S\in\mathbf{lSch}

\mathbf{SmlSm}/S

full subcategory of
\mathbf{lSm}/S
of log smooth fs log schemes
X
such

that the underlying scheme
\underline{X}
is smooth over
\underline{S}

\operatorname{id}\xrightarrow{ad}f_{*}f^{*}

unit of an adjunction
(f^{*},f_{*})

f^{*}f_{*}\xrightarrow{ad^{\prime}}\operatorname{id}

counit of an adjunction
(f^{*},f_{*})

\mathbf{Psh}(\mathbf{C})

presheaves (of sets) on a category
\mathbf{C}

\mathbf{Shv}_{\tau}(\mathbf{C})

\tau
-sheaves (of sets) on a category
\mathbf{C}
with respect to a topology
\tau

\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{C})

\infty
-category of presheaves of spaces on category
\mathbf{C}

\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}_{\ast}(\mathbf{C})

\infty
-category of presheaves of pointed spaces on category
\mathbf{C}

\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}^{(\wedge)}_{\tau}(\mathbf{C})

\infty
-category of
\tau
-(hyper)sheaves on a category
\mathbf{C}

\mathcal{S}

\infty
-category of Kan complexes (spaces)

\mathcal{S}\mathrm{pt}

\infty
-category of spectra

\operatorname{Map}_{\mathcal{C}}(-,-)

mapping space in an
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}

We freely use some classical results from log geometry. Our main reference is Ogus’ monograph on the subject
[
78
]
. See also Appendix A to our previous work
[
22
]
for a short recollection. If
S
is a (classical) scheme,
X
is a smooth
S
-scheme and
D=D_{1}+\ldots D_{n}
is a strict normal crossing divisor on
X
over
S
, we will write
(X,D)
for the log smooth log scheme obtained by considering
X
with the compactifying log structure determined by the open immersion
X-D\hookrightarrow X
.

2.
Construction of logarithmic motivic homotopy categories

The purpose of this section is to provide several equivalent
\infty
-categorical constructions
for our homotopy categories:

\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H},\;\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\ast},\;\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}},\;\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH},\;\mathrm{log}\mathcal{DA}^{\mathrm{eff}},\text{ and }\mathrm{log}\mathcal{DA}.

The linear versions
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{DA}^{\mathrm{eff}}=\mathrm{log}\mathcal{DA}^{\mathrm{eff}}(S,\Lambda)
and
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{DA}(S,\Lambda)
appeared before in our previous work
[
22
]
,
[
19
]
, and are constructed as localization (and
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-stabilization) of the category of presheaves of chain complexes of
\Lambda
-modules on the category of log smooth log schemes over a fixed log scheme
S
. As in
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy theory, there are of course non-linear versions, constructed starting from presheaves of spaces or of spectra.

The basic idea is the following. For every
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
,
Morel-Voevodsky
[
70
]
constructed their unstable
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-motivic homotopy category
\mathcal{H}(S)

by using the following ingredients:

\text{(1) $\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{Sm}/S,\mathcal{S})$, (2) Nisnevich topology, (3) $\mathbb{A}^{1}$-localization}.

Starting with any
S\in\mathbf{lSch}
,
we construct our unstable logarithmic motivic homotopy category
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}
by using:

\text{(1) $\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{lSm}/S,\mathcal{S})$, (2) dividing Nisnevich topology, (3) $(\mathbb{P}^{1},\infty)$-localization}.

We will often abbreviate the log scheme
(\mathbb{P}^{1},\infty)
using the symbol
\square
.
The dividing Nisnevich topology was introduced in
[
22
, Definition 3.1.4]
as a finer variant of the (strict) Nisnevich topology, obtained by including proper surjective log étale monomorphisms as covers.
It played an important role in the development of logarithmic motives in
[
22
, §7]
.

We will also consider an alternative but equivalent construction, practically useful in many contexts (this is used extensively in
[
19
]
and
[
18
]
), using

\text{(1) $\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{SmlSm}/S,\mathcal{S})$, (2) strict Nisnevich topology, (3) $(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})$-localization, $n\geq 1$}.

In our treatment of the general formalism for the construction of motivic
\infty
-categories (suitably tailored to the particular situations of the
\square
-localization, or the
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})_{n\geq 1}
-localization), we follow the presentation of Ayoub–Gallauer–Vezzani
[
11
]
, which provides a clean
\infty
-categorical construction from the beginning.

2.1.
Premotivic
\infty
-categories

The notion of premotivic categories was introduced by Cisinski-Déglise in
[
30
, §1]
;
as the name suggests,
this is an axiomatic setting for dealing with categories appearing in motivic homotopy theory.
In this subsection, we consider the
\infty
-version of premotivic categories.

Definition 2.1.1
.

Let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime*}}

be a commutative square of
\infty
-categories (or just ordinary categories).
If both
g^{*}
and
g^{\prime*}
admit right adjoints
g_{*}
and
g_{*}^{\prime}
, the
Beck-Chevalley transformation

is defined to be the composite

(2.1.1)

f^{*}g_{*}\xrightarrow{ad}g_{*}^{\prime}g^{\prime*}f^{*}g_{*}\xrightarrow{\simeq}g_{*}^{\prime}f^{\prime*}g^{*}g_{*}\xrightarrow{ad^{\prime}}g_{*}^{\prime}f^{\prime*}.

Here,

ad
(resp.
ad^{\prime}
) denotes the unit (resp. counit) of the adjunction
(g^{\prime*},g_{*}^{\prime})
.
On the other hand,
if both
f^{*}
and
f^{\prime*}
admit left adjoints
f_{\sharp}
and
f_{\sharp}^{\prime}
,
the
Beck-Chevalley transformation
is defined as the composite

(2.1.2)

f_{\sharp}^{\prime}g^{\prime*}\xrightarrow{ad}f_{\sharp}^{\prime}g^{\prime*}f^{*}f_{\sharp}\xrightarrow{\simeq}f_{\sharp}^{\prime}f^{\prime*}g^{*}f_{\sharp}\xrightarrow{ad^{\prime}}g^{*}f_{\sharp}.

When all of
f_{\sharp}
,
f_{\sharp}^{\prime}
,
g_{*}
, and
g_{*}^{\prime}
exist,
it is a routine exercise to show that (
2.1.1
) can be obtained from (
2.1.2
) by adjunction.
In particular,
(
2.1.1
) is an equivalence if and only if (
2.1.2
) is an equivalence.

Definition 2.1.2
.

Let
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{L}})
denote the
\infty
-category of symmetric monoidal presentable
\infty
-categories
and colimit preserving symmetric monoidal functors.
Suppose that
\mathcal{C}
is an
\infty
-category and let
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{L}})
be a functor.
If
f
is a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
, we often use the abbreviated notation

f^{*}:=\mathcal{F}(f).

Since
f^{*}
preserves small colimits, it admits a right adjoint
f_{*}
.
All
2
-morphisms in
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{L}}
are invertible,
so for all composable morphisms
f\colon Y\to X
and
g\colon Z\to Y
in
\mathcal{C}
,
there is a natural equivalence

g^{*}\circ f^{*}\simeq(f\circ g)^{*}.

If
X
is an object of
\mathcal{C}
, let
\otimes_{X}
(resp.
\mathbf{1}_{X}
) denote the monoidal product (resp. monoidal unit) in
\mathcal{F}(X)
.
For all
f\colon Y\to X
, the functor
f^{*}
is symmetric monoidal.
Therefore, there are natural equivalences

f^{*}(-)\otimes_{Y}f^{*}(-)\simeq f^{*}((-)\otimes_{X}(-))\text{ and }f^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{X})\simeq\mathbf{1}_{Y}.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be an essentially small class of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
.
Then
\mathcal{F}
is a
\mathcal{P}
-premotivic (presentable)
\infty
-category over

\mathcal{C}
if the following conditions are satisfied:

(i)

For every morphism
f
in
\mathcal{P}
, the functor
f^{*}
admits a left adjoint
f_{\sharp}
.

(ii)

For every cartesian square in
\mathcal{C}

(2.1.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

with
f,f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}
, the Beck-Chevalley transformation given by the composite

f_{\sharp}^{\prime}g^{\prime*}\xrightarrow{ad}f_{\sharp}^{\prime}g^{\prime*}f^{*}f_{\sharp}\xrightarrow{\simeq}f_{\sharp}^{\prime}f^{\prime*}g^{*}f_{\sharp}\xrightarrow{ad^{\prime}}g^{*}f_{\sharp}

is a natural equivalence.

(iii)

For every morphism
f\colon Y\to X
in
\mathcal{P}
, the natural transformation given by the composite

\displaystyle f_{\sharp}((-)\otimes_{Y}f^{*}(-))\xrightarrow{ad}

\displaystyle f_{\sharp}(f^{*}f_{\sharp}(-)\otimes_{Y}f^{*}(-))

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}

\displaystyle f_{\sharp}f^{*}(f_{\sharp}(-)\otimes_{X}(-))\xrightarrow{ad^{\prime}}f_{\sharp}(-)\otimes_{X}(-)

is a natural equivalence.

We often just say that
\mathcal{F}
is a premotivic
\infty
-category if
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{P}
are clear from the context.

Definition 2.1.3
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be an essentially small class of morphisms in an
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
.
Suppose
\varphi^{*}\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G}
is a natural transformation,
where
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{L}})
are
\mathcal{P}
-premotivic
\infty
-categories.
For every object
X
of
\mathcal{C}
,
\varphi^{*}
defines a functor

\varphi_{X}^{*}:=\varphi^{*}(X)\colon\mathcal{F}(X)\to\mathcal{G}(X).

Furthermore,
for every morphism
f\colon Y\to X
in
\mathcal{C}
,
\varphi^{*}
defines a natural equivalence

\varphi_{Y}^{*}f^{*}\simeq f^{*}\varphi_{X}^{*}.

We say that
\varphi^{*}
is a
functor of
\mathcal{P}
-premotivic
\infty
-categories
if the following condition is satisfied:
For every morphism
f\colon Y\to X
in
\mathcal{P}
, the Beck-Chevalley transformation given by the composite

(2.1.4)

f_{\sharp}\varphi_{Y}^{*}\xrightarrow{ad}f_{\sharp}\varphi_{Y}^{*}f^{*}f_{\sharp}\xrightarrow{\simeq}f_{\sharp}f^{*}\varphi_{X}^{*}f_{\sharp}\xrightarrow{ad^{\prime}}\varphi_{X}^{*}f_{\sharp}

is a natural equivalence.

We say that
\varphi^{*}
is an
equivalence of
\mathcal{P}
-premotivic
\infty
-categories
if
\varphi_{X}^{*}

is an equivalence for all
X\in\mathcal{C}
.

The following is immediate from the definitions.

Proposition 2.1.4
.

If
\varphi^{*}
is an equivalence of
\mathcal{P}
-premotivic
\infty
-categories,
then
\varphi^{*}
is a functor of
\mathcal{P}
-premotivic
\infty
-categories.

2.2.
Stabilizations, spectra and symmetric spectra

We briefly review the process of constructing the stabilization of an
\infty
-category, and the formal inversion of a given object, see
[
87
, §2.2]
. We will also review the construction of symmetric spectra from an
\infty
-categorical point of view. This material is not new, but is recorded for the reader’s convenience. See also
[
18
, §2.2]
. The basic references here are Robalo’s paper
[
87
]
, and
[
64
]
.

Construction 2.2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}\in\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{L}}
be a presentable
\infty
-category, and suppose we are given an adjunction

(2.2.1)

G:\mathcal{C}\rightleftarrows\mathcal{C}:U.

The
G
-stabilization of
\mathcal{C}
is defined to be

(2.2.2)

\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(\mathcal{C}):=\mathop{\rm lim}(\cdots\xrightarrow{U}\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{U}\mathcal{C}),

where the limit is taken in
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
, and we view the diagram
(\cdots\xrightarrow{U}\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{U}\mathcal{C})
as a functor of
\infty
-categories
\mathbb{N}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
.

Observe that by
[
63
, Corollary 3.3.3.2]
, an object of
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(\mathcal{C})
can be written as a sequence of objects
(X_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of
\mathcal{C}
,
together with equivalences (the
bonding maps
)
X_{i}\xrightarrow{\simeq}UX_{i+1}
in
\mathcal{C}
for all integers
i\geq 0
.
Such a sequence is called a
G
-spectrum
.
Morphisms are given by maps of sequences with obvious compatibility conditions.

Alternatively, one can proceed as follows.
The inclusion
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{R}}\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
preserves small limits by
[
63
, Proposition 5.5.7.6]
, and the equivalence of
\infty
-categories

(2.2.3)

(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{R}})^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{L}}

in
[
63
, Corollary 5.5.3.4]
sends a functor in
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{R}}
to its left adjoint.
Use (
2.2.3
) to obtain an equivalence of
\infty
-categories

(2.2.4)

\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(\mathcal{C})\simeq\operatorname{colim}_{\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{L}}}(\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{G}\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{G}\cdots)

where the colimit is taken in
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{L}}
, and we view the diagram
(\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{G}\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{G}\cdots)
as a functor of
\infty
-categories
\mathbb{N}\to\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{L}}
.

The identity functors from
\mathcal{C}
to the 0th
\mathcal{C}
in the colimit (
2.2.4
) and from the 0th
\mathcal{C}
in the limit (
2.2.2
) to
\mathcal{C}
naturally induce functors

(2.2.5)

G^{\infty}:\mathcal{C}\rightleftarrows\mathrm{Stab}_{B}(\mathcal{C}):U^{\infty}.

Using (
2.2.3
), this can be shown to be an adjoint pair.
The functor
G^{\infty}
is called the
infinite suspension functor
, and the functor
U^{\infty}
is called the
infinite loop space functor
.
For a
G
-spectrum
X:=(X_{0},X_{1},\ldots)
, we have

(2.2.6)

U^{\infty}X\simeq X_{0}.

Remark 2.2.2
.

Of course the stabilization
\mathrm{Stab}(\mathcal{C})
of an
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
with finite limits with respect to the
S^{1}
-suspension functor agrees with the free stable
\infty
-category on
\mathcal{C}
. See
[
64
, Proposition 1.4.2.21]
. For
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{S}
, we get the category of spectra
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{pt}
.

We will mainly use the construction of
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(\mathcal{C})
when
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{\langle 1\rangle}^{\otimes}
for a symmetric monoidal presentable
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}^{\otimes}
such that the tensor product commutes with small colimits in each variable and
G:=\Sigma_{B}:=(-)\otimes B
for some object
B\in\mathcal{C}
. We denote by
\Omega_{B}
its right adjoint.

In good cases, the
\Sigma_{B}
-stabilization is equipped with a particularly nice symmetric monoidal structure, using the presentation given by the
\infty
-category of symmetric
B
-spectra in
\mathcal{C}
, which we now review.

Definition 2.2.3
.

Let
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at})\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}
be the forgetful functor from the
\infty
-category of symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories. By
[
64
, Example 3.1.3.14]
it admits a left adjoint, denoted
\mathrm{Sym}^{\infty}(-)
. We
let
\mathrm{Sym}^{\infty}(*)
be the free symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category generated by one object
*
. This was denoted
free^{\otimes}(\Delta[0])
in
[
87
, §2.1]
. Note that the underlying
\infty
-category of
\mathrm{Sym}^{\infty}(*)
agrees with
B\Sigma_{\mathbb{N}}=\coprod_{n}B\Sigma_{n}
.

Definition 2.2.4
.

Let
\mathcal{C}^{\otimes}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{L}})
, and let
\mathcal{C}^{\Sigma}=\operatorname{Fun}^{\rm L}(\mathrm{Sym}^{\infty}(*),\mathcal{C})^{\otimes}
be the category of symmetric sequences in
\mathcal{C}
(following
[
64
, Definition 6.3.0.2]
), equipped with the Day convolution product
[
64
, §2.2.6]
.

We have
\operatorname{Fun}^{\rm L}(\mathrm{Sym}^{\infty}(*),\mathcal{C})_{\langle 1\rangle}=\prod_{n}\operatorname{Fun}^{\rm L}(B\Sigma_{n},\mathcal{C})
.
Informally, its objects are given by collections
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, where each
A_{n}
is an object of
\mathcal{C}
equipped with an action of
\Sigma_{n}
.

Construction 2.2.5
.

We define an endofunctor
(-)\{-1\}
of
\mathcal{C}^{\Sigma}
as follows (see
[
18
, Construction 2.9]
). Given
X\in\mathcal{C}^{\Sigma}
, we set
X\{-1\}_{0}=0
and
X\{-1\}_{n}=\Sigma_{n}\times_{\Sigma_{n-1}}X_{n-1}
, where
\Sigma_{n}\times_{\Sigma_{n-1}}X_{n-1}
is the equalizer

\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\Sigma_{n}}X_{n-1}\xrightarrow{(\tau_{\sigma}-\tau^{\prime}_{\sigma})_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{n-1}}}\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\Sigma_{n}}X_{n-1}

and
\tau_{\sigma}
(resp.
\tau_{\sigma}^{\prime}
) denotes the endomorphism of
\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\Sigma_{n}}X_{n-1}
given by the action of
\Sigma_{n-1}\subset\Sigma_{n}
permuting the components (resp. given by the diagonal action). See
[
29
, §6.2]
.

Let
\mathbbm{1}\{-1\}\in\mathcal{C}^{\Sigma}
be given as
(\mathbbm{1}\{-1\})_{n}=\mathbbm{1}
if
n=1
and
0
otherwise, where
\mathbbm{1}
is the unit of
\mathcal{C}^{\otimes}
. By definition of the convolution product, we observe that
M\{-1\}=M\otimes\mathbbm{1}\{-1\}.

Recall now from
[
87
, Definition 2.16]
that an object
B
in a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}^{\otimes}
is called
symmetric
if the cyclic permutation
\sigma_{3}
on
B\otimes B\otimes B
is equivalent to the identity.

Definition 2.2.6
.

Let
B
be a symmetric object of
\mathcal{C}^{\otimes}
, and let
S_{B}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathcal{C}^{\Sigma})
be the free commutative algebra on
B\{-1\}
.
Following
[
46
, Definition 6.2]
or
[
29
, §6.6]
, we define the
\infty
-category
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{re}\mathcal{S}\mathrm{pt}_{B}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{C})^{\otimes}
of symmetric
B
-pre-spectra in
\mathcal{C}
as the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Mod}_{S_{B}}(\mathcal{C}^{\Sigma})
.

For a symmetric
B
-pre-spectrum
M
in
\mathcal{C}
, we have the structure map
S_{B}\otimes M\to M
. Precomposing with the canonical morphism
B\{-1\}\to S_{B}
, we obtain a map

\sigma_{M}\colon B\{-1\}\otimes M\simeq(B\otimes M)\{-1\}\to M

where the tensor product is the convolution product in
\mathcal{C}^{\Sigma}
. This induces a map

(2.2.7)

\gamma_{M}\colon M\to\underline{\operatorname{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}^{\Sigma}}(B\{-1\},M)=:\Omega_{B}M.

Definition 2.2.7

(
[
46
, Definitions 7.6 and 7.7]
,
[
29
, §6.23]
)
.

The symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category of symmetric
B
-spectra
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{pt}_{B}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{C})^{\otimes}
in
\mathcal{C}
is the Bousfield localization of the
\infty
-category
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{re}\mathcal{S}\mathrm{pt}_{B}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{C})^{\otimes}
of symmetric
B
-pre-spectra with respect to the collection of morphisms
\gamma_{M}
of (
2.2.7
) for
M\in\mathcal{C}^{\Sigma}
.

Proposition 2.2.8
.

The
\infty
-category
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{pt}_{B}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{C})^{\otimes}
is a presentable stable symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category, and it is naturally equivalent to the formal inversion of the object
B
in
\mathcal{C}
constructed in
[
87
, Proposition 2.9]
. The underlying
\infty
-category
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{pt}_{B}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{C})_{\langle 1\rangle}
is equivalent to
\mathrm{Stab}_{B}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

Combine
[
87
, Corollary 2.22, Theorem 2.26]
.
∎

The construction provides a morphism
\Sigma^{\infty}_{B}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\otimes}\to\mathcal{S}\mathrm{pt}_{B}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{C})^{\otimes}
in
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{L}})
, sending
B
to a
\otimes
-invertible object, and the category
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{pt}_{B}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{C})^{\otimes}
is initial for this property. We denote by
\Omega^{\infty}_{B}
its right adjoint.

2.3.
Topologies on log schemes

In this subsection,
we will review various cd-structures and topologies on log schemes that will be used throughout this paper, and we briefly review some useful notions from log geometry that we will use in a slightly non-standard level of generality. We refer to
[
59
, Appendix A]
for a more detailed discussion. All schemes are assumed to be quasi-compact and quasi-separated.

Recall that for a pre-log ring
(A,M_{A})
, we write
\operatorname{Spec}(A,M_{A})
for the log scheme corresponding to
(A,M_{A})
, that is, the log scheme whose underlying scheme is
X=\operatorname{Spec}(A)
and with log structure given by
(M_{A}\to\mathcal{O}_{X})^{a}
, where
(-)^{a}
denotes the associated log structure to the pre-log structure
M_{A}\to\mathcal{O}_{X}
. Here, we see
M_{A}
as constant sheaf of monoids on
\operatorname{Spec}(A)
.

Definition 2.3.1
.

Let
S
be a quasi-coherent integral log scheme. A morphism
X\to S
of quasi-coherent integral log schemes is
log smooth
(resp.
log étale
) if, étale locally on
\underline{X}
and
\underline{S}
there exists a fine log structure
\mathcal{M}_{S,0}
on
\underline{S}
such that
\mathcal{M}_{S,0}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{S}
, and a log smooth (resp. log étale) morphism
(\underline{X},\mathcal{M}_{X,0})\to(\underline{S},\mathcal{M}_{S,0})
in the sense of
[
78
, Definition IV.3.1.1]
or
[
55
, §3.3]
such that
X\cong(\underline{X},\mathcal{M}_{X,0})\times_{(\underline{S},\mathcal{M}_{S,0})}S
.

Note that the class of log smooth (resp. log étale) morphisms in the previous sense is closed under composition and base-change (in the category of integral log schemes). Also, note that by assumption, the underlying morphism of schemes
\underline{X}\to\underline{S}
is locally of finite presentation.

Remark 2.3.2
.

Let
S
be a quasi-coherent integral log scheme.
In light of
[
17
, Proposition 2.4]
, one has the following result. Let
f\colon X\to Y
be any morphism of log smooth log schemes over
S
. Then, étale locally on
X
,
Y
and
S
, there is a fine log structure
\mathcal{M}_{S,0}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{S}
on
\underline{S}
and a morphism
f_{0}\colon(\underline{X},\mathcal{M}_{X,0})\to(\underline{Y},\mathcal{M}_{Y,0})
of log smooth log schemes over
(\underline{S},\mathcal{M}_{S,0})
such that
f
is the pullback of
f_{0}
along
(\underline{S},\mathcal{M}_{S,0})\to S
. See also Proposition
3.1.6
below.

Definition 2.3.3
.

Let
S=\operatorname{Spec}(A,M_{A})
be an affine integral log scheme. A morphism
X\to S
of log schemes is
chart log smooth
(resp.
chart log étale
) if, étale locally on
\underline{X}
, there exists a chart
P\to\Gamma(X,\mathcal{M}_{X})
over
M_{A}
such that the following conditions hold.

(1)

P
is integral and relatively coherent over
M_{A}
, that is,
P
is finitely presented over
M_{A}
;

(2)

the map
M_{A}^{\mathrm{gp}}\to P^{\mathrm{gp}}
is injective, and the torsion part of its cokernel is a finite group of order invertible in
A
(resp. the cokernel is a finite group of order invertible in
A
);

(3)

the induced morphism of schemes
X\to\operatorname{Spec}(A)\times_{\operatorname{Spec}(A[M_{A}])}\operatorname{Spec}(A[P])
is étale.

Remark 2.3.4
.

(1) This definition depends a priori on the choice of the chart
M_{A}\to A
. However, if
M_{A}
and
\mathcal{M}_{X}
are fine, then the above notion of smoothness coincide with Kato’s original definition of smoothness in terms of infinitesimal lifting property
[
55
, §3.3]
. In particular, it is independent on the choice of the chart. See
[
59
, Remark A.13]
.

(2) One could insist on an additional condition, namely that the morphism
M_{A}\to P
is integral. This guarantees that the underlying morphism of schemes is flat. This is automatically satisfied when
M_{A}
is a valuative monoid in light of
[
78
, Proposition I.4.6.3(5)]
.

Proposition 2.3.5
.

Let
f\colon X\to S
be a morphism of integral quasi-coherent log schemes. Then
f
is log smooth in the sense of Definition
2.3.1
if and only if, strict étale locally on
X
and
S
, it is chart log smooth.

Proof.

This is essentially discussed in
[
59
]
: we review the argument. A log smooth morphism in the sense of Definition
2.3.1
is chart log smooth, since locally it is the base change of a log smooth morphism locally of finite presentation between fine log schemes, and Kato’s chart lemma applies. Conversely, assume that
S=\operatorname{Spec}(A,M_{A})
and that
f
satisfies the conditions of Definition
2.3.3
.
The assumption that
P
is finitely presented (as monoid) over
M_{A}
implies that
A[P]
is of finite presentation (as a ring) over
A[M_{A}]
so that, in particular,
\underline{X}
is locally of finite presentation over
A
. By
[
59
, Proposition A.15]
, this implies the following: there exists a
fine
submonoid
M_{A,0}\subset M_{A}
and a fine log structure
\mathcal{M}_{X,0}
on
X
with a chart
M_{A,0}\to P_{0}
such that
(X,\mathcal{M}_{X,0})\to\operatorname{Spec}(A,M_{A,0})
is log smooth in the sense of
[
55
, §3.3]
, and
(X,\mathcal{M}_{X})=(X,\mathcal{M}_{X,0})\times_{\operatorname{Spec}(A,M_{A,0})}\operatorname{Spec}(A,M_{A})
. This completes the proof. ∎

Remark 2.3.6
.

From this moment on, we will further restrict our objects of study to be quasi-coherent
saturated
log schemes. This condition is commonly satisfied in all concrete applications.

We freely use some basic properties of toric varieties and fans. Suppose that
P
is an fs monoid.
Let
\operatorname{Spec}(P)
be the monoscheme associated with
P
as in
[
78
, Definition II.1.2.1]

(denoted by
\mathrm{spec}(P)
in loc. cit.).
Let
\mathbb{A}_{P}
be the fs affine log scheme
\operatorname{Spec}(P\to\mathbb{Z}[P])
,
see the discussion following
[
78
, Definition III.1.2.3]
.

There is a fully faithful functor from the category of toric fans to the category of monoschemes,
see
[
78
, Theorem II.1.9.3]
.
If
P
is an fs monoid such that
P^{\mathrm{gp}}
is torsion-free, then
\operatorname{Spec}(P)
corresponds to the fan in the lattice
(P^{\mathrm{gp}})^{\vee}
with a single maximal cone
P^{\vee}
,
where
(-)^{\vee}
denotes the dual cone or lattice.
If
\Sigma
is a toric fan,
we let
\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}
denote the associated log scheme in the sense of
[
78
, p. 276, §III.1.2]
.
Observe that the underlying scheme
\underline{\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}}
is the toric variety associated with
\Sigma
.
For every fs log scheme
X
,
we set

X-\partial X:=\{x\in X:\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{X,x}=0\}.

This is an open subset of
X
by
[
78
, Proposition III.1.2.8]
.
We regard
X-\partial X
as an open subscheme of
X
.

Remark 2.3.7
.

In fact, the proof of
[
78
, Proposition III.1.2.8]
works under the weaker assumption that
X
is coherent, that is, if Zariski locally on
X
there is a chart subordinate to a finitely generated monoid (in particular, without the assumption of being integral or saturated).

Definition 2.3.8
.

Suppose
f\colon Y\to X
is a morphism in
\mathbf{lSch}
.
We say that
f
is a
dividing cover
if
f
is a proper surjective log étale monomorphism.

In this case, the naturally induced morphism of schemes
Y-\partial Y\to X-\partial X
is an isomorphism by

[
22
, Remark 3.1.7(1)]
.

Example 2.3.9
.

Every log blow-up
[
78
, Definition III.2.6.2]
is a dividing cover, see
[
22
, Example A.11.1]
.

Example 2.3.10
.

Here’s an elementary way to produce dividing covers.
Suppose
X
is an fs log scheme with a strict morphism
X\to\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}
,
where
\Sigma
is a fan.
For any subdivision of fans
\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
,
the naturally induced morphism
\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}\to\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}
is Zariski locally of the form

\mathbb{A}_{\theta}\colon\mathbb{A}_{Q}\to\mathbb{A}_{P}
,
where
\theta\colon P\to Q
is a monomorphism of fs monoids such that
P^{\mathrm{gp}}\to Q^{\mathrm{gp}}
is an isomorphism of
abelian groups.
Hence the induced morphism
\mathbb{A}_{\theta}
is log étale by
[
78
, Corollary IV.3.1.10]
. By base change, the same holds for the projection
p\colon X\times_{\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}}\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}\to X
.
Since
\underline{\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}}\to\underline{\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}}
is proper, the same holds for
p
.
By
[
22
, Lemma A.11.4]
,
p
is universally surjective.
In conclusion,
p
is a dividing cover.
Geometrically, any fan subdivision as above is realized as a blow-up of the corresponding toric variety
\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}
. A typical example of a fan subdivision arises in the resolution of singularities of
\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}
.

Remark 2.3.11
.

A morphism
f\colon Y\to X
is called a
log modification
if Zariski locally on
X
,
there exists a log blow-up
g\colon Z\to Y
such that
fg
is a log blow-up too.
In
[
22
, Proposition A.11.9]
, it was claimed that a log modification is the same as a dividing cover.
However, this is false, see the second part of
[
78
, Example IV.4.3.4]
.
To fix this,
one replaces “log modification” by “dividing cover” in
[
22
]
,
so that
[
22
, Proposition A.11.9]
becomes obsolete.
Example A.11.8 is the only part in
[
22
]
where the definition of log modification is used;
however, we can replace this by Example
2.3.10
.
In summary,
this correction does not affect the content of
[
22
]
.

The notion of cd-structure was introduced by Voevodsky
[
102
]
.

Definition 2.3.12
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category with an initial object.
A
cd-structure
on
\mathcal{C}
is a collection
P
of commutative squares of the form

(2.3.1)
