{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.89159pt}{-13.45142pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

such that
Q\cong Q_{i}\times_{S_{i}}S
.
Using
[
34
, Théorème IV.8.10.5(iii)]
,
we may further assume that
u_{i}
and
v_{i}
are open immersions.
Let
X_{i}
(resp.
Y_{i}
) be the log scheme with the underlying scheme
\underline{X_{i}}
(resp.
\underline{Y_{i}}
) and with the compactifying log structure associated with
u_{i}
(resp.
v_{i}
).

We need to show that there exists a morphism
j\to i
in
I
such that
X_{j},Y_{j}\in\mathbf{lSm}/S
,
U_{j}\cong X_{j}-\partial X_{j}
,
V_{i}\cong Y_{j}-\partial Y_{j}
, and
f_{j}(V_{j})\subset U_{j}
since we can recover a morphism
f_{j}\colon Y_{j}\to X_{j}
by
[
78
, Proposition III.1.6.2]
.
If
f
is log étale (resp. log smooth),
then we also require that
f_{j}
is log étale (resp. log smooth).
This question is étale local on
\underline{X}
and
\underline{Y}
by Lemma
3.1.3
.
Hence, we may assume that there exists a chart
\theta\colon P\to Q
of
f
.
If
f
is log étale (resp. log smooth),
then by
[
78
, Theorem IV.3.3.1]
,
we may also assume that the induced morphisms
X\to S\times\mathbb{A}_{P}
and
Y\to X\times_{\mathbb{A}_{P}}\mathbb{A}_{Q}
are strict étale,
\theta
is injective,
and
\lvert\operatorname{Coker}(\theta^{\mathrm{gp}})\rvert
(resp.
\lvert\operatorname{Coker}(\theta^{\mathrm{gp}})^{\mathrm{tor}}\rvert
) is invertible in
\mathcal{O}_{X}
.
If
f
is a log étale monomorphism,
then we also require that
f_{j}
is a log étale monomorphism.
This question is Zariski local on
\underline{X}
and
\underline{Y}
by Lemma
3.1.3
.
Hence, by
[
22
, Lemma A.11.3]
(see also Remark
2.3.20
),
we may also assume that the induced morphism
Y\to X\times_{\mathbb{A}_{P}}\mathbb{A}_{Q}
is an open immersion and
\theta^{\mathrm{gp}}
is an isomorphism.

By
[
34
, Théorème IV.8.8.2]
,
there exists a morphism
j\to i
and a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{Y_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{X_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{j}\times\underline{\mathbb{A}_{Q}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{j}\times\underline{\mathbb{A}_{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}\times\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\theta}}}

such that
U_{j}\cong S_{j}\times(\mathbb{A}_{P}-\partial\mathbb{A}_{P})
and
V_{j}\cong S_{j}\times(\mathbb{A}_{Q}-\partial A_{Q})
.
If
f
is log étale (resp. log smooth),
then by
[
34
, Proposition IV.17.7.8]
,
we may also assume that the induced morphisms
\underline{X_{j}}\to S_{j}\times\underline{\mathbb{A}_{P}}
and
\underline{Y_{j}}\to\underline{X_{j}}\times_{\underline{\mathbb{A}_{P}}}\underline{\mathbb{A}_{Q}}
are étale.
If
f
is a log étale monomorphism,
then by
[
34
, Théorème IV.8.10.5(3)]
,
we may also assume that the induced morphism
\underline{Y_{j}}\to\underline{X_{j}}\times_{\underline{\mathbb{A}_{P}}}\underline{\mathbb{A}_{Q}}
is an open immersion.
The induced morphism of fs log schemes
f_{j}\colon Y_{j}\to X_{j}
satisfies the desired properties.
∎

3.2.
Properties of (pointed) log motivic spaces

The purpose of this subsection is to explore several basic properties that are satisfied in the unstable motivic
homotopy categories
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}
and
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\ast}
and their variants.
Many of the results can be transferred to
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}
and
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}
by applying infinite suspension functors. For the sake of exposition, we will restrict in this section to the case where the topology
\tau
on
\mathbf{lSch}
used to construct our categories is the dividing Nisnevich topology, unless explicitly stated.

Notation 3.2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a symmetric monoidal pointed
\infty
-category with small colimits.
Fix a symmetric monoidal functor

(3.2.1)

M\colon\mathscr{S}/S\to\mathcal{C}.

where
\mathscr{S}
is either
\mathbf{lSm}
or
\mathbf{SmlSm}
.

If
Y\to X
is a morphism in
\mathscr{S}/S
, we set
M(X/Y):=\operatorname{cofib}(M(Y)\to M(X))
for abbreviation.
We shall consider the following properties.

•

Zariski separation
If
\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\}
is a finite Zariski cover of
X
, we write

X_{I}:=X_{i_{1}}\times_{X}\cdots\times_{X}X_{i_{r}}

if
I:=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\}\subset\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
Suppose

(3.2.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

is a square in
\mathscr{S}/S
.
If

(3.2.3)

M(X^{\prime}\times_{X}X_{I}/Y^{\prime}\times_{X}X_{I})\to M(X_{I}/Y\times_{X}X_{I})

is an equivalence in
\mathcal{C}
for every nonempty
I\subset\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
then the naturally induced map

(3.2.4)

M(X^{\prime}/Y^{\prime})\to M(X/Y)

is an equivalence in
\mathcal{C}
.

•

\square
-invariance
For every
X\in\mathscr{S}/S
,
the projection
X\times\square\to X
naturally induces an equivalence in
\mathcal{C}

M(X\times\square)\to M(X).

•

sNis
-descent
If (
3.2.2
) is a strict Nisnevich distinguished triangle,
then the naturally induced map

M(X^{\prime}/Y^{\prime})\to M(X/Y)

is an equivalence in
\mathcal{C}
.

•

Invariance under admissible blow-ups along smooth centers

If
Y\to X
is an admissible blow-up along a smooth center in
\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
,
then the naturally induced map

M(Y)\to M(X)

is an equivalence in
\mathcal{C}
.

The functor
M
is, informally, the functor that associates to an object
X
its
motive
.

Throughout this subsection, we assume that
M

satisfies the basic properties listed in Notation
3.2.1
.

Remark 3.2.2
.

To study
M
we will attempt to apply the arguments in
[
22
, §7]
.
However,
in our setting,
we
cannot
take the following for given:

(3.2.5)

For a map
\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{F}
in
\mathcal{C}
,
\operatorname{cofib}(\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{F})\simeq 0
implies
\mathcal{G}\simeq\mathcal{F}
.

But this is the only obstruction, in the sense that if an argument in
[
22
, §7]

does not appeal to (
3.2.5
),
then it carries over to
M
as above.
More precisely,
it will be important to provide alternatives to
[
22
, Propositions C.1.5, C.1.7]

since their proofs are based upon (
3.2.5
).
With this in mind,
we will proceed by adapting the arguments in
[
22
, §7]
to our setting.

Remark 3.2.3
.

In
[
22
, §7]
,
we deduced basic properties of logarithmic motives by considering a functor

(3.2.6)

M\colon\mathbf{Square}_{\mathscr{S}/S}\to\mathcal{T},

where
\mathscr{S}
is either
\mathbf{lSm}
or
\mathbf{SmlSm}
,
S
is a scheme, and
\mathcal{T}
is a triangulated category.
We refer to
[
22
, Appendix C.1]
for the definition of
\mathbf{Square}_{\mathscr{S}/S}
.

By considering total cofibers of cubes and the homotopy category of
\mathcal{C}
,
we can recover (
3.2.6
) from (
3.2.1
) if
\mathcal{C}
is stable and
\mathcal{T}=\mathbf{Ho}(\mathcal{C})
.
Furthermore,
the properties (Sq) and (MSq) in
[
22
, Appendix C.1]
are automatically satisfied since the
cofiber construction is unique for
\infty
-categories unlike the cone construction which is not
unique for triangulated categories.
Hence,
in order to incorporate unstable settings,
we proceed with (
3.2.1
) instead of (
3.2.6
).

First we discuss an example of fundamental importance for our purposes.

Proposition 3.2.4
.

Suppose that
\tau
is a topology in
\mathbf{lSch}
generated by a family of morphisms in
\mathbf{lSm}
and let
S
be a scheme.
Then
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\ast,\tau}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
admits a functor

M\colon\mathbf{SmlSm}/S\to\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\ast,t}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)

satisfying the above properties.

Proof.

Let
M\colon\mathbf{SmlSm}/S\to\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\ast,t}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
be the naturally induced symmetric monoidal functor
sending
X\in\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
to
X_{+}
.
Let
\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\}
be a finite Zariski cover of
X
.
To verify the Zariski separation property,
suppose (
3.2.5
) is a cartesian square in
\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
.
If (
3.2.3
) is an equivalence for all nonempty
I:=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\}\subset\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
with
X_{I}:=X_{i_{1}}\times_{X}\cdots\times_{X}X_{i_{r}}
,
then (
3.2.4
) is also an equivalence by Čech descent for the Zariski topology.
The other properties are trivially satisfied.
∎

Suppose
X
is a scheme in
\mathbf{Sm}/S
with a strict normal crossing divisor
Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{n}
.
If
\mathcal{Y}
is the cubical horn associated with the Zariski cover
\{X-Z_{i}\to X\}_{1\leq i\leq n}

in the sense of Definition
A.0.9
, then by Zariski descent there is an equivalence

\mathop{\rm colim}(\mathcal{Y})\simeq X-(Z_{1}\cap\cdots\cap Z_{r})

in
\mathcal{H}(S)
.
Our compactified version of the previous equivalence reads as follows.

Proposition 3.2.5
.

Suppose
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
and
X
is a scheme in
\mathbf{Sm}/S
with a strict normal crossing divisor
Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{n}
.
Then there is an equivalence

\mathop{\rm colim}(\mathrm{horn}(X,Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{n}))\simeq({\mathrm{Bl}}_{W}X,E)

in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
, where
W=Z_{1}\cap\cdots\cap Z_{n}
and
E
is the exceptional divisor.

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{Z}_{i}
be the strict transform of
Z_{i}
in
{\mathrm{Bl}}_{W}X
for all
1\leq i\leq n
.
Then
\{{\mathrm{Bl}}_{W}X-\widetilde{Z}_{i}\to{\mathrm{Bl}}_{W}X\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
is a Zariski cover.
Apply Proposition
2.5.10
to the scheme
{\mathrm{Bl}}_{W}E
with the strict normal crossing divisor
\widetilde{Z}_{1}+\cdots+\widetilde{Z}_{n}+E
to have an equivalence

(3.2.7)

\mathop{\rm colim}(\mathrm{horn}(({\mathrm{Bl}}_{W}X,E),\widetilde{Z}_{1}+\cdots+\widetilde{Z}_{n}))\simeq({\mathrm{Bl}}_{W}X,E)

in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
.
For all integers
1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{r}\leq n
, the morphism

({\mathrm{Bl}}_{W}X,\widetilde{Z}_{i_{1}}+\cdots+\widetilde{Z}_{i_{r}}+E)\xrightarrow{\simeq}(X,Z_{i_{1}}+\cdots+Z_{i_{r}})

is an equivalence in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
since it is an admissible blow-up along a smooth center.
Hence we have an equivalence

(3.2.8)

\mathop{\rm colim}(\mathrm{horn}(({\mathrm{Bl}}_{W}X,E),\widetilde{Z}_{1}+\cdots+\widetilde{Z}_{n}))\simeq\mathop{\rm colim}(\mathrm{horn}(X,Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{n}))

in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
.
Combine (
3.2.7
) and (
3.2.8
) to have the desired isomorphism.
∎

Next we recall logarithmic vector bundles and their Thom spaces.

Definition 3.2.6
.

If
Y\in\mathbf{lSch}
,
then a
vector bundle on
Y
is a strict morphism
p\colon\mathcal{E}\to Y
in
\mathbf{lSch}

such that
\underline{p}\colon\underline{\mathcal{E}}\to\underline{Y}
is a vector bundle.
The
rank of
p
is the rank of the underlying vector bundle
\underline{p}
.

Definition 3.2.7
.

Suppose
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
and
X\in\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
.
If
p\colon\mathcal{E}\to X
is a rank
d
vector bundle with zero section
Z\to\mathcal{E}
,
then the
Thom space
associated with
p
is defined as

(3.2.9)

\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}/X):=M(\mathcal{E}/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}(\mathcal{E}),E))\in\mathcal{C},

where
E
is the exceptional divisor on
{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}(\mathcal{E})
.
We often omit
X
in the notation when no confusion seems likely to arise.

Remark 3.2.8
.

In
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy theory,
there are at least two definitions of the Thom space of a vector bundle
\underline{p}\colon\underline{\mathcal{E}}\to\underline{X}
,
namely

(3.2.10)

\mathrm{Th}(\underline{\mathcal{E}}/\underline{X}):=M(\underline{\mathcal{E}}/(\underline{\mathcal{E}}-\underline{Z}))

and

(3.2.11)

\mathrm{Th}(\underline{\mathcal{E}}/\underline{X}):=\underline{p}_{\sharp}\underline{i}_{*}\mathbf{1}.

The localization property
[
70
, Theorem 2.21, p. 114]

implies the equivalence between (
3.2.10
) and (
3.2.11
) in
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy theory.
The localization property
seems to fail in
\square
-homotopy theory as explained in the introduction of
[
84
]
,
so we do not expect that (
3.2.10
) and (
3.2.11
) are equivalent in
\square
-homotopy theory.
However,
it is currently unclear whether (
3.2.9
) and (
3.2.11
) are equivalent or not.

Definition 3.2.9
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be pointed by
\operatorname{pt}
.
We write
\wedge
for the monoidal product of
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathbf{1}
for the monoidal unit of
\mathcal{C}
.
For all
\mathcal{F}\in\mathcal{C}
and integers
p\geq q\geq 0
, we set

\Sigma^{p,q}\mathcal{F}:=M(T)^{\wedge q}\wedge S^{p-q}\wedge\mathcal{F}

and
\Sigma^{p,q}:=\Sigma^{p,q}\mathbf{1}
. See Definition
2.5.19

Proposition 3.2.10
.

Suppose
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
,
X\in\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
, and
p\colon\mathcal{E}\to X
is a rank
d
trivial bundle.
Then, in
\mathcal{C}
, there is a canonical equivalence

\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}/X)\simeq\Sigma^{2d,d}M(X).

Proof.

This is an analogue of
[
22
, Proposition 7.4.5]
.
By base change (since the bundle is trivial), we reduce to the case when
X=S
.
Let
H_{1},\ldots,H_{d}
be the standard hyperplanes in
\mathbb{A}^{d}
.
Due to Proposition
3.2.5
, there is an equivalence in
\mathcal{C}

(3.2.12)

\mathop{\rm colim}(\mathrm{horn}(M(\mathbb{A}^{d},H_{1}+\cdots+H_{d})))\simeq M({\mathrm{Bl}}_{O}\mathbb{A}^{d},E).

Here
O:=(0,\ldots,0)\in\mathbb{A}^{d}
and
E
is the exceptional divisor.
Due to Proposition
A.0.8
, there is an equivalence

(3.2.13)

\Sigma^{2d,d}\simeq\mathop{\rm colim}(\mathrm{horn}(M(\mathbb{A}^{d},H_{1}+\cdots+H_{d})))

in
\mathcal{C}
.
Combine (
3.2.12
) and (
3.2.13
) to deduce the desired equivalence.
∎

Example 3.2.11
.

If
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
,
then Propositions
2.5.12
and
3.2.10
yield equivalences

(3.2.14)

M(\mathbb{P}^{n}/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{O}(\mathbb{P}^{n}),E))\simeq M(\mathbb{A}^{n}/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{O}(\mathbb{A}^{n}),E))\simeq\Sigma^{2n,n}

in
\mathcal{C}
for every integer
n\geq 1
.

We will frequently encounter the following situation.

Lemma 3.2.12
.

Suppose
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
and let
f_{0},f_{1}\colon X\to Y
be morphisms in
\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
.
If there is a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times\square}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

in
\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
such that
p
is a composite of admissible blow-ups along smooth centers,
pi_{0}
(resp.
pi_{1}
) is the zero section (resp. one section),
f_{0}=gi_{0}^{\prime}
, and
f_{1}=gi_{1}^{\prime}
, then
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
are equivalent in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
.

Proof.

By definition,
p
is an equivalence in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
.
Since the projection
X\times\square\to X
is an equivalence in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
,
pi_{0}^{\prime}
and
pi_{1}^{\prime}
are equivalent in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
.
From these, we deduce that
i_{0}^{\prime}
and
i_{1}^{\prime}
are equivalent in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
.
Compose
i_{0}^{\prime}
and
i_{1}^{\prime}
with
g
to complete the proof.
∎

Lemma 3.2.13
.

Suppose
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
and let
f\colon\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{O}^{2})\to\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{O}^{2})
be the map given by the matrix

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&1\\
0&1\end{array}\right)\colon\mathcal{O}^{2}\to\mathcal{O}^{2}.

Then there is a homotopy
f\simeq\operatorname{id}
in
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

We set
A:=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
and consider the Zariski cover consisting of

A_{1}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x,y]),\;A_{2}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[1/x,y]).

Let
B
be the blow-up of
A
along the closed subscheme
V:=([0:1],0)
,
which has a Zariski cover consisting of

B_{1}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x/y,y]),\;B_{2}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[y/x,x]),\;B_{3}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[1/x,y]).

The scheme
\mathbb{A}^{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
has a Zariski cover consisting of

C_{1}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x,y,t]),\;C_{2}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x,y,1/t])\cong\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x,x/t+y,1/t]).

Let
C
be the blow-up of
\mathbb{A}^{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
along
(x/t+y=0)\cap(1/t=0)
in
C_{2}
;
it has a Zariski cover consisting of

C_{1},\;C_{21}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x,1/t,x+ty]),\;C_{22}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x,x/t+y,1/(x+ty)]).

There are morphisms
C_{1},C_{21}\to A_{1}
and
C_{22}\to A_{2}
determined by

(3.2.15)

x\mapsto x+ty,\;y\mapsto y.

We glue the latter morphisms to obtain

(3.2.16)

C\to A.

We consider the blow-up
D
of
C
with the Zariski cover consisting of

\displaystyle D_{1}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[y/x,x,t]),\;D_{2}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x/y,y,t]),

\displaystyle D_{3}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x/y,1/t,ty]),\;D_{4}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x/y,y,1/(ty)]),

\displaystyle D_{5}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x,y/x,1/(ty)]),\;D_{6}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[tx,y/x,x/(ty)]),

\displaystyle D_{7}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[1/t,tx,ty/x]),\;D_{8}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x,1/(tx),ty/x]),

\displaystyle D_{9}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x/(ty),1/(tx),ty]).

To aid the exposition,
we include the following toric figure illustrating the
D_{i}
’s.

Let us explain how
D_{3}
arises in the above figure.
Denote by
\sigma_{3}
the monoid in
\mathbb{Z}^{3}
generated by
(1,0,0)
,
(1,1,0)
, and
(1,1,-1)
.
The procedure of obtaining the coordinate ring from a cone in a fan is by taking the dual monoid.
Hence the coordinate ring of
D_{3}
is generated by the dual monoid
\sigma_{3}^{\vee}
of
\sigma_{3}
.
The dual monoid can be described as

\sigma_{3}^{\vee}=\{(a,b,c)\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}:a\geq 0,\;a+b\geq 0,\;a+b-c\geq 0\}.

From the above,
we can see that
\sigma_{3}^{\vee}
is generated by
(1,-1,0)
,
(0,1,1)
, and
(0,0,-1)
.
Correspondingly, we form
x^{1}y^{-1}t^{0}
,
x^{0}y^{1}t^{1}
, and
x^{0}y^{0}t^{-1}
,
which produce the coordinate ring of
D_{3}
.

The closed subscheme
x/(ty)+1=0
(resp.
1/t=0
, resp.
1/(tx)=0
) in
D_{6}
(resp.
D_{7}
, resp.
D_{8}
)
has a closure in
D
, which we denote by
Z_{1}
(resp.
Z_{2}

).

.

Let

be the blow-up of

along

, let

in

in

for

.
Furthermore, let

be the blow-up of

along

, and let

be the preimage of
D_{i}
in

for

.
Since
D_{i}\cap Z_{1}\cap Z_{2}=D_{i}\cap Z_{1}\cap Z_{3}=\emptyset
for

, we have
D_{i}=D_{i}^{\prime}=D_{i}^{\prime\prime}
for

.
Similarly, we have
D_{i}=D_{i}^{\prime}
for

and
D_{i}^{\prime}=D_{i}^{\prime\prime}
for

.

Since
(y/x\cdot t+1=0)\cap(y/x=0)=\emptyset
in
D_{1}
,
D_{1}
has a Zariski cover consisting of

D_{11}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[y/x,x,t,x/(x+ty)]),\;D_{12}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[y/x,x,t,x/y]).

Since

in
D_{3}
,

has a Zariski cover consisting of

D_{31}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x/y,1/t,ty,ty/(x+ty)]),\;D_{32}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x/y,1/t,ty,t]).

Moreover,

(1/(ty)\cdot y\cdot x/y+1=0)\cap((1/(ty)=0)\cup(y=0))=\emptyset
in
D_{4}
,
so that
D_{4}
has a Zariski cover consisting of

D_{41}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x/y,y,1/(ty),ty/(x+ty)]),\;D_{42}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x/y,y,1/(ty),ty,1/y]).

The formula (
3.2.15
) gives morphisms

(3.2.17)

D_{12},D_{2},D_{32},D_{42}\to B_{1},\;D_{11},D_{31}\to B_{2},\;D_{41}\to B_{3}.

Since
(x\cdot 1/(ty)+1=0)\cap((x=0)\cup(1/(ty)=0))=\emptyset
in
D_{5}
,
D_{5}
has a Zariski cover consisting of

D_{51}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x,y/x,1/(ty),ty/(x+ty)]),\;D_{52}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x,y/x,1/(ty),1/x,ty]).

The closed subscheme
D_{52}\cap Z_{1}\cap Z_{3}
is given by
(x+ty=0)\cap(y/x)
.
Hence
D_{5}^{\prime}
has a Zariski cover consisting of
D_{51}
and

\displaystyle D_{521}^{\prime}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x+ty,y/(x^{2}+txy),1/(ty),1/x,ty]),

\displaystyle D_{522}^{\prime}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[y/x,x^{2}/y+tx,1/(ty),1/x,ty]).

The formula (
3.2.15
) gives morphisms

(3.2.18)

D_{51}\to B_{3},\;D_{521}^{\prime}\to B_{2},\;D_{522}^{\prime}\to B_{1}.

The scheme
D_{6}^{\prime\prime}
has a Zariski cover consisting of

D_{61}^{\prime\prime}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[tx,y/x,x^{2}/(ty^{2})+x/y]),\;D_{62}^{\prime\prime}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[tx,x/(ty)+1,ty^{2}/(x^{2}+txy)]).

The formula (
3.2.15
) gives morphisms
D_{61}^{\prime\prime},D_{62}^{\prime\prime}\to A_{1}
.
If we set
a=tx
,
b=y/x
, and
c=x^{2}/(ty^{2})+x/y
, then
y=ab^{2}(bc-1)
and
x+ty=ab^{2}c
.
If we set
a=tx
,
b=x/(ty)+1
, and
c=ty^{2}/(x^{2}+txy)
, then
y=ab^{2}c^{2}(b-1)
and
x+ty=ab^{2}c
.
Hence the preimage
V_{61}^{\prime\prime}
(resp.
V_{62}^{\prime\prime}
) of
V\subset A
in
D_{61}^{\prime\prime}
(resp.
D_{62}^{\prime\prime}
) is a Cartier divisor, so the blow-up of
D_{61}^{\prime\prime}
(resp.
D_{62}^{\prime\prime}
) along
V_{61}^{\prime\prime}
(resp.
V_{62}^{\prime\prime}
) is again
D_{61}^{\prime\prime}
(resp.
D_{62}^{\prime\prime}
).
It follows that we have naturally induced morphisms

(3.2.19)

D_{61}^{\prime\prime},D_{62}^{\prime\prime}\to B.

The scheme
D_{7}^{\prime\prime}
has a Zariski cover consisting of

D_{71}^{\prime\prime}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[1/t,tx,t^{2}y/x+t]),\;D_{72}^{\prime\prime}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[tx,ty/x+1,x/(tx+t^{2}y)]).

The formula (
3.2.15
) gives morphisms
D_{71}^{\prime\prime},D_{72}^{\prime\prime}\to A_{1}
.
If we set
a=1/t
,
b=tx
, and
c=t^{2}y/x+t
, then
y=a^{2}b(ac-1)
and
x+ty=a^{2}bc
.
If we set
a=tx
,
b=ty/x+1
, and
c=x/(tx+t^{2}y)
, then
y=ab^{2}c^{2}(b-1)
and
x+ty=ab^{2}c
.
Hence the preimage
V_{71}^{\prime\prime}
(resp.
V_{72}^{\prime\prime}
) of
V
in
D_{71}^{\prime\prime}
(resp.
D_{72}^{\prime\prime}
) is a Cartier divisor.
As before, we have naturally induced morphisms

(3.2.20)

D_{71}^{\prime\prime},D_{72}^{\prime\prime}\to B.

The scheme
D_{8}^{\prime}
has a Zariski cover consisting of

D_{81}^{\prime}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x,ty/x+1,1/(tx+t^{2}y)]),\;D_{82}^{\prime}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x,1/(tx),tx+t^{2}y]).

The formula (
3.2.15
) gives a morphism

(3.2.21)

D_{81}^{\prime}\to B_{2}.

The scheme
D_{8}^{\prime\prime}
has a Zariski cover consisting of

\displaystyle D_{82}^{\prime},\;D_{821}^{\prime\prime}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x,1/(tx),t+t^{2}y/x]),

\displaystyle D_{822}^{\prime\prime}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[1/(tx),tx+t^{2}y,x/(tx+t^{2}y)]).

The formula (
3.2.15
) gives morphisms
D_{81}^{\prime},D_{82}^{\prime\prime}\to A_{1}
.
If we set
a=x
,
b=1/(tx)
, and
c=t+t^{2}y/x
, then
y=a^{2}b(abc-1)
and
x+ty=a^{2}bc
.
If we set
a=1/(tx)
,
b=tx+t^{2}y
, and
c=x/(tx+t^{2}y)
, then
ab^{2}c^{2}(ab-1)=y
and
x+ty=ab^{2}c
.
Hence the preimage
V_{821}^{\prime\prime}
(resp.
V_{822}^{\prime\prime}
) of
V
in
D_{821}^{\prime\prime}
(resp.
D_{822}^{\prime\prime}
) is a Cartier divisor.
As before, we have naturally induced morphisms

(3.2.22)

D_{821}^{\prime\prime},D_{822}^{\prime\prime}\to B.

The scheme
D_{9}^{\prime}
has a Zariski cover consisting of

D_{91}^{\prime}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x/(ty)+1,1/(tx+t^{2}y),ty]),\;D_{92}^{\prime}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[1/(tx),x^{2}/y+tx,ty]).

The formula (
3.2.15
) gives a morphism

(3.2.23)

D_{91}^{\prime}\to B_{2}.

Finally,
the scheme
D_{9}^{\prime\prime}
has a Zariski cover consisting of

\displaystyle D_{91}^{\prime},\;D_{921}^{\prime\prime}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[1/(tx),ty,x^{2}/(ty^{2})+x/y]),

\displaystyle D_{922}^{\prime\prime}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[1/(tx),x^{2}/y+tx,ty^{2}/(x^{2}+txy)]).

The formula (
3.2.15
) gives morphisms
D_{91}^{\prime},D_{92}^{\prime\prime}\to A_{1}
.
If we set
a=1/(tx)
,
b=ty
, and
c=x^{2}/(ty^{2})+x/y
, then
x+ty=ab^{2}c
and
y=(ab^{2}c-b)ab
.
If we set
a=1/(tx)
,
b=x^{2}/y+tx
, and
c=ty^{2}/(x^{2}+txy)
, then
x+ty=ab^{2}c
and
y=(ab^{2}c-bc)abc
.
Hence the preimage
V_{921}^{\prime\prime}
(resp.
V_{922}^{\prime\prime}
) of
V
in
D_{921}^{\prime\prime}
(resp.
D_{922}^{\prime\prime}
) is a Cartier divisor.
As before, we have naturally induced morphisms

(3.2.24)

D_{921}^{\prime\prime},D_{922}^{\prime\prime}\to B.

Glue (
3.2.17
)–(
3.2.24
) all together to have a morphism

(3.2.25)

D^{\prime\prime}\to B.

Together with (
3.2.16
), we have a commutative square

(3.2.26)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A.}

Let
\widetilde{B}
(resp.
\widetilde{C}
, resp.
\widetilde{D^{\prime\prime}}
) be the log scheme whose underlying scheme is
B
(resp.
C
, resp.
D^{\prime\prime}
) and whose log structure is the compactifying log structure associated with the open immersion
A-V\to B
(resp.
\mathbb{A}^{2}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\to C
, resp.
(\mathbb{A}^{2}-(0,0))\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\to D^{\prime\prime}
).
The square (
3.2.26
) gives a map

u\colon M(\widetilde{C}/\widetilde{D^{\prime\prime}})\to M(A/\widetilde{B})

in
\mathcal{C}
.
By strict Nisnevich descent, we have an equivalence in
\mathcal{C}

v\colon\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{O}^{2})\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(A/\widetilde{B}).

The naturally induced morphisms
\widetilde{C}\to\mathbb{A}^{2}\times\square
and
\widetilde{D^{\prime\prime}}\to({\mathrm{Bl}}_{(0,0)\times\square}(\mathbb{A}^{2}\times\square),E)
are admissible blow-ups along smooth centers, where
E
is the exceptional divisor.
The zero section and one section
i_{0},i_{1}\colon\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{O}^{2})\rightrightarrows\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{O}^{2})\wedge M(\square)
can be lifted to maps
i_{0}^{\prime},i_{1}^{\prime}\colon\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{O}^{2})\rightrightarrows M(\widetilde{C}/\widetilde{D^{\prime\prime}})
.
Since
u\circ i_{0}^{\prime}=v\circ\operatorname{id}
and
u\circ i_{1}^{\prime}=v\circ f
, we have
f\simeq\operatorname{id}
as in Lemma
3.2.12
.
∎

Remark 3.2.14
.

The analogous result of Lemma
3.2.13
in
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy theory can be easily proven by considering the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopies

\mathbb{A}^{2}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\to\mathbb{A}^{2}\text{ and }(\mathbb{A}^{2}-(0,0))\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\to\mathbb{A}^{2}-(0,0)

given by
(x,y,t)\mapsto(x+ty,y)
. In our case we consider a compactification of the above morphisms, and this creates a nontrivial geometric problem.

Proposition 3.2.15
.

If
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
,
then the permutation
(123)\colon M(T)^{\wedge 3}\to M(T)^{\wedge 3}
is equivalent to the identity on
\mathcal{C}
. In particular,
M(T)
is a symmetric object of
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

Owing to Proposition
3.2.10
, there is a canonical equivalence in
\mathcal{C}

M(T)^{\wedge n}\simeq\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{O}^{n}).

Let
f\colon\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{O}^{3})\to\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{O}^{3})
be the map given by the matrix

A:=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&1&0\\
0&0&1\\
1&0&0\end{array}\right)\colon\mathcal{O}^{3}\to\mathcal{O}^{3}.

It suffices to show that
f\simeq\operatorname{id}
in
\mathcal{C}
.
Similarly to the above,
let
g\colon\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{O}^{2})\to\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{O}^{2})
be the map given by the matrix

B:=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
-1&0\end{array}\right)\colon\mathcal{O}^{2}\to\mathcal{O}^{2}.

Apply Lemma
3.2.13
to the relation

B=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&1\\
0&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
-1&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&1\\
0&1\end{array}\right)

to obtain a homotopy

(3.2.27)

g\simeq\operatorname{id}

in
\mathcal{C}
.
Apply (
3.2.27
) to the relation

A=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&-1&0\\
1&0&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&0&-1\\
0&1&0\end{array}\right)

to finish the proof.
∎

Proposition 3.2.16
.

Suppose
\mathcal{E}_{1}\to X_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}\to X_{2}
are vector bundles in
\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
, where
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
.
Then there is a canonical equivalence

(3.2.28)

\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}_{1})\wedge\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}_{2})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}_{1}\times_{S}\mathcal{E}_{2})

in
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

The question is Zariski local on
S
.
Therefore, we may assume that
S
is affine.
By Proposition
3.1.5
,
we reduce to the case where
S\in\mathbf{Sch}_{noeth}
.
With this assumption, the claim
was proved in
[
22
, Proposition 7.4.15]
if
\mathcal{C}
is stable.
Since the proof used (
3.2.5
), we will explain an alternate approach.

We set
T:=\mathcal{E}_{1}\times_{S}\mathcal{E}_{2}\times_{S}\mathcal{E}_{3}
, where
\mathcal{E}_{3}:=S

(in
[
22
, Proposition 7.4.16]
we used a nontrivial
\mathcal{E}_{3}
).
In
[
22
, Construction 7.4.14]
,
we constructed 55 fs log schemes
T_{I}
corresponding to all nonempty subset
I\subset\{1,\ldots,6\}
such that

I\cap\{4,5,6\}\neq\{4,5\}
.
Furthermore,
it was shown that the morphisms
T_{14}\to T_{1}
,
T_{24}\to T_{2}
, and
T_{124}\to T_{12},T_{4}

are composites of admissible blow-ups along smooth centers.

To construct (
3.2.28
),
we first observe that
\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}_{1})\wedge\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}_{2})
is equivalent to the total cofiber of the square

is equivalent to
0
in

.
Since there is a cocartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tcofib}(Q^{\prime})}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(T_{4})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(T),}

we have an equivalence

\operatorname{tcofib}(Q^{\prime})\simeq\operatorname{cofib}(M(T_{4})\to M(T))=\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}_{1}\times_{S}\mathcal{E}_{2}).

This finishes the construction of the equivalence (
3.2.28
).
∎

Proposition 3.2.17
.

Suppose
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
and let
\mathcal{E}_{i}\to X
be a vector bundle in
\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
for
i=1,2,3
.
Then the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}_{1})\wedge\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}_{2})\wedge\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}_{1})\wedge\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}_{2}\times_{S}\mathcal{E}_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}_{1}\times_{S}\mathcal{E}_{2})\wedge\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}_{1}\times_{S}\mathcal{E}_{2}\times_{S}\mathcal{E}_{3})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

commutes in
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

As before,
we reduce to the case when
S\in\mathbf{Sch}_{noeth}
.
With this assumption, the claim
in the stable setting was established in
[
22
, Proposition 7.4.16]
.
However,
the proof does not use (
3.2.5
),
and hence applies verbatim to
\mathcal{C}
.
∎

We often regard
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
as the hyperplane defined by
x_{n}=0
in
\mathbb{P}^{n}
,
where we write
[x_{0}:\cdots:x_{n}]
for the coordinate on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
.
We are interested in the fs log scheme
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
. Clearly, this is a log compactification of
\mathbb{A}^{n}
different from
\square^{n}
.
The contractibility of
\square
trivially implies the contractibility of
\square^{n}
, but the following is not an immediate result. In fact, it is one of the main features of our theory, as seen in
[
22
]
.

Proposition 3.2.18
.

For all
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
,

X\in\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
,
and
n\geq 1
, the map

M(X\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1}))\to M(X)

naturally induced by the projection
X\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})\to X
is an equivalence in
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

The question is Zariski local on
S
.
Hence we may assume that
S
is affine.
By Proposition
3.1.4
,
we reduce to the case when
S\in\mathbf{Sch}_{noeth}
.
With this assumption, the claim
was proven in
[
22
, Proposition 7.3.1]
for
\mathcal{C}
stable.
Since (
3.2.5
) was not used in the proof, the same argument holds.
∎

Proposition 3.2.19
.

Suppose
\mathcal{E}\to X
is a vector bundle in
\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
, where
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
.
Then there is a canonical equivalence

\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E})\simeq\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{O})/\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})

in
\mathcal{C}
, where we view
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})
as the divisor at
\infty
.

Proof.

As before,
we reduce to the case when
S\in\mathbf{Sch}_{noeth}
.
With this assumption, the claim
was proven in
[
22
, Proposition 7.4.5]
for
\mathcal{C}
stable.
Again, (
3.2.5
) was not used in the proof.
∎

Proposition 3.2.20
.

Suppose
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
,
X\in\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
,
and
Z\in\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
is a strict closed subscheme of
X
having strict normal crossing with
\partial X
over
S
.
If
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
is a strict étale morphism such that
Z^{\prime}:=f^{-1}(Z)\to Z
is an isomorphism,
then the naturally induced square

(3.2.29)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z^{\prime}}X^{\prime},E^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(X^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}X,E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(X)}

is cocartesian
\mathcal{C}
, where
E
and
E^{\prime}
are the exceptional divisors.

Proof.

This was proven in
[
22
, Proposition 7.4.2]
if
\mathcal{C}
is stable.
To avoid (
3.2.5
), consider the naturally induced commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}-Z^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z^{\prime}}X^{\prime},E^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X-Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}X,E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(b)}

Both
(a)
and
(a)\cup(b)
are strict Nisnevich distinguished squares.
Apply
M(-)
to the diagram above and incorporate the pasting law to deduce that
(
3.2.29
) is cocartesian in
\mathcal{C}
.
∎

Suppose
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
, and that
X
in
\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
is of the form
(\underline{X},Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{r})
. Let

Z
be a smooth closed subscheme of
X
having strict normal crossing with
Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{r}
.
We define

{\mathrm{D}}_{Z}X:={\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}(X\times\square)-{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}X,

where we regard
Z
as the closed subscheme
Z\times\{0\}
of
X\times\square
.
The blow-up
{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}(X\times\square)
contains
Z\times\square
as a divisor.
Let
{\mathrm{N}}_{Z}X:=({\mathrm{N}}_{\underline{Z}}\underline{X})\times_{\underline{X}}X

be the normal bundle of
Z
in
X
.
With these definitions,
we obtain the commutative diagram for the deformation to the normal cone

(3.2.30)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z\times\square}({\mathrm{D}}_{Z}X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}({\mathrm{N}}_{Z}X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{D}}_{Z}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{N}}_{Z}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pt}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pt},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}}

where
i_{0}
is the zero section and
i_{1}
is the one section. It is a compactified version of the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-deformation to the normal cone.

Theorem 3.2.21
.

There are canonical equivalences in
\mathcal{C}

(3.2.31)

M(X/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}X,E))\xrightarrow{\simeq}M({\mathrm{D}}_{Z}X/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z\times\square}({\mathrm{D}}_{Z}X),E^{D}))\xleftarrow{\simeq}M({\mathrm{N}}_{Z}X/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}({\mathrm{N}}_{Z}X),E^{N})).

Here,
E
,
E_{D}
, and
E_{N}
denote the exceptional divisors on
{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}X
,

{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z\times\square}({\mathrm{D}}_{Z}X)
,
and
{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}({\mathrm{N}}_{Z}X)
,
respectively.

Proof.

As before,
use Proposition
3.1.4
to reduce to the case where
S\in\mathbf{Sch}_{noeth}
.
With this assumption,
this was proven in
[
22
, Theorem 7.5.4]
for
\mathcal{C}
stable.
Our generalization to the unstable case modifies three parts of the proof detailed below.

In Step 3 of the proof of
[
22
, Theorem 7.5.4]
,
we use Proposition
3.2.20
instead of
[
22
, Proposition 7.4.2]
.

In Step 5 of the proof of
[
22
, Theorem 7.5.4]
,
we constructed the fs log schemes
\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{i},\Sigma_{i}^{\prime})}
,
for
i=1,\ldots,7
.
and noted that the morphisms

\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{6},\Sigma_{6}^{\prime})}\to\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{5},\Sigma_{5}^{\prime})}\text{ and }\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{3},\Sigma_{3}^{\prime})}\to\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{2}^{\prime})}

are admissible blow-ups along smooth centers.
This directly implies that
[
22
, (7.5.4)]
is an equivalence in
\mathcal{C}

(without reference to
[
22
, Proposition C.1.7]
).

Again in Step 5,
to show that
[
22
, (7.5.3)]
is an equivalence in
\mathcal{C}
,
we consider the naturally induced commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{4},\Sigma_{4}^{\prime})}\cap\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{7},\Sigma_{7}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{4},\Sigma_{4}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{1}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{7},\Sigma_{7}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{6},\Sigma_{6}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{3},\Sigma_{3}^{\prime})}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(b)}

Both
(a)
and
(a)\cup(b)
are Zariski distinguished squares.
Apply
M(-)
to the diagram above and incorporate the pasting law to deduce that
[
22
, (7.5.3)]

is an equivalence in
\mathcal{C}
(without referring to
[
22
, Proposition C.1.7]
).
∎

As a consequence of Theorem
3.2.21
,
we obtain the Gysin equivalence

(3.2.32)

\mathfrak{p}_{X,Z}\colon M(X/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}X,E))\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{Th}({\mathrm{N}}_{Z}X)

in
\mathcal{C}
.
Moreover, we have the commutative diagram

(3.2.33)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z\times\square}({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}(X\times\square))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}(\mathbb{P}({\mathrm{N}}_{Z}X\oplus\mathcal{O}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}(X\times\square)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}({\mathrm{N}}_{Z}X\oplus\mathcal{O}).}

Combining Proposition
3.2.19
and (
3.2.31
) yields canonical equivalences in
\mathcal{C}

(3.2.34)

\begin{split}M(X/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}X,E))&\xrightarrow{\simeq}M({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}(X\times\square)/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z\times\square}({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}(X\times\square)),E^{D}))\\
&\xleftarrow{\simeq}M(\mathbb{P}({\mathrm{N}}_{Z}X\oplus\mathcal{O})/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}(\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{N}_{Z}X\oplus\mathcal{O})),E^{N})).\end{split}

Remark 3.2.22
.

The Gysin equivalence (
3.2.32
) provides a cofiber sequence in
\mathcal{C}

M({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}X,E)\to M(X)\to\mathrm{Th}({\mathrm{N}}_{Z}X)

that we call the
Gysin sequence
or the
residue sequence
for the pair
(X,Z)
. This is functorial in
(X,Z)
in the following sense:
suppose that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

is a cartesian square in
\mathbf{Sm}/S
.
Then there is a commutative diagram

(3.2.35)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z^{\prime}}X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z^{\prime}\times\square}({\mathrm{D}}_{Z^{\prime}}X^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z^{\prime}}({\mathrm{N}}_{Z^{\prime}}X^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z\times\square}({\mathrm{D}}_{Z}X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}({\mathrm{N}}_{Z}X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{D}}_{Z^{\prime}}X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{N}}_{Z^{\prime}}X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{D}}_{Z}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{N}}_{Z}X.}

For the above we obtain the naturally induced commutative diagram

(3.2.36)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(X/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}X,E))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Th}({\mathrm{N}}_{Z}X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(X^{\prime}/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z^{\prime}}X^{\prime},E^{\prime}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Th}({\mathrm{N}}_{Z^{\prime}}X^{\prime}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{p}_{X,Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{p}_{X^{\prime},Z^{\prime}}}

Lemma 3.2.23
.

Suppose
\mathcal{E}\to X
is a rank
n
vector bundle in
\mathbf{lSm}/S
, where
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
.
Let
Z
be the zero section of
\mathcal{E}
,
which is isomorphic to
X
.
With the identification
\mathcal{E}\cong{\mathrm{N}}_{Z}\mathcal{E}
, the two maps

(3.2.37)

M(\mathcal{E}/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}\mathcal{E},E))\rightrightarrows M({\mathrm{D}}_{Z}\mathcal{E}/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{X\times\square}({\mathrm{D}}_{Z}\mathcal{E}),E^{D}))

obtained by (
3.2.31
) are equivalent in
\mathcal{C}
, where
E
and
E^{D}
are the exceptional divisors.

Proof.

As before,
use Proposition
3.1.5
to reduce to the case when
S\in\mathbf{Sch}_{noeth}
.
With this assumption,
We set

X_{2}:=\mathcal{E}\times\square,\;X_{3}:=({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z\times\infty}X_{2},E_{3}),\;X_{1}:=X_{3}-{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z\times\infty}(\mathcal{E}\times\infty),

where
E_{3}
is the exceptional divisor.
Let
Z_{i}
be the closure of
\mathcal{E}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
in
X_{i}
for
i=1,2,3
.
We also set

X_{4}:=({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z_{1}}X_{1},E_{4}),\;X_{5}:=({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z_{2}}X_{2},E_{5}),\;X_{6}:=({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z_{3}}X_{3},E_{6}),

where
E_{4}
,
E_{5}
, and
E_{6}
are the corresponding exceptional divisors.

If
U=\operatorname{Spec}(A)
is an open affine subscheme of
\underline{X}
such that
\mathcal{E}
is trivial over
U
,
then
\underline{\mathcal{E}}|_{U}
is of the form
A[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
.
If
V=\operatorname{Spec}(B)
is an open affine subscheme of
U
,
then
\underline{\mathcal{E}}|_{V}\to\underline{\mathcal{E}}|_{U}
is associated with a homomorphism

(3.2.38)

B[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]\xrightarrow{\cdot M}A[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]

sending
x_{i}
to
\sum_{j=1}^{n}m_{ij}x_{j}
for some invertible matrix
M=(m_{ij})
.
The scheme
({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z\times 0}(\mathcal{E}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})-{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z\times 0}(\mathcal{E}\times 0))\times_{X}U

has a Zariski cover consisting of

A[x_{1}/t,\ldots,x_{n}/t,t]\text{ and }A[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},1/t].

On the other hand,

({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z\times\infty}(\mathcal{E}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})-{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z\times\infty}(\mathcal{E}\times\infty))\times_{X}U

has a Zariski cover consisting of

A[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},t]\text{ and }A[tx_{1},\ldots,tx_{n},1/t].

There are commutative diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},t]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B[x_{1}/t,\ldots,x_{n}/t,t]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},t]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[x_{1}/t,\ldots,x_{n}/t,t]}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B[tx_{1},\ldots,tx_{n},1/t]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},1/t]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[tx_{1},\ldots,tx_{n},1/t]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},1/t],}

where the horizontal homomorphisms send
x_{i}
to
x_{i}/t
for all
1\leq i\leq n
and
t
to
t
, and the vertical homomorphisms are naturally induced by (
3.2.38
).
Therefore, for all open affine subschemes
U
of
X
such that
\mathcal{E}
is trivial over
U
, we have naturally induced isomorphisms of fs log schemes

({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z\times 0}(\mathcal{E}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})-{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z\times 0}(\mathcal{E}\times 0))\times_{X}U\to({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z\times\infty}(\mathcal{E}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})-{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z\times\infty}(\mathcal{E}\times\infty))\times_{X}U.

We can glue these and impose log structures to have an isomorphism of fs log schemes

(3.2.39)

{\mathrm{D}}_{Z}\mathcal{E}\cong X_{1}.

This gives an isomorphism of fs log schemes

({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z\times\square}({\mathrm{D}}_{Z}\mathcal{E}),E^{D})\cong X_{4}.

Let us show that the naturally induced morphisms

(3.2.40)

M(X_{1}/X_{4})\to M(X_{3}/X_{6})\to M(X_{2}/X_{5})

are equivalences in
\mathcal{C}
.
This question is Zariski local on
X
, so we may assume that
\mathcal{E}
is trivial and
X=\operatorname{Spec}(A)
.
In this case,

\underline{X_{2}}
has a Zariski cover consisting of

A[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},t],\;A[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},1/t],

and
\underline{X_{3}}
has a Zariski cover consisting of

A[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},t],\;A[tx_{1},\ldots,tx_{n},1/t],\;A[x_{1}/x_{i},\ldots,x_{n}/x_{i},x_{i},1/(tx_{i})]\;(1\leq i\leq n).

The formula
x_{i}\mapsto x_{i}/t

(1\leq i\leq n)
and
t\mapsto t
identifies (
3.2.40
) with the maps in

[
22
, (7.5.3), (7.5.4)]
, which are equivalences in
\mathcal{C}
.
Hence, (
3.2.40
) are equivalences.

If the morphisms
\mathcal{E}/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}\mathcal{E},E)\to X_{1}/X_{4}
and

{\mathrm{N}}_{Z}(\mathcal{E})/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}({\mathrm{N}}_{Z}\mathcal{E}),E^{N})\to X_{1}/X_{4}
become the one section and zero section

\mathcal{E}/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}\mathcal{E},E)\rightrightarrows X_{2}/X_{5}
after composing with (
3.2.40
),
then we can finish the proof by
\square
-invariance.
To check this, we can work Zariski locally on
X
.
So we may assume that
\mathcal{E}
is trivial and
X=\operatorname{Spec}(A)
.
The morphisms
\mathcal{E}\rightrightarrows{\mathrm{D}}_{Z}\mathcal{E}
appearing in (
3.2.37
) can be written as the
homomorphisms

A[x_{1}/t,\ldots,x_{n}/t,t]\rightrightarrows A[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]

sending
x_{i}/t
to
x_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
and
t
to
0
for the first one and to
1
for the second one.
By (
3.2.39
), these can be written as the homomorphisms

A[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},t]\rightrightarrows A[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]

sending
x_{i}
to
x_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
and
t
to
0
for the first one and to
1
for the second one.
These correspond to the zero section and one section, respectively.
∎

Theorem 3.2.24
.

Suppose
S=\operatorname{Spec}(k)
,
where
k
is a perfect field that admits resolution of singularities.
If
f\colon Y\to X
is an admissible blow-up in
\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
,
then
M(f)
is an equivalence in
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

By resolution of singularities,
there exists an admissible blow-up
Y^{\prime}\to X
along a smooth center that factors through
f
.
As in
[
22
, Propositions 7.6.4, 7.6.6]
,
one shows that the classes of admissible blow-ups along smooth centers and
admissible blow-ups in
\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
satisfy a calculus of right fractions.
To finish the proof,
we appeal to
[
22
, Lemma C.2.1]
.
∎

3.3.
Properties of log motivic spectra

We now move to discuss some properties that hold in the stable setting.
Throughout this subsection we fix a symmetric monoidal functor

M\colon\mathscr{S}/S\to\mathcal{C},

where
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
,
\mathscr{S}
is either
\mathbf{lSm}
or
\mathbf{SmlSm}
, and
\mathcal{C}
is a symmetric monoidal
stable

\infty
-category.
We assume that
M
satisfies the following properties:

(1)

Zariski separation,

(2)

\square
-invariance,

(3)

sNis
-descent,

from §
3.2
, plus the following property:

(4)

div
-descent property.
If
Y\to X
is a dividing cover in
\mathscr{S}/S
, then the naturally induced map

M(Y)\to M(X)

is an equivalence in
\mathcal{C}
.

The following result crucially relates property (1)–(4) above with the results from the previous subsection.

Theorem 3.3.1
.

With the above assumptions, the functor
M
is invariant under admissible blow-ups along smooth centers.
Hence, the results in §
3.2
hold for
M
.

Proof.

The proof of
[
22
, Proposition 7.2.12]
applies to our setting.
∎

It is unknown whether Theorem
3.3.1
holds without assuming that
\mathcal{C}
is stable.
We give the following two fundamental examples of
M
.

Proposition 3.3.2
.

Suppose
\tau
is a topology in
\mathbf{lSch}
generated by a family of morphisms in
\mathbf{lSm}
, and
S
is a scheme.
The Zariski separation,
\square
-invariance,
sNis
-descent, and
div
-descent properties hold for

\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1},\tau}(S)
and
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{\tau}(S)
.

Proof.

For
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1},t}(S)
(resp.
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{t}(S)
),
let
M
be the functor from
\mathbf{lSm}/S
sending
X
to
\Sigma_{S^{1}}X_{+}
(resp.
\Sigma_{T}X_{+}
).
The Zariski separation property follows as in the proof of Proposition
3.2.4
.
The other properties hold trivially.
∎

Remark 3.3.3
.

(1) Let
S\in\mathbf{lSch}
, and let
p\colon S\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
be the structure map. Since
M(T)\in\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{\tau}^{\rm eff}(S,\Lambda)
is by definition the pullback
p^{*}M(\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})},0+\infty)\otimes\Lambda
for
M(\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})},0+\infty)\in\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{\tau}^{\rm eff}(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{\mathbb{Z}}))
, Theorem
3.3.1
implies that Proposition
3.2.15
applies to
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{\tau}^{\rm eff}(S,\Lambda)
as well, for an arbitrary
S
.

(2) By base-change, it follows immediately from Proposition
3.3.2
that the properties of Zariski separation,
\square
-invariance,
sNis
-descent, and
div
-descent hold for
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{\tau}^{\rm eff}(S,\Lambda)
and
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{\tau}(S,\Lambda)
for an arbitrary commutative connective ring spectrum
\Lambda
.

Remark 3.3.4
.

Suppose that
\tau
and
S
are as above,
As a consequence of Theorem
3.3.1
and Proposition
3.3.2
,
we see that
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{\tau}^{\rm eff}(S,\Lambda)
and
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{\tau}(S,\Lambda)
satisfy the results of §
3.2
for every commutative connected ring spectrum
\Lambda
.

Theorem 3.3.5
.

Suppose
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
and
Z\to X
is a closed immersion in
\mathbf{Sm}/S
.
Then the naturally induced square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(Z\times_{X}{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(X)}

is cocartesian in
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

As before,
use Proposition
3.1.4
to reduce to the case where
S\in\mathbf{Sch}_{noeth}
.
With this assumption,
this is
[
22
, Theorem 7.3.3]
.
The outline of the proof is as follows.
We first reduce to the case where
X=Z\times\mathbb{A}^{n}
for some integer
r\geq 0
using the deformation to the normal cone.
After compactification,
we work with the case where
X=Z\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
.
Then the morphism
M(Z)\to M(X)
is an equivalence in
\mathcal{C}
.
Since
{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}X
is a
\square
-bundle on
\mathbb{P}_{Z}^{n-1}
,
the morphism
M(Z\times_{X}{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}X)\to M({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}X)
is an equivalence in
\mathcal{C}
.
∎

We end this subsection by providing the
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
-invariance.

Theorem 3.3.6
.

For all
S\in\mathbf{lSch}
,

X\in\mathbf{lSm}/S
and integer
n\geq 1
,
the map

\Sigma_{S^{1}}^{\infty}(X\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1}))_{+}\to\Sigma_{S^{1}}^{\infty}X_{+}

naturally induced by the projection is an equivalence in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}(S)
.
The same holds in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(S)
.

Proof.

We focus on
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}(S)
since the proofs are similar.
If
S
is a scheme, this is done in Proposition
3.2.18
.
For general
S
, using the monoidal product, we reduce to the case when
X=S
.
Let
p\colon S\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
be the structure morphism.
The map

\Sigma_{S^{1}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})_{+}\to\Sigma_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\operatorname{pt}_{+}

is an equivalence in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}))
.
Applying
p^{*}
to this map finishes the proof.
∎

3.4.
A universal property of
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}

It is a well-known fact that the category of presheaves of sets on an ordinary category
\mathbf{C}
is the free cocompletion of
\mathbf{C}
. As such, it enjoys an obvious universal property. Passing from presheaves of sets to a homotopical context,
Lurie showed that for every
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
,
the Yoneda embedding

\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{S})

exhibits
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{S})

as a universal presentable
\infty
-category
built from
\mathcal{C}
(this is a rephrasing of a classical result of Dugger,
[
35
, Proposition 2.1]
).
Based on Lurie’s result,
Robalo
[
87
, Corollary 2.39]
proved that
\mathcal{SH}
enjoys an intuitive universal property among the stable presentable
\infty
-categories with a functor from
\mathbf{Sch}
.
We follow Robalo’s argument and deduce an analogous statement for
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}
. The proofs are formal, and we leave the details to the interested reader.

We fix a topology
\tau
on
\mathbf{lSch}
generated by a family of morphisms in
\mathbf{lSm}
.

Proposition 3.4.1
.

For every
S\in\mathbf{lSch}
and
\infty
-category
\mathcal{D}
with small colimits,
the Yoneda functor
\mathbf{lSm}/S\to\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\tau}(S)
naturally induces a fully faithful functor

\operatorname{Fun}^{\mathrm{L}}(\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\tau}(S),\mathcal{D})\to\operatorname{Fun}(\mathbf{lSm}/S,\mathcal{D}).

Here the left-hand side is the full subcategory of
\operatorname{Fun}(\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{t}(S),\mathcal{D})
spanned by functors preserving colimits.
Its essential image consists of functors satisfying
\square
-invariance and
\tau
-descent.

Proof.

As in
[
87
, Theorem 2.30]
,
this follows from the universal property of
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(-,\mathcal{S})

and the universal property of localizations given in
[
63
, Proposition 5.5.4.20]
.
∎

Equipping
\mathbf{lSm}/S
with the cartesian symmetric monoidal structure,
we have the naturally induced symmetric monoidal functor

(3.4.1)

\mathbf{lSm}/S^{\times}\to\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\tau}^{\otimes}(S).

where the target is equipped with the Day convolution product. We have the following monoidal enhancement of Proposition
3.4.1
.

Proposition 3.4.2
.

For every
S\in\mathbf{lSch}
and symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathcal{D}^{\otimes}
with small colimits,
the functor (
3.4.1
) naturally induces a fully faithful functor

\operatorname{Fun}^{\otimes,\mathrm{L}}(\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\tau}^{\otimes}(S),\mathcal{D}^{\otimes})\to\operatorname{Fun}^{\otimes}(\mathbf{lSm}/S^{\times},\mathcal{D}^{\otimes}).

Here the left-hand side is the full subcategory of
\operatorname{Fun}^{\otimes}(\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\tau}^{\otimes}(S),\mathcal{D}^{\otimes})

spanned by functors preserving colimits.
Its essential image consists of the functors satisfying
\square
-invariance and
\tau
-descent.

We can pass to the pointed versions. Recall that for any category
\mathcal{C}
there is a canonical equivalence of
\infty
-categories
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{S}\mathrm{pc}_{\ast})\simeq\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{S})_{\ast}
. As a consequence, we have a canonical equivalence of
\infty
-categories

(3.4.2)

\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\ast,t}(S)\simeq\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{t}(S)_{\ast}.

Let
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{L}}_{\ast}
be the full subcategory
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{L}}
spanned by pointed presentable
\infty
-categories
and the functors that preserve pointed objects.
As observed in the paragraph prior to
[
87
, Corollary 2.32]
,
the inclusion functor
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{L}}_{\ast})\to\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{L}})
admits a left adjoint

(3.4.3)

\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{L}})\to\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{L}}_{\ast})

sending
\mathcal{C}\in\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{L}}
to
\mathcal{C}_{\ast}
.

Proposition 3.4.3
.

For every
S\in\mathbf{lSch}
and pointed presentable symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathcal{D}^{\otimes}
,
there is a naturally induced fully faithful functor

\operatorname{Fun}^{\otimes,\mathrm{L}}(\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{\tau}^{\otimes}(S),\mathcal{D}^{\otimes})\to\operatorname{Fun}^{\otimes}(\mathbf{lSm}/S^{\times},\mathcal{D}^{\otimes}).

Its essential image consists of the functors
\mathcal{F}
satisfying
\square
-invariance and
\tau
-descent
such that the cofiber of
\mathcal{F}(1)\xrightarrow{\mathcal{F}(i_{1})}\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{P}^{1})
is an invertible object,
where
i_{1}
is the one section.

Proof.

Use (
3.4.2
) and (
3.4.3
) to have an equivalence of
\infty
-categories

\operatorname{Fun}^{\otimes,\mathrm{L}}(\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\ast,\tau}^{\otimes}(S),\mathcal{D}^{\otimes})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\operatorname{Fun}^{\otimes,\mathrm{L}}(\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\tau}^{\otimes}(S),\mathcal{D}^{\otimes}).

Due to
[
87
, Proposition 2.9]
,
there is a fully faithful functor of
\infty
-categories

\operatorname{Fun}^{\otimes,\mathrm{L}}(\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{\tau}^{\otimes}(S),\mathcal{D}^{\otimes})\to\operatorname{Fun}^{\otimes,\mathrm{L}}(\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\ast,\tau}^{\otimes}(S),\mathcal{D}^{\otimes})

whose essential image consists of the functors mapping
\mathbb{P}^{1}/1
to an invertible object.
Combined with Proposition
3.4.2
, we conclude.
∎

3.5.
Various models for
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}

Let
S
be a scheme equipped with a trivial log structure.
The standard construction of
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}^{\rm eff}(S,\Lambda)
and
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(S,\Lambda)
is obtained by considering the category of dividing Nisnevich (or étale) sheaves on
\mathbf{lSm}/S
, localized to
\square
-local equivalences (followed by Tate stabilization in the non-effective case). This model has a major disadvantage in applications: any cohomology theory representable in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(S,\Lambda)
has, by design, dividing descent. This condition is not easy to verify in practice: concretely, it boils down to requiring invariance under admissible blow-ups.

As we have recalled in Theorem
3.3.6
, dividing descent and
\square
-invariance together imply
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
-invariance. It turns out that, after restriction to the subcategory
\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
, the converse is true:
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
-invariance and
strict
Nisnevich descent imply dividing descent. This provides an alternative, but equivalent, motivic category, that is much easier to use in applications. This is one of the key results in
[
22
]
for
\mathbf{logDM}(k)
and
\mathbf{logDA}^{\rm eff}(S)
. In what follows, we explain the modifications necessary to adapt the arguments to our more general framework.

Notation 3.5.1
.

Throughout this subsection,
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
is a scheme with trivial log structure. We also fix a connective commutative ring spectrum
\Lambda
. We sometimes use an abbreviated notation
\mathbb{P}^{1}
for
\mathbb{P}^{1}/1
.

For every
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
,
the inclusion of categories
\iota\colon\mathbf{SmlSm}/S\to\mathbf{lSm}/S
naturally induces adjunctions

(3.5.1)

\iota_{\sharp}:\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{SmlSm}/S)\rightleftarrows\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{lSm}/S):\iota^{*}.

Lemma 3.5.2
.

For every
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
the functor
\iota_{\sharp}
naturally induces an equivalence of
\infty
-categories

\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{dNis}^{(\wedge)}(\mathbf{SmlSm}/S)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{dNis}^{(\wedge)}(\mathbf{lSm}/S).

The same is true for the
\infty
-categories of (hyper)sheaves of
\Lambda
-modules.

Proof.

The question is Zariski local on
S
. Hence we can assume that
S=\operatorname{Spec}(A)
is affine, and that
S\in\mathbf{Sch}_{noeth}
.
Suppose
\{U_{i}\to X\}_{i\in I}
is a dividing Nisnevich cover.
It has a refinement
\{V_{i}\to X\}_{i\in J}
such that each
V_{i}
has a chart
\mathbb{A}_{P_{i}}
.
There is a dividing cover of
\mathbb{A}_{P_{i}}
that belongs to
\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
by toric resolution of singularities

[
31
, Theorem 11.1.9]
.
Hence
\{U_{i}\to X\}_{i\in I}
has a refinement
\{W_{i}\to X\}_{i\in K}
such that
W_{i}
belongs to
\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
.
By the implication (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) in
[
4
, Théorème III.4.1]
, we conclude in the case of sheaves of sets. For sheaves of spaces or, more generally, for (hyper)sheaves of
\Lambda
-modules, we can use
[
11
, Lemma 2.1.4]
.
∎

An immediate consequence of Lemma
3.5.2
is the following result.

Proposition 3.5.3
.

For every
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
, let
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{dNis,\square}(\mathbf{SmlSm}/S,\Lambda)
be the full
\infty
-subcategory of
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{dNis}(\mathbf{SmlSm}/S,\Lambda)
spanned by the objects
M
that are local with respect to the maps
\Lambda(X\times\square)\to\Lambda(X)
for all
X\in\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
.
Then there is an equivalence of monoidal stable
\infty
-categories

\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{dNis,\square}(\mathbf{SmlSm}/S,\Lambda)^{\otimes}\to\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}^{\rm eff}_{dNis}(S,\Lambda)^{\otimes}

Corollary 3.5.4
.

There are equivalences of
\mathbf{Sm}
-premotivic
\infty
-categories

\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{dNis}^{\rm eff}(-,\Lambda)^{\otimes}\simeq\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(-,\Lambda)^{\otimes}\text{ and }\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(-,\Lambda)^{\otimes}\simeq\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(-,\Lambda)^{\otimes}

Proof.

By base change we can assume
\Lambda=\mathbb{S}
, and we can prove the equivalence for the underlying stable
\infty
-categories (forgetting the monoidal structure). By definition we have a canonical natural transformation of premotivic
\infty
-categories

\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{dNis,\square}(\mathbf{SmlSm}/-,\mathcal{S}\mathrm{pt})\to\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(-),

which is, for every
S
, a localization with respect to the maps
f\in\mathbf{SmAdm}/S
.
By Proposition
3.5.3
, the left-hand side is equivalent to
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1},dNis}(S)
, and by Theorem
3.3.1
, every map in
\mathbf{SmAdm}/S
is inverted. The claim follows.
∎

Definition 3.5.5
.

Let
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
and let
\mathscr{S}/S
be either
\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
or
\mathbf{lSm}/S
. Write

\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{dNis,(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})}(\mathscr{S}/S)_{(*)}\subset\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{dNis}(\mathscr{S}/S)_{(*)}

for the full
\infty
-subcategory of (pointed)
dNis
-sheaves on
\mathscr{S}/S
spanned by the objects
M
that are local with respect to the maps
L_{dNis}(X\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1}))_{(+)}\to L_{dNis}(X)_{(+)}
, for all
X\in\mathscr{S}/S
and every
n\geq 1
. A similar definition holds for the
\Lambda
-linear version.
As before, this construction underlies a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category.

There is clearly a localization functor

\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{dNis,\square}(\mathscr{S}/S)_{(*)}\to\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{dNis,(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})}(\mathscr{S}/S)_{(*)}

Theorem 3.5.6
.

For every
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
, there are equivalences of monoidal stable
\infty
-categories

\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{dNis,\square}(\mathbf{SmlSm}/S,\Lambda)^{\otimes}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{dNis,(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})}(\mathbf{SmlSm}/S,\Lambda)^{\otimes}

\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{dNis,\square}(\mathbf{lSm}/S,\Lambda)^{\otimes}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{dNis,(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})}(\mathbf{lSm}/S,\Lambda)^{\otimes}

Proof.

We consider first the case of
\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
and assume again
\Lambda=\mathbb{S}
. We drop the
dNis
sheafification functor to simplify the notation. As above, it is enough to show the equivalence for the underlying
\infty
-categories. Since
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{dNis,(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})}(\mathbf{SmlSm}/S,\mathcal{S}\mathrm{pt})
is the localization of
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{dNis,\square}(\mathbf{SmlSm}/S,\mathcal{S}\mathrm{pt})
with respect to the projections

p\colon\Sigma_{S^{1}}^{\infty}((\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})\times X)_{+}\to\Sigma_{S^{1}}^{\infty}X_{+}

for all
X\in\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
and integers
n\geq 1
, it suffices to show that
p
is already an equivalence. This is Proposition
3.2.18
.
For the case of
\mathbf{lSm}/S
,
use Proposition
3.1.6
(2) for the noetherian reduction, and apply again
[
22
, Proposition 7.3.1]
(this holds for
X\in\mathbf{lSm}/S
, not just for
\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
, if
dNis
-descent holds for the functor
M(-)
).
∎

The following theorem is crucial for us.

Theorem 3.5.7
.

For every
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
there is an equivalence of monoidal stable
\infty
-categories

\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{sNis,(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})}(\mathbf{SmlSm}/S,\Lambda)^{\otimes}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{dNis,(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})}(\mathbf{SmlSm}/S,\Lambda)^{\otimes}.

Proof.

As before, assume
\Lambda=\mathbb{S}
. According to Remark
2.4.7
, it is enough to show that every morphism of the form

\Sigma_{S^{1}}^{\infty}Y_{+}\to\Sigma_{S^{1}}^{\infty}X_{+}

for all dividing covers
Y\to X
is an equivalence in
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{sNis,(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})}(\mathbf{SmlSm}/S,\mathcal{S}\mathrm{pt})
.

This question is Zariski local on
S
.
Hence we may assume that
S
is affine.
By Proposition
3.1.6
(4),
we reduce to the case when
S\in\mathbf{Sch}_{noeth}
.
This is done in
[
22
, Theorem 7.7.4]
.
∎

Corollary 3.5.8
.

Let
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
. Then for every
\Lambda
there are equivalences of monoidal stable
\infty
-categories

\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{dNis}^{\rm eff}(S,\Lambda)^{\otimes}\simeq\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{sNis,(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})}(\mathbf{SmlSm}/S,\Lambda)^{\otimes}

\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{dNis}(S,\Lambda)^{\otimes}\simeq\mathcal{S}\mathrm{pt}^{\Sigma}_{T}(\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{sNis,(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})}(\mathbf{SmlSm}/S,\Lambda))^{\otimes}.

Remark 3.5.9
.

Proposition
3.5.3
and Theorems
3.5.6
and
3.5.7
hold for
T
-spectra and
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-spectra too. Note that the arguments work
verbatim
if the strict Nisnevich topology is replaced by the strict étale topology, and the dividing Nisnevich topology is replaced by the dividing étale topology.

In summary,
when
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
,
we have the following models for
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}^{\rm eff}(S,\Lambda)
and
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(S,\Lambda)
:

Category

Topology

Localization

Model 1

\mathbf{lSm}/S

dNis

(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})

Model 2

\mathbf{lSm}/S

dNis

\square

Model 3

\mathbf{SmlSm}/S

dNis

\square

Model 4

\mathbf{SmlSm}/S

dNis

(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})

Model 5

\mathbf{SmlSm}/S

sNis

(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})

4.
Motivic versus logarithmic motivic theories

We begin by fixing some notation and introducing the functors relating the categories of log schemes with the ordinary categories of schemes.

Definition 4.0.1
.

Suppose
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
.
We define functors

\rho\colon\mathbf{SmlSm}/S\to\mathbf{Sm}/S,\;\lambda\colon\mathbf{Sm}/S\to\mathbf{SmlSm}/S,\;\mathbf{lSm}/S

and

\omega\colon\mathbf{SmlSm}/S,\;\mathbf{lSm}/S\to\mathbf{Sm}/S

as follows:

\rho(X):=\underline{X}
,
\lambda(X):=X
, and
\omega(X):=X-\partial X
.
One can readily check that

(4.0.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{SmlSm}/S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Sm}/S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}

and

(4.0.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{lSm}/S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Sm}/S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}

are adjoint functors.
Our convention for such diagrams is that
\rho
is left adjoint to
\lambda
, and
\lambda
is left adjoint to
\omega
.

The diagrams (
4.0.1
) and (
4.0.2
) naturally induces sequences of adjunctions of presheaves of (pointed) spaces

(4.0.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{SmlSm}/S)_{(*)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{Sm}/S)_{(*)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{*}\cong\lambda_{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{*}\cong\lambda^{*}\cong\omega_{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{*}\cong\omega^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{*}}

and

(4.0.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{lSm}/S)_{(*)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{Sm}/S)_{(*)}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{*}\cong\lambda_{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{*}\cong\lambda^{*}\cong\omega_{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{*}\cong\omega^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{*}}

We now recall the construction of the motivic homotopy categories due to Morel and Voevodsky
[
70
]
in our context.

Definition 4.0.2
.

Suppose
\tau
is a topology on
\mathbf{Sch}
generated by a family of morphisms in
\mathbf{Sm}
.
Let

\mathcal{H}_{(\ast),\tau}\colon\mathbf{Sch}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{L}})

be the
\mathbf{Sm}
-premotivic
\infty
-categories associated with
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{\tau,\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathbf{Sm}/-)_{(\ast)}
.

Let

\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1},\tau},\mathcal{SH}_{\tau}\colon\mathbf{Sch}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{L}})

be the
S^{1}
(resp.
T
) stabilization. We often omit
\tau
in this notation when
\tau=Nis
.

By e.g., Proposition
2.2.8
we have equivalences of
\infty
-categories

(4.0.5)

\mathcal{SH}(S)\simeq\operatorname{colim}_{\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{L}}}(\mathcal{H}_{\ast}(S)\xrightarrow{\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}}\mathcal{H}_{\ast}(S)\xrightarrow{\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}}\cdots)

and

(4.0.6)

\mathcal{SH}(S)\simeq\mathop{\rm lim}(\cdots\xrightarrow{\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}}\mathcal{H}_{\ast}(S)\xrightarrow{\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}}\mathcal{H}_{\ast}(S)).

The idea of contracting the projective line
\mathbb{P}^{1}
instead of the affine line
\mathbb{A}^{1}
was considered in
[
10
]
.
It is not clear how to compare this theory directly with our construction, but we offer a variant of Ayoub’s construction, in which we further localize with respect to all projections
X\times\mathbb{P}^{n}\to X
for all
X\in\mathbf{Sm}/S
and integers
n\geq 1
. We refer to this localization as the
\mathbb{P}^{\bullet}
-localization
. The definition of the
\mathbb{P}^{\bullet}
-localized motivic homotopy category is completely straightforward.

Definition 4.0.3
.

Let

\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{P}^{\bullet}}_{(\ast)}\colon\mathbf{Sch}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{L}})

be the
\mathbf{Sm}
-premotivic
\infty
-category associated with
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{Nis,\mathbb{P}^{\bullet}}(\mathbf{Sm}/-)_{(\ast)}
. Let

\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}^{\mathbb{P}^{\bullet}}\colon\mathbf{Sch}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{L}})

be its
S^{1}
-stabilization. We can similarly define
\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{P}^{1}}
,
\mathcal{H}_{\ast}^{\mathbb{P}^{1}}
, and
\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}^{\mathbb{P}^{1}}
.

Question 4.0.4
.

Is there an equivalence of
\mathbf{Sm}
-premotivic
\infty
-categories

\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}^{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\simeq\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}^{\mathbb{P}^{\bullet}}?

Example 4.0.5
.

Let
X
be a scheme.
If
\mathcal{F}
be a quasi-coherent sheaf on
X
, we regard
\mathcal{F}
as a Nisnevich sheaf on
\mathbf{Sm}/X
associated with the presheaf given by

\underline{\mathcal{F}}(Y):=\Gamma(Y,(Y\to X)^{*}\mathcal{F}).

If
X=\operatorname{Spec}(A)
is affine and
\mathcal{F}
corresponds to an
A
-module
M
, then
R\Gamma(Y,\underline{\mathcal{F}})=R\Gamma(Y,\mathcal{O}_{Y})\otimes_{A}M
;
see
[
10
, §3]
.
The sheaf
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
is
\mathbb{P}^{n}
-local for every
n\geq 1

by the argument for
[
10
, Proposition 3.3]
.

Construction 4.0.6
.

The functor
\rho_{\sharp}
maps
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
to
\mathbb{P}^{n}
for every integer
n\geq 1
and every strict Nisnevich distinguished square to a Nisnevich distinguished square. By Corollary
3.5.8
, we obtain a functor of
\mathbf{Sm}
-premotivic
\infty
-categories

\rho_{\sharp}\colon\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}\to\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}^{\mathbb{P}^{\bullet}}.

We denote its right adjoint by
\rho^{*}
.

By using the functor
\rho_{\sharp}
, various properties of motives in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}
can be translated into properties of motives in
\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}^{\mathbb{P}^{\bullet}}
.
For example, we have the following result.

Proposition 4.0.7
.

Suppose
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
and
i\colon Z\to X
is a closed immersion in
\mathbf{Sm}/S
.
Then the projection
p\colon{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}X\to X
is an equivalence in
\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}^{\mathbb{P}^{\bullet}}(S)
.

Proof.

We may assume that
i
has codimension
d
.
Theorem
3.3.5
gives a cocartesian square

(4.0.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}({\mathrm{N}}_{Z}X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}(S)
.
Apply
\rho_{\sharp}
to (
4.0.7
) to see that (
4.0.7
) is a cocartesian square in
\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}^{\mathbb{P}^{\bullet}}(S)
too.
Hence it remains to check that the projection

\mathbb{P}({\mathrm{N}}_{Z}X)\to Z

is an equivalence in
\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}^{\mathbb{P}^{\bullet}}(S)
.

By induction on the number of open subsets in a trivialization of the projective bundle
\mathbb{P}({\mathrm{N}}_{Z}X)
, we reduce to showing that the projection

Z\times\mathbb{P}^{n}\to Z

is an equivalence in
\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}^{\mathbb{P}^{\bullet}}(S)
for every integer
n\geq 1
.
This follows from the definition.
∎

Construction 4.0.8
.

The functor
\omega_{\sharp}
maps
\square
to
\mathbb{A}^{1}
,
\mathbb{P}^{1}
to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and every strict Nisnevich distinguished square to a Nisnevich distinguished square.
This naturally induces a functor of
\mathbf{Sm}
-premotivic
\infty
-categories

\omega_{\sharp}\colon\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}\to\mathcal{SH}.

We similarly have functors of
\mathbf{Sm}
-premotivic
\infty
-categories

\omega_{\sharp}\colon\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H},\text{ and }\omega_{\sharp}\colon\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\ast}\to\mathcal{H}_{\ast},

and a functor of
\mathbf{Sm}
-premotivic
\infty
-categories

\omega_{\sharp}\colon\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}\to\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}.

Their right adjoints, denoted by
\omega^{*}
,
are both fully faithful by
[
84
, Proposition 2.5.7]
.
This means there is an embedding of motivic homotopy theories:

(\mathbb{A}^{1}\text{-local homotopy theory})\subset(\square\text{-local homotopy theory})

Remark 4.0.9
.

For the convenience of the reader,
we outline the proof for
\omega^{*}\colon\mathcal{H}(S)\to\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}(S)
.
To begin with,
the left adjoint functor
\omega_{\sharp}\colon\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}(S)\to\mathcal{H}(S)
admits a factorization

\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}(S)\xrightarrow{L_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}}(\mathbb{A}^{1})^{-1}\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}(S)\xrightarrow{\omega_{\sharp}}\mathcal{H}(S),

where
L_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
denotes the localization functor.
Let us explain why the latter
\omega_{\sharp}
is an equivalence of
\infty
-categories,
which shows the claim since a right adjoint of
L_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
is fully faithful.
There is a functor

\lambda_{\sharp}\colon\mathcal{H}(S)\to(\mathbb{A}^{1})^{-1}\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}(S)

sending
X\in\mathbf{Sm}/S
to
\underline{X}
.
We have an isomorphism of schemes
\omega\lambda(X)\cong X
,
and this implies
\omega_{\sharp}\lambda_{\sharp}\simeq\operatorname{id}
.
On the other hand,
we have an isomorphism of schemes
\lambda\omega(Y)\cong Y-\partial Y
for all
Y\in\mathbf{lSm}/S
.
One can show
Y-\partial Y\simeq Y
in
(\mathbb{A}^{1})^{-1}\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}(S)
as in
[
84
, Proposition 2.4.3]
,
and this implies
\lambda_{\sharp}\omega_{\sharp}\simeq\operatorname{id}
.
Hence
\omega_{\sharp}
is an equivalence of
\infty
-categories.

Remark 4.0.10
.

If
S\in\mathbf{lSch}
has a nontrivial log structure and
f\colon X\to S
is a log smooth morphism of fs log schemes,
then taking the complement
X-\partial X
is a drastic operation since it removes everything over the boundary

\partial S
.
Instead,
we can remove the “vertical boundary”
\partial_{S}X
,
which is defined to be the set of points
x\in X
such that
\mathcal{M}_{S,f(X)}\to\mathcal{M}_{X,x}
is not vertical
in the sense of
[
78
, Definition I.4.3.1]
.

However,
there is a technical difficulty of removing vertical boundaries because there is no functor

\omega\colon\mathbf{lSm}/S\to\mathbf{lSm}/S
sending
X\in\mathbf{lSm}/S
to
X-\partial_{S}X

(unless
S
has trivial log structure).
For example,
consider the morphisms

Z:=\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{N}}\times\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{N}}\xrightarrow{h}Y:=\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{N}}\times\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{N}}\xrightarrow{g}X:=\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{N}}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\xrightarrow{f}S:=\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{N}},

where
f
is the first projection,

g
removes the log structure on the second factor,
and
h
is naturally induced by the homomorphism
\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}^{2}
sending
(x,y)
to
(x,x+y)
.
Then we have isomorphisms of fs log schemes
X-\partial_{S}X\cong X
and

Y-\partial_{S}Y\cong\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{N}}\times{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
.
Since the diagonal homomorphism
\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}^{2}
is vertical,
we also have an isomorphism
Z-\partial_{S}Z\cong Z
.
Taking the existence of
\omega
for granted,
then
f
agrees with
\omega(Z)\to\omega(X)
up to isomorphism.
However, unlike
\omega(Z)\to\omega(X)
, the map
f
does not factor through
w(Y)
.

Construction 4.0.11
.

For
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
,
let
\omega\colon\mathbf{cSm}/S\to\mathbf{Sm}/S
be the functor sending
X\in\mathbf{cSm}/S
to
X-\partial X
.
As in Construction
4.0.8
,
we have functors of
\mathbf{Sm}
-premotivic
\infty
-categories

\omega_{\sharp}\colon\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{Adm}}\to\mathcal{H},\text{ and }\omega_{\sharp}\colon\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\ast}^{\mathbf{Adm}}\to\mathcal{H}_{\ast},

and functors of
\mathbf{Sm}
-premotivic
\infty
-categories

\omega_{\sharp}\colon\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}^{\mathbf{Adm}}\to\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}},\text{ and }\omega_{\sharp}\colon\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}^{\mathbf{Adm}}\to\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}.

Let
\omega^{*}
denote the corresponding right adjoints.

Construction 4.0.12
.

For
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
,
let
\psi\colon\mathbf{SmlSm}/S\to\mathbf{cSm}/S
be the inclusion functor.
This maps
\square
to
\square
,
\mathbb{P}^{1}
to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, every strict Nisnevich cover to strict Nisnevich cover,
and every admissible blow-up along a smooth center to an admissible blow-up.

Using Corollary
3.5.4

we have a functor of
\mathbf{Sm}
-premotivic
\infty
-categories

\psi_{\sharp}\colon\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}\to\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}^{\mathbf{Adm}}

We similarly have a functor of
\mathbf{Sm}
-premotivic
\infty
-categories

\psi_{\sharp}\colon\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}\to\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}^{\mathbf{Adm}}.

The corresponding right adjoints are denoted by
\psi^{*}
.

Observe that there is a commutative triangle

(4.0.8)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}^{\mathbf{Adm}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{SH}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{\sharp}}

We also have similar commutative triangles for
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}
.

Proposition 4.0.13
.

Suppose
k
is a perfect field admitting resolution of singularities.
Then the functors

\psi_{\sharp}\colon\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}(k)\to\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(k)\text{ and }\psi_{\sharp}\colon\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(k)\to\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(k)

are equivalences. In particular, every map in
\mathbf{Adm}
is invertible in
\mathbf{logSH}(k)
.

Proof.

We define the functor

\alpha\colon\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}(k)\to\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{cSm}/k,\mathcal{S}\mathrm{pt})

by setting

\alpha(\mathcal{F})(X):=\mathop{\rm colim}_{Y\in(\mathbf{Adm}\downarrow X)\cap\mathbf{lSm}/k}\mathcal{F}(Y)

for all
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}(k)
and
X\in\mathbf{cSm}/k
.
Here,
the colimit runs over the category
(\mathbf{Adm}\downarrow X)\cap\mathbf{lSm}/k
,
whose objects are admissible blow-ups
Y\to X
with
Y\in\mathbf{lSm}/k
and whose morphisms are
admissible blow-ups
Z\to Y
over
X
.

Suppose
Y,Y^{\prime}\in(\mathbf{Adm}\downarrow X)\cap\mathbf{lSm}/k
.
By resolution of singularities,
there exists a blow-up
\underline{Y^{\prime\prime}}\to\underline{Y}\times_{\underline{X}}\underline{X^{\prime}}
such that
\underline{Y^{\prime\prime}}\in\mathbf{Sm}/k
and the preimage
Y^{\prime\prime}-\partial Y^{\prime\prime}
of
X-\partial X
in
\underline{Y^{\prime\prime}}
is the complement of a strict normal crossing divisor on
\underline{Y^{\prime\prime}}
.
We set
Y^{\prime\prime}:=(\underline{Y^{\prime\prime}},Y^{\prime\prime}-\partial Y^{\prime\prime})
,
which is an object of
\mathbf{SmlSm}/k
.
Then the naturally induced morphisms
Y^{\prime\prime}\to Y,Y^{\prime}
are admissible blow-ups,
so the category
(\mathbf{Adm}\downarrow X)\cap\mathbf{lSm}/k
is connected.

Suppose
f,g\colon Y^{\prime}\rightrightarrows Y
