are two morphisms in
(\mathbf{Adm}\downarrow X)\cap\mathbf{lSm}/k
.
Since
f
and
g
agree on the dense open subset
Y^{\prime}-\partial Y^{\prime}
on
Y^{\prime}
,
we have
f=g
.
Hence the category
(\mathbf{Adm}\downarrow X)\cap\mathbf{lSm}/k
is filtered.
Together with Theorem
3.2.24
,
we have
\alpha(\mathcal{F})(X)\simeq\mathcal{F}(Y)
whenever
Y\in(\mathbf{Adm}\downarrow X)\cap\mathbf{lSm}/k
.

If
X\in\mathbf{cSm}/k
,
then there exists an object
Y
of
(\mathbf{Adm}\downarrow X)\cap\mathbf{lSm}/k
by resolution of singularities.
Since
Y\times\square\in(\mathbf{Adm}\downarrow X\times\square)\cap\mathbf{lSm}/k
,
we have equivalences of spectra

\alpha(\mathcal{F})(X)\simeq\mathcal{F}(Y)\simeq\mathcal{F}(Y\times\square)\simeq\alpha(\mathcal{F})(X\times\square).

Hence
\alpha(\mathcal{F})
is
\square
-invariant,
and we obtain the factorization

\alpha\colon\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}(k)\to\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(k).

Conversely,
we have the functor

\psi^{*}\colon\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(k)\to\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}(k)

given by

\psi^{*}(\mathcal{G})(Y):=\mathcal{G}(Y)

for all
\mathcal{G}\in\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(k)
and
Y\in\mathbf{lSm}/k
.
There is a natural equivalence
\alpha\psi^{*}\simeq\operatorname{id}
.
Due to Theorem
3.2.24
,
we also have a natural equivalence
\psi^{*}\alpha\simeq\operatorname{id}
.
Hence
\psi^{*}
is an equivalence of
\infty
-categories.
This implies that
\psi_{\sharp}
is an equivalence of
\infty
-categories too.

The claim about
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}
holds true by passing to the
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}
-stabilization.
∎

Remark 4.0.14
.

Suppose
k
is a perfect field admitting resolution of singularities.
As above,
one can similarly show that there are equivalences of symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories

\psi_{\sharp}\colon\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(k)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(k)\text{ and }\psi_{\sharp}\colon\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\ast}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(k)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\ast}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(k).

Construction 4.0.15
.

The functor
\lambda
maps
\mathbb{P}^{1}
to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
and every Nisnevich distinguished square to a strict
Nisnevich distinguished square. We obtain functors of
\mathbf{Sm}
-premotivic
\infty
-categories:

\lambda_{\sharp}\colon\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{Nis}(\mathbf{Sm}/-)\to\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}

\lambda_{\sharp}\colon\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{Nis}(\mathbf{Sm}/-,\mathcal{S}\mathrm{pt})\to\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}.

We denote the right adjoints by
\lambda^{*}
.

Question 4.0.16
.

Is the functor

\lambda^{*}\colon\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}(S)\to\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{Nis}(\mathbf{Sm}/S,\mathcal{S}\mathrm{pt})

fully faithful for
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
?

Remark 4.0.17
.

We can regard the essential image of
\lambda^{*}
as a
homotopy theory of schemes without
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance
.
We find it an appealing question to describe the said image using exclusively the language of schemes with no mention of logarithmic structures. An analogous (but less structured) problem was considered in
[
16
, 3.3.5]
.

Construction 4.0.18
.

Suppose
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
.
Let us compare
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(S)
with the
\infty
-category
\mathbf{MS}_{S}
(following the notation in
[
1
, §4]
) of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-spectra due to Annala-Iwasa
[
2
]
.
Consider the full subcategory of
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{Sm}/S,\mathcal{S}\mathrm{pt})
spanned by the presheaves satisfying Zariski descent and elementary blow-up excision,
see
[
1
, Definition 2.1(i)]
for the latter notion.
Then
\mathbf{MS}_{S}
is defined to be the
\infty
-category of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-spectra in this
\infty
-category.

The functor
\lambda_{\sharp}\colon\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{Sm}/S,\mathcal{S}\mathrm{pt})\to\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{lSm}/S,\mathcal{S}\mathrm{pt})
maps
\mathbb{P}^{1}
to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
and every Zariski distinguished square to a strict Nisnevich distinguished square and every elementary blow-up square to an elementary blow-up square.
Furthermore, an elementary blow-up square is cocartesian in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
and hence in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(S)
by Proposition
3.2.20
.
It follows that we have the induced (monoidal) functor of
\mathbf{Sm}
-premotivic
\infty
-categories

\lambda_{\sharp}\colon\mathbf{MS}^{\otimes}\to\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}^{\otimes}.

We denote its right adjoint by
\lambda^{*}
. We expect this functor to be fully faithful for
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
.

Remark 4.0.19
.

Let us further elaborate on Remark
4.0.17
.
The construction of
\mathbf{MS}^{\otimes}_{S}
is inherently
stable
(since it is built as the category of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-spectra). Our approach allows us to consider an
unstable
motivic homotopy theory without
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance, given by the essential image of
\lambda^{*}\colon\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}(S)\to\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{Nis}(\mathbf{Sm}/S,\mathcal{S})
for any
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
, or alternatively by the essential image of

\lambda^{*}\colon\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)\to\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{Nis}(\mathbf{Sm}/S).

Let us provisionally denote the latter category by
\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
.
It is an interesting question to study explicitly
\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
, without referring to log structures. We make a couple of immediate remarks. First, every elementary blow-up square is cocartesian in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
, thus the same is true in
\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
; that is,
\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
is a subcategory of the category of
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{Nis}(\mathbf{Sm}/S,\mathcal{S})
spanned by those sheaves
\mathcal{F}
satisfying elementary blow-up excision.
Second, the Gysin sequence in Remark
3.2.22
suggests that the objects of
\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
are local with respect to the inclusions
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\to\mathbb{P}^{n}
.
We leave the study of this category for future work.

5.
Logarithmic Picard groups and
\mathrm{B}\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}

The goal of this section is to discuss the cohomology of different sheaves of
units
associated to a log scheme, namely
\mathcal{M}^{{\mathrm{gp}}}
and
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}
. Our main result is Theorem
5.2.10
, proving that for every
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
there is an equivalence
\mathrm{B}\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}\simeq\mathbb{P}^{\infty}
in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
(and therefore in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(S,\Lambda)
for
S\in\mathbf{lSch}
thanks to Corollary
3.5.4
).

5.1.
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
-invariance of logarithmic Picard groups

Let us start with a definition.

Definition 5.1.1
.

If
X
is a log scheme,
we define the
logarithmic Picard group

as

(5.1.1)

\operatorname{logPic}(X):=H_{sNis}^{1}(X,\mathcal{M}^{{\mathrm{gp}}}).

If
X
is a scheme, we observe that there is an isomorphism of abelian groups
\operatorname{logPic}(X)\cong\operatorname{Pic}(X)
with the Picard group.

Remark 5.1.2
.

Our
\operatorname{logPic}(X)
is different from Kato’s original definition in
[
49
]
as

H_{{s\acute{e}t}}^{1}(X,\mathcal{M}^{{\mathrm{gp}}}),

which we denote by
\operatorname{logPic}_{{s\acute{e}t}}(X)
.
It is natural to ask whether the canonical homomorphism of abelian groups

(5.1.2)

\operatorname{logPic}(X)\to\operatorname{logPic}_{{s\acute{e}t}}(X)

is an isomorphism or not.
This question is a log version of Hilbert’s theorem 90.

In this subsection we study the
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
-invariance of
\mathcal{M}^{{\mathrm{gp}}}
,
\operatorname{logPic}
, and
\operatorname{logPic}_{{s\acute{e}t}}
,
i.e., whether the naturally induced homomorphisms of abelian groups

(5.1.3)

\begin{split}\mathcal{M}^{{\mathrm{gp}}}(X)\to\mathcal{M}^{{\mathrm{gp}}}(X\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1}))&,\;\operatorname{logPic}(X)\to\operatorname{logPic}(X\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})),\\
\operatorname{logPic}_{{s\acute{e}t}}(X)\to&\operatorname{logPic}_{{s\acute{e}t}}(X\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1}))\end{split}

are isomorphisms or not for, say, fs log schemes
X
.

We will show that the homomorphisms in (
5.1.3
) are isomorphisms in the following two cases:

(1)

S
is a geometrically unibranch scheme, and
X\in\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
.

(2)

X
is a saturated log scheme such that
\underline{X}
is the spectrum of a field.

Proposition 5.1.3
.

Let
X
be a locally noetherian connected scheme.
Then there is an isomorphism of abelian groups

\operatorname{Pic}(X)\times\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{\cong}\operatorname{Pic}(X\times\mathbb{P}^{n})

sending
(\mathcal{L},d)\in\operatorname{Pic}(X)\times\mathbb{Z}
to
q^{*}\mathcal{L}(d)
, where
q\colon X\times\mathbb{P}^{n}\to X
is the projection.

Proof.

We refer to
[
34
, Remarque II.4.2.7]
and
[
71
, Proposition 3, Lecture 13]
.
∎

Proposition 5.1.4
.

Let
X
be a unibranch (resp. geometrically unibranch) scheme.
Then for every integer
i>0
, we have the vanishing

H_{Nis}^{i}(X,\mathbb{Z})=0\text{ (resp.\ }H_{{\acute{e}t}}^{i}(X,\mathbb{Z})=0\text{)}.

Proof.

For the étale case, we refer to
[
4
, Proposition XI.3.6(2)]
.
The Nisnevich case can be proven similarly,
with reference to
[
34
, Proposition IV.18.12.6]
instead of
[
34
, Proposition IV.18.14.15]
.
∎

Proposition 5.1.5
.

Let
X
be a geometrically unibranch irreducible scheme.
Then the homomorphisms in (
5.1.3
) are isomorphisms.

Proof.

Let
i\colon X\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\to X\times\mathbb{P}^{n}
be the closed immersion at
\infty
.
There is an exact sequence of sheaves of abelian groups

0\to\mathcal{M}_{X\times\mathbb{P}^{n}}^{{\mathrm{gp}}}\to\mathcal{M}_{X\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})}^{{\mathrm{gp}}}\to i_{*}\mathbb{Z}\to 0.

Hence we have a long exact sequence

(5.1.4)

\begin{split}0&\to\mathcal{M}^{{\mathrm{gp}}}(X\times\mathbb{P}^{n})\to\mathcal{M}^{{\mathrm{gp}}}(X\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1}))\to H_{{\acute{e}t}}^{0}(X\times\mathbb{P}^{n},i_{*}\mathbb{Z})\\
&\xrightarrow{\delta}\operatorname{Pic}(X\times\mathbb{P}^{n})\to\operatorname{Pic}(X\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1}))\to H_{{\acute{e}t}}^{1}(X\times\mathbb{P}^{n},i_{*}\mathbb{Z})\to\cdots.\end{split}

Since
X\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
is irreducible, there are isomorphisms of abelian groups

(5.1.5)

\Gamma(X\times\mathbb{P}^{n},i_{*}\mathbb{Z})\cong\Gamma(X\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1},\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}.

Furthermore, since
X\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
is geometrically unibranch,
Proposition
5.1.4
implies the vanishing

(5.1.6)

H_{Nis}^{1}(X\times\mathbb{P}^{n},i_{*}\mathbb{Z})=H_{Nis}^{1}(X\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1},\mathbb{Z})=H_{{\acute{e}t}}^{1}(X\times\mathbb{P}^{n},i_{*}\mathbb{Z})=H_{{\acute{e}t}}^{1}(X\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1},\mathbb{Z})=0.

The element
1\in\mathbb{Z}
corresponds to a section
s\in\Gamma(X\times\mathbb{P}^{n},i_{*}\mathbb{Z})
.
There is a Zariski cover of
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
consisting of

U_{i}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{N}(x_{n}/x_{i})\to\mathbb{Z}[x_{0}/x_{i},\ldots,x_{n}/x_{i}])

for
0\leq i\leq n-1
and

U_{n}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x_{0}/x_{n},\ldots,x_{n}/x_{n}]).

The section
s
can be locally lifted to

x_{n}/x_{i}\in\Gamma(X\times U_{i},\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gp}})

for
0\leq i\leq n
.
The Zariski cover
\{X\times U_{i}\}
of
X\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
defines a sequence

0\to\bigoplus_{i}\Gamma(X\times U_{i},\mathcal{M}^{{\mathrm{gp}}})\to\bigoplus_{i\neq j}\Gamma(X\times(U_{i}\cap U_{j}),\mathcal{M}^{{\mathrm{gp}}}).

The section
(x_{n}/x_{0},\ldots,x_{n}/x_{n})
maps to a section whose components have the form

x_{j}/x_{i}\in\Gamma(X\times(U_{i}\cap U_{j}),\mathcal{O}^{*})\subset\Gamma(X\times(U_{i}\cap U_{j}),\mathcal{M}^{{\mathrm{gp}}}).

This is exactly the element in the Čech cohomology associated with
\mathcal{O}(1)\in\operatorname{Pic}(X\times\mathbb{P}^{n})
.
Hence the boundary map
\delta
in (
5.1.4
) sends
s
to
\mathcal{O}(1)\in\operatorname{Pic}(X\times\mathbb{P}^{n})
.
Together with Proposition
5.1.3
, (
5.1.5
), and (
5.1.6
) we conclude.
∎

Proposition 5.1.6
.

Suppose
S
is a geometrically unibranch scheme and
X\in\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
.
Then the homomorphisms in (
5.1.3
) are isomorphisms.

Proof.

By
[
34
, Proposition 17.5.7]
,
X
is geometrically unibranch.
Hence we only need to consider the case when
X
is irreducible since every connected component of
X
is an irreducible component.

We proceed by induction on the number of irreducible components
r
of
\partial X
.
The case
r=0
is done in Proposition
5.1.5
.
If
r>0
, let
D
be an irreducible component of
\partial X
.
Let
Y
be the fs log scheme whose underlying scheme is
\underline{X}
and whose log structure is the compactifying log structure associated with the open immersion

X-\overline{\partial X-D}\to X.

For simplicity of notation, we set

X^{\prime}:=X\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1}),\text{ }Y^{\prime}:=Y\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1}),\text{ and }D^{\prime}:=D\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1}).

Let
i\colon D\to X
and
i^{\prime}\colon D^{\prime}\to X^{\prime}
denote the closed immersions.
Since
D
and
D^{\prime}
are geometrically unibranch by
[
34
, Proposition 17.5.7]
,
as in (
5.1.5
) and (
5.1.6
) there are isomorphisms of abelian groups

(5.1.7)

\Gamma(X,i_{*}\mathbb{Z})\cong\Gamma(X^{\prime},i_{*}^{\prime}\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}.

and the vanishing

(5.1.8)

H_{sNis}^{1}(X,i_{*}\mathbb{Z})=H_{sNis}^{1}(X^{\prime},i_{*}^{\prime}\mathbb{Z})=H_{{s\acute{e}t}}^{1}(X,i_{*}\mathbb{Z})=H_{{s\acute{e}t}}^{1}(X^{\prime},i_{*}^{\prime}\mathbb{Z})=0.

There are exact sequences

0\to\mathcal{M}_{Y}^{{\mathrm{gp}}}\to\mathcal{M}_{X}^{{\mathrm{gp}}}\to i_{*}\mathbb{Z}\to 0\text{ and }0\to\mathcal{M}_{Y^{\prime}}^{{\mathrm{gp}}}\to\mathcal{M}_{X^{\prime}}^{{\mathrm{gp}}}\to i_{*}^{\prime}\mathbb{Z}\to 0.

Together with (
5.1.7
) and (
5.1.8
), these give a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{{\mathrm{gp}}}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{{\mathrm{gp}}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{logPic}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{logPic}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{{\mathrm{gp}}}(Y^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{{\mathrm{gp}}}(X^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{logPic}(Y^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{logPic}(X^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}

with exact rows.
We have a similar commutative diagram for
\operatorname{Pic}_{{s\acute{e}t}}
.
By induction,
a
and
c
are isomorphisms.
An application of the five lemma shows that
b
and
d
are isomorphisms too.
∎

We do not know whether there is an isomorphism of abelian groups

\operatorname{logPic}(X)\xrightarrow{\cong}\operatorname{logPic}(X\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1}))

for arbitrary fs log schemes
X
and
n\geq 1
.
The assumption imposed in Proposition
5.1.6
seems too restrictive.
In the following, we investigate another case.

Proposition 5.1.7
.

Let
X
be a saturated log scheme such that
\underline{X}
is the spectrum of a field.
Then the homomorphisms in (
5.1.3
) are isomorphisms.

Proof.

We set
G:=\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{{\mathrm{gp}}}(X)
as abbreviation.
The assumption that
X
is saturated implies that
G
is torsion-free.
Hence,

[
4
, Proposition XI.3.6(2)]
is applicable to
G
due to
[
4
, Remarques XI.3.7]
.
Since
\underline{X}
and
\underline{X}\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
are irreducible and geometrically unibranch, as in (
5.1.5
) and (
5.1.6
) there are isomorphisms of abelian groups

(5.1.9)

\Gamma(X,G)\cong\Gamma(X\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1}),G)\cong G

and the vanishing

(5.1.10)

H_{sNis}^{1}(X,G)=H_{sNis}^{1}(X\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1},G)=H_{{s\acute{e}t}}^{1}(X,G)=H_{{s\acute{e}t}}^{1}(X\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1},G)=0.

Let
i\colon\underline{X}\times\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\to\underline{X}\times\mathbb{P}^{n}
be the closed immersion at
\infty
.
There are exact sequences

0\to\mathcal{M}_{\underline{X}}^{\mathrm{gp}}\to\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gp}}_{X}\to G\to 0\text{ and }0\to\mathcal{M}_{\underline{X}\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})}^{{\mathrm{gp}}}\to\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gp}}_{X\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})}\to i_{*}G\to 0.

As abbreviations, we set
Y:=\underline{X}
,
X^{\prime}=X\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
, and
Y^{\prime}:=\underline{X}\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
.
Together with (
5.1.9
) and (
5.1.10
), these give a commutative diagram with exact rows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{{\mathrm{gp}}}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{{\mathrm{gp}}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{logPic}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{logPic}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{{\mathrm{gp}}}(Y^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{{\mathrm{gp}}}(X^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{logPic}(Y^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{logPic}(X^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}

We have a similar commutative diagram for
\operatorname{Pic}_{{s\acute{e}t}}
.
By Proposition
5.1.5
,
a
and
c
are isomorphisms.
The five lemma lets us conclude that also
b
and
d
are isomorphisms.
∎

5.2.
The infinite projective space

Morel-Voevodsky showed the equivalence

(5.2.1)

\mathbb{P}^{\infty}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{B}{\mathbb{G}}_{m}

in
\mathcal{H}(S)
, where
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
, see
[
70
, Proposition 3.7, p. 138]
.
As argued in this text,

\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}
replaces
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
in
\square
-motivic homotopy theory.
The purpose of this section is to show that there is an equivalence

(5.2.2)

\mathbb{P}^{\infty}\to\mathrm{B}\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}

in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
for every
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
.

There is an explicit description of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopies that yields the equivalence
(
5.2.1
) in
[
74
, §2]
.
Our method of establishing (
5.2.2
) is to “compactify” their argument.

Definition 5.2.1
.

In this subsection, we use the abbreviation

V_{n}:=({\mathrm{Bl}}_{O}(\mathbb{P}^{n}),H+E)

for every integer
n\geq 1
,
where
O
is the point
[0:\cdots:0:1]
in
\mathbb{P}^{n}
,

H
is the divisor
\mathbb{P}^{n}-\mathbb{A}^{n}
,
and
E
is the exceptional divisor.
Observe that the log structure on
V_{n}
is the compactifying log structure
associated with the open immersion
\mathbb{A}^{n}-0\to{\mathrm{Bl}}_{O}(\mathbb{P}^{n})
.

There is a closed immersion

\mathbb{P}^{n}\to\mathbb{P}^{n+1}

sending
[a_{1}:\cdots:a_{n+1}]
to
[a_{1}:\cdots:a_{n}:0:a_{n+1}]
.
Since the preimage of
O\in\mathbb{P}^{n+1}
is
O\in\mathbb{P}^{n}
, there is a naturally induced morphism

(5.2.3)

{\mathrm{Bl}}_{O}(\mathbb{P}^{n})\to{\mathrm{Bl}}_{O}(\mathbb{P}^{n+1}).

This is an extension of the closed immersion

(5.2.4)

\mathbb{A}^{n}-0\to\mathbb{A}^{n+1}-0

sending
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
to
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},0)
.
Combine (
5.2.3
) and (
5.2.4
) to produce a strict closed immersion

(5.2.5)

i\colon V_{n}\to V_{n+1}.

Finally,
we form a colimit in the category of presheaves

V_{\infty}:=\mathop{\rm colim}_{n}V_{n}.

Lemma 5.2.2
.

Let
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
and
\operatorname{pt}=\mathcal{F}_{0}\to\mathcal{F}_{1}\to\cdots
be an infinite sequence in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
.
Suppose for every integer
i\geq 0
,
there exists
j>i
such that
\mathcal{F}_{i}\to\mathcal{F}_{j}
is equivalent to the constant morphism given by the composite

\mathcal{F}_{i}\to\operatorname{pt}\to\mathcal{F}_{1}\to\cdots\to\mathcal{F}_{j}.

Then
\mathop{\rm colim}_{i}\mathcal{F}_{i}
is equivalent to
\operatorname{pt}
in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
.

Proof.

Due to Corollary
2.7.2
, it suffices to show that

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{logH}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)}(\Sigma_{S^{1}}^{n}X,\mathop{\rm colim}_{i}\mathcal{F}_{i})

is trivial for all
X\in\mathbf{lSm}/S
and integer
n\geq 0
.
By a standard reduction, we can assume that
S=\operatorname{Spec}(A)
is affine, and noetherian.
Since
X
is a compact object (see Remark
2.7.4
), it suffices to show that

(5.2.6)

\mathop{\rm colim}_{i}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{logH}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)}(\Sigma_{S^{1}}^{n}X,\mathcal{F}_{i})

is trivial.
If
f\colon\Sigma_{S^{1}}^{n}X\to\mathcal{F}_{i}
is any morphism,
the composite

\Sigma_{S^{1}}^{n}X\to\mathcal{F}_{i}\to\mathcal{F}_{j}

is equivalent to the constant morphism given by the composite

\Sigma_{S^{1}}^{n}X\to\operatorname{pt}\to\mathcal{F}_{1}\to\cdots\to\mathcal{F}_{j}.

This shows the equivalence in (
5.2.6
).
∎

For every integer
n\geq 1
, let
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}
be the points
(1,0,\ldots,0)
,
\ldots
,
(0,\ldots,0,1)
in
\mathbb{A}^{n}-0
.

Lemma 5.2.3
.

Suppose
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
.
For every integer
n\geq 1
, the inclusion

i\colon V_{n}\rightarrow V_{n+1}

in (
5.2.5
) is equivalent to the constant map to
v_{n+1}\in V_{n+1}
in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
.

Proof.

Let
u\colon S\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
be the structure morphism.
If we show the claim for
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
, then we can use
u^{*}
to deduce the claim for
S
.
Hence we may assume
S=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
.

The closed immersion
v
in (
5.2.4
) is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopic to a constant map via the morphism

h\colon(\mathbb{A}^{n}-0)\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\to\mathbb{A}^{n+1}-0

sending
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},t)
to
(ta_{1},\ldots,ta_{n},1-t)
.
We use the coordinates
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
for
\mathbb{A}^{n}
,
t
for
\mathbb{A}^{1}
, and
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y\}
for
\mathbb{A}^{n+1}
.
The morphism
h
induces a homomorphism

u\colon\mathbb{Z}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y)\to\mathbb{Z}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},t)

sending
x_{i}
to
tx_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
and
y
to
1-t
.

We note that

h
extends to a morphism

g\colon{\mathrm{Bl}}_{E\times[1:1]+H\times[0:1]}({\mathrm{Bl}}_{O}(\mathbb{P}^{n})\times\mathbb{P}^{1})\to{\mathrm{Bl}}_{O}(\mathbb{P}^{n+1}),

where
E
is the exceptional divisor on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
, and
H:=\mathbb{P}^{n}-\mathbb{A}^{n}
.
To show this, we will define
g
Zariski locally as follows.
The scheme
{\mathrm{Bl}}_{O}(\mathbb{P}^{n+1})
has a Zariski cover consisting of spectra of the rings

\displaystyle A_{i}:=\mathbb{Z}[x_{i},x_{1}/x_{i},\ldots,x_{n}/x_{i},y/x_{i}],\;B:=\mathbb{Z}[y,x_{1}/y,\ldots,x_{n}/y],

\displaystyle C_{i}:=\mathbb{Z}[1/x_{i},x_{1}/x_{i},\ldots,x_{n}/x_{i},y/x_{i}],\;D:=\mathbb{Z}[1/y,x_{1}/y,\ldots,x_{n}/y]

for
1\leq i\leq n
.
The scheme
{\mathrm{Bl}}_{O}(\mathbb{P}^{n})\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
has a Zariski cover consisting of spectra of the rings

\displaystyle E_{i}:=\mathbb{Z}[x_{i},x_{1}/x_{i},\ldots,x_{n}/x_{i},1/(1-t)],\;F_{i}:=\mathbb{Z}[x_{i},x_{1}/x_{i},\ldots,x_{n}/x_{i},t,1/t],

\displaystyle G_{i}:=\mathbb{Z}[1/x_{i},x_{1}/x_{i},\ldots,x_{n}/x_{i},t,1/(1-t)],\;H_{i}:=\mathbb{Z}[1/x_{i},x_{1}/x_{i},\ldots,x_{n}/x_{i},1/t]

for
1\leq i\leq n
.
The scheme
{\mathrm{Bl}}_{E\times[1:1]+H\times[0:1]}({\mathrm{Bl}}_{O}(\mathbb{P}^{n})\times\mathbb{P}^{1})
has a Zariski cover
consisting of spectra of the rings
E_{i}
,
H_{i}
, and

\displaystyle F_{i}^{\prime}:=\mathbb{Z}[x_{i},(1-t)/x_{i},x_{1}/x_{i},\ldots,x_{n}/x_{i},1/t],

\displaystyle F_{i}^{\prime\prime}:=\mathbb{Z}[1-t,x_{i}/(1-t),x_{1}/x_{i},\ldots,x_{n}/x_{i},1/t],

\displaystyle G_{i}^{\prime}:=\mathbb{Z}[1/x_{i},tx_{i},x_{1}/x_{i},\ldots,x_{n}/x_{i},1/(1-t)],

\displaystyle G_{i}^{\prime\prime}:=\mathbb{Z}[t,1/(tx_{i}),x_{1}/x_{i},\ldots,x_{n}/x_{i},1/(1-t)]

for
1\leq i\leq n
.
For convenience, we include the following expressions

\displaystyle u(A_{i})=\mathbb{Z}[tx_{i},x_{1}/x_{i},\ldots,x_{n}/x_{i},(1-t)/(tx_{i})],

\displaystyle u(B_{i})=\mathbb{Z}[1-t,tx_{1}/(1-t),\ldots,tx_{n}/(1-t)],

\displaystyle u(C_{i})=\mathbb{Z}[1/(tx_{i}),x_{1}/x_{i},\ldots,x_{n}/x_{i},(1-t)/(tx_{i})],

\displaystyle u(D_{i})=\mathbb{Z}[1/(1-t),tx_{1}/(1-t),\ldots,tx_{n}/(1-t)].

There are inclusions

u(D_{i})\subset E_{i},\;u(A_{i})\subset F_{i}^{\prime},\;u(B_{i})\subset F_{i}^{\prime\prime},\;u(D_{i})\subset G_{i}^{\prime},\;u(C_{i})\subset G_{i}^{\prime\prime},\;u(C_{i})\subset H_{i}.

From these inclusions, we can construct the morphism
g
that extends
h
.

Let
X
be the fs log scheme with the underlying scheme

{\mathrm{Bl}}_{E\times[1:1]+H\times[0:1]}({\mathrm{Bl}}_{O}(\mathbb{P}^{n})\times\mathbb{P}^{1})
,
and whose log structure is the compactifying log structure associated with the open immersion

(\mathbb{A}^{n}-0)\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\to{\mathrm{Bl}}_{E\times[1:1]+H\times[0:1]}({\mathrm{Bl}}_{O}(\mathbb{P}^{n})\times\mathbb{P}^{1}).

Next,
g
gives a morphism

f\colon Y\to V_{n+1}

extending
h
, and there is a projection
p\colon Y\to V_{n}\times\square
.
The zero section and one section
i_{0},i_{1}\colon V_{n}\to V_{n}\times\square
admits lifts

i_{0}^{\prime},i_{1}^{\prime}\colon V_{n}\to Y.

Since
p
is a composite of admissible blow-ups along smooth centers,
we can apply Lemma
3.2.12
to deduce that
i=fi_{1}^{\prime}
is equivalent to the constant map

fi_{0}^{\prime}
in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
.
∎

Remark 5.2.4
.

We note that
h
appearing in Lemma
5.2.3
cannot be extended to a morphism

{\mathrm{Bl}}_{O}(\mathbb{P}^{n})\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\to{\mathrm{Bl}}_{O}(\mathbb{P}^{n+1})

since
g
does not factor through the blow-down
{\mathrm{Bl}}_{O}(\mathbb{P}^{n})\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

Lemma 5.2.5
.

For every
S\in\mathbf{lSch}
and
n\geq 2
, the two constant maps

\operatorname{pt}\rightrightarrows V_{n}

to
v_{1}
and
v_{n}
are equivalent in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}(S)
.

Proof.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{n}
be the morphism given by

[a:b]\mapsto[a:0\cdots:0:b-a:b].

Since
f(\mathbb{A}^{1})\subset\mathbb{A}^{n}
,

f
naturally induces a morphism
g\colon\square\to(\mathbb{P}^{n},H)
, where
H
is the divisor
\mathbb{P}^{n}-\mathbb{A}^{n}
.
The image of
f
does not intersect with the point
O:=[0:\cdots:0:1]
, so
g
can be lifted to a morphism

h\colon\square\to V_{n}.

To conclude, observe that
h([0:1])=v_{n}
and
h([1:1])=v_{1}
.
∎

Proposition 5.2.6
.

For every
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
,
the object
V_{\infty}
is equivalent to
\operatorname{pt}
in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
.

Proof.

By Lemmas
5.2.3
and
5.2.5
,
the morphism
V_{n}\to V_{n+1}
is equivalent to the the constant map to
v_{1}
in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
.
Then use Lemma
5.2.2
.
∎

Definition 5.2.7
.

Suppose
S\in\mathbf{lSch}
and
G\in\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{dNis}(\mathbf{lSm}/S)
is a group object acting on
X\in\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{dNis}(\mathbf{lSm}/S)
.
Let
X/^{h}G
denote the homotopy colimit of the simplicial object

(5.2.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times G^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}

If
X\to Y
is a
G
-equivariant morphism in
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{dNis}(\mathbf{lSm}/S)
with trivial
G
-action on
Y
,
there is a naturally induced morphism
X/^{h}G\to Y
.

Example 5.2.8
.

For every
S\in\mathbf{lSch}
there is no morphism

m\colon\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}\to\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}

in
\mathbf{lSm}/S
that extends the multiplication
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}\times{\mathbb{G}}_{m}\to{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
sending
(x,y)
to
xy

since it is impossible to define
m
at
(0,\infty)
and
(\infty,0)
.
However,
m
exists in
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{dNis}(\mathbf{lSm}/S)
.
To show this, consider the morphism

p\colon Y:=({\mathrm{Bl}}_{(0,\infty)\cup(\infty,0)}(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}),\widetilde{H_{1}}+\widetilde{H_{2}}+\widetilde{H_{3}}+\widetilde{H_{4}}+E_{1}+E_{2})\to\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}},

where
\widetilde{H_{i}}
is the strict transforms of
0\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times 0
,
\infty\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
or
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\infty
, and
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
are the exceptional divisors.
Since
p
is a dividing cover,

p
is an equivalence as a morphism of sheaves.
We now claim that there exists a morphism
n\colon Y\to\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}
extending the multiplication morphism
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}\times{\mathbb{G}}_{m}\to{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
.
To show this,
glue the morphisms of schemes associated with the six homomorphisms

\mathbb{Z}[x]\to\mathbb{Z}[x,y],\;\mathbb{Z}[xy,1/y],\;\mathbb{Z}[x,1/(xy)],\;\mathbb{Z}[1/x,xy],\;\mathbb{Z}[1/(xy),y],\;\mathbb{Z}[1/x,1/y]

sending
x
to
xy
,
and check that the morphisms are compatible with the log structures.
We set
m
to be the composite
np^{-1}
.

The unit of
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}
is defined to be the one section
u\colon 1\to\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}
.
The morphism
\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
sending
[a:b]
to
[b:a]
sends
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
onto
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
.
By
[
78
, Proposition III.1.6.2]
, this naturally induces a morphism
i\colon\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}\to\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}
.

With the construction described above, the fact that
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
is a group object readily implies that
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}
is a group object in
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{dNis}(\mathbf{lSm}/S)
.

Lemma 5.2.9
.

Suppose
S\in\mathbf{lSch}
.
There is an equivalence

(5.2.8)

V_{n+1}/^{h}\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathbb{P}^{n}

in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}(S)
.

Proof.

The scheme
\mathbb{P}^{n}
has a Zariski cover consisting of

U_{i}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x_{0}/x_{i},\ldots,x_{n}/x_{i}])

for
0\leq i\leq n
.
The fs log scheme
V_{n+1}
has a Zariski cover consisting of

V_{i}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{N}(x_{i})\to\mathbb{Z}[x_{i},x_{0}/x_{i},\ldots,x_{n}/x_{i}])

and

V_{i}^{\prime}:=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{N}(1/x_{i})\to\mathbb{Z}[1/x_{i},x_{0}/x_{i},\ldots,x_{n}/x_{i}])

for
0\leq i\leq n
,
where
\mathbb{N}(x_{i})
and
\mathbb{N}(1/x_{i})
denotes the free monoid generated by
x
and
1/x_{i}
.
There is a canonical isomorphism of fs log schemes

V_{i}\cup V_{i}^{\prime}\cong U_{i}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}.

If
I
is a subset of
\{0,\ldots,n\}
, we set

U_{I}:=\cap_{i\in I}U_{i},\text{ }V_{I}:=\cap_{i\in I}V_{i},\text{ and }V_{I}^{\prime}:=\cap_{i\in I}V_{i}^{\prime}.

Then there is a canonical isomorphism of fs log schemes

(5.2.9)

V_{I}\cup V_{I}^{\prime}\cong U_{I}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}.

Glue these all together to have a morphism

f\colon V_{n+1}\to\mathbb{P}^{n}.

Furthermore, from (
5.2.9
), we have a
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}
-action on
V_{n+1}
.
The morphism
f
is
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}
-equivariant with trivial
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}
-action on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
.
This constructs the morphism in (
5.2.8
).
We show that it is an equivalence.
By considering the Čech nerve of
\mathbb{P}^{n}
associated with
\{U_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
,
it suffices to show

(U_{I}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}})/^{h}\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}\simeq U_{I}

for all subsets
I
of
\{0,\ldots,n\}
.
This follows since the simplicial diagram (
5.2.7
) defining
(U_{I}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}})/^{h}\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}

admits an evident retraction.
∎

Theorem 5.2.10
.

For every
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
there is an equivalence

\mathrm{B}\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}\simeq\mathbb{P}^{\infty}

in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
,
which is natural in
S
.

Proof.

Colimits preserve equivalences, so Lemma
5.2.9
yields an equivalence

(5.2.10)

V_{\infty}/^{h}\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}\simeq\mathbb{P}^{\infty}

in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{SmAdm}}(S)
.
Proposition
5.2.6
and
[
70
, Proposition 2.14, p. 74]
finish the proof.
∎

Corollary 5.2.11
.

For every
S\in\mathbf{lSch}
,
there is an equivalence

\mathrm{B}\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}\simeq\mathbb{P}^{\infty}

in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}^{\rm eff}(S,\Lambda)
and
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(S,\Lambda)
,
which is natural in
S
.

Proof.

The claim holds for
\underline{S}
by Proposition
3.5.4
and Theorem
5.2.10
.
We deduce the claim for
S
by pulling back.
∎

6.
K
-theory of fs log schemes

6.1.
Setting and summary of the results

Morel and Voevodsky
[
70
, Propositions 3.7 and 3.9]
proved that the algebraic
K
-theory of regular schemes is represented
geometrically by the infinite Grassmannian
\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}}
in the unstable motivic homotopy category
\mathcal{H}(S)_{\ast}
for any noetherian scheme
S
of finite Krull dimension. This result was later refined by Voevodsky in
[
98
]
, where the Bott periodic
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-spectrum
{\mathbf{KGL}}
was constructed. In
[
98
]
Voevodsky announced that
{\mathbf{KGL}}

represents homotopy invariant algebraic
K
-theory for schemes in the
stable
motivic homotopy category.
Cisinski completed the proof in
[
28
, Théorème 2.20]
. See also
[
81
]
for a detailed discussion.

The main goal of this section is to construct a logarithmic
K
-theory spectrum
\mathbf{logKGL}
in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(S)
for any
regular scheme
S
(see Definition
6.5.6
), and
show that there is an equivalence

(6.1.1)

\mathbf{logKGL}\simeq(\mathbb{L}_{sNis,\square\cup\mathbf{Adm}}\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}},\mathbb{L}_{sNis,\square\cup\mathbf{Adm}}\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}},\ldots)

in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(S)
.
Here the pointed log motivic space
\mathbb{L}_{sNis,\square\cup\mathbf{Adm}}\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}}
can be viewed as a
logarithmic replacement for the infinite Grassmannian employed by Morel-Voevodsky
[
70
]
.
Our proof of the equivalence (
6.1.1
) uses the singular functor
\operatorname{Sing}
discussed
in §
6.3
.
This is the root cause for assuming that
S
is a regular scheme
in (
6.1.1
). At the end of this section,
we will compare our logarithmic
K
-theory with Niziol’s
K
-theories of fs log schemes defined in
[
77
]
.
Elementary computations verify that the two theories are different.

The main application of the above-mentioned result will be discuss in §
8.6
, where we will use the geometric representability of logarithmic
K
-theory
for constructing a logarithmic cyclotomic trace map, as a morphism internal to our motivic homotopy theory. Note that the corresponding construction would dramatically fail in
\mathcal{SH}(S)
.

Remark 6.1.1
.

Let
\mathrm{K}\in\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{Sch}_{noeth},\mathcal{S}\mathrm{pt})

denote the Bass
K
-theory presheaf of spectra.
If
X\in\mathbf{Sch}
, then

\pi_{n}\mathrm{K}(X)\cong\mathrm{K}_{n}(X)

for all integers
n
, where
\mathrm{K}_{n}(X)
is the
n
th Bass
K
-group of perfect complexes on
X
.

Due to
[
70
, Propositions 3.9]
,
there is a canonical equivalence of sheaves of pointed spaces

(6.1.2)

\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}\simeq\mathbb{L}_{Nis}\Omega\mathrm{B}\big(\coprod_{n\geq 0}{\mathrm{BGL}}_{n}\big),

where
\mathbb{L}_{Nis}
is the Nisnevich localization functor, and
\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}
is the infinite suspension functor.

If
X\in\mathbf{Sch}_{noeth}
is regular,
then the Bass
K
-theory spectrum
\mathrm{K}(X)
and
\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}
participates in an equivalence of spectra

(6.1.3)

\mathrm{K}(X)\simeq(\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}(X),\Omega_{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}(X),\Omega_{\mathbb{G}_{m}}^{2}\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}(X),\ldots),

where the bonding maps

\Sigma_{S^{1}}\Omega_{\mathbb{G}_{m}}^{n}\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}(X)\to\Omega_{\mathbb{G}_{m}}^{n+1}\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}(X)

are naturally induced by the map

\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}\to\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}

appearing in the projective bundle formula
[
95
, Theorem 7.3]
for
K
-theory.
Restricting
\mathrm{K}
to
\mathbf{Sm}/S^{\mathrm{op}}
defines an object of
\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}(S)
,
since
\mathrm{K}(-)
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant for regular schemes and satisfies Nisnevich descent.

6.2.
The singular functor

In this subsection,
we begin by reviewing the singular functor in
[
22
, §6]
.

Throughout,

\mathbf{T}
is a small ordinary category with finite limits,

\operatorname{pt}
denotes the initial object, and
\mathcal{A}
is a set of morphisms in
\mathbf{T}
that admits a calculus of right fractions.
Moreover,

I
is an interval object of
\mathbf{T}
in the sense of
[
70
, §2.3]
,
i.e., an object of
\mathbf{T}
together with morphisms

i_{0},i_{1}\colon\operatorname{pt}\to I,\;p\colon I\to\operatorname{pt}

in
\mathbf{T}
and with a morphism

\mu\colon I\times I\to I

in
\mathbf{T}[\mathcal{A}^{-1}]
satisfying the identities

\displaystyle\mu\circ(i_{0}\times\operatorname{id})=(\operatorname{id}\times i_{0})\circ\mu=i_{0}p,

\displaystyle\mu\circ(i_{1}\times\operatorname{id})=(\operatorname{id}\times i_{1})\circ\mu=\operatorname{id}.

We also assume that
i_{0}\amalg i_{1}\colon\operatorname{pt}\amalg\operatorname{pt}\to I
is a monomorphism.

There is a localization functor

v\colon\mathbf{T}\rightarrow\mathbf{T}[\mathcal{A}^{-1}]

inducing an adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{T})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{T}[\mathcal{A}^{-1}]).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{*}}

Here
v_{\sharp}
is left adjoint to
v^{*}
,
and
v^{*}
is left adjoint to
v_{*}
.
Let
\mathcal{A}\downarrow X
denote the over category of
X
,
whose objects are the morphisms
f\colon Y\to X
in
\mathcal{A}
, with the obvious convention for maps. The functors
v_{\sharp}
and
v^{*}
are then given objectwise by

(6.2.1)

v_{\sharp}\mathcal{F}(X)\cong\mathop{\rm colim}_{Y\in\mathcal{A}\downarrow X}\mathcal{F}(Y),\quad v^{*}\mathcal{G}(X):=\mathcal{G}(X)

for any presheaf of Kan complexes
\mathcal{F}\in\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{T})
,
\mathcal{G}\in\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{T}[\mathcal{A}^{-1}])
, and
X\in\mathbf{T}
.

For abbreviation, let
I
be the set of projections
X\times I\to X
for all
X\in\mathbf{T}
.

Proposition 6.2.1
.

The unit
\operatorname{id}\to v^{*}v_{\sharp}
is an
\mathcal{A}
-local equivalence.

Proof.

We refer to
[
22
, Proposition 6.1.11]
.
∎

By assumption,
I
is an interval object of
\mathbf{T}[\mathcal{A}^{-1}]
in the sense of
[
70
, §2.3]
. Recall that there is a functor

\operatorname{Sing}^{I}\colon\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{T}[\mathcal{A}^{-1}])\to\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{T}[\mathcal{A}^{-1}]).

that is defined as follows: let
\Delta_{I}^{\bullet}
be the simplicial object of
\mathbf{T}[\mathcal{A}^{-1}]
defined objectwise by
[n]\mapsto I^{n}
(see
[
70
, p.88]
, or
[
22
, 6.2]
). Then we have the
\infty
-functor

\mathcal{F}\mapsto\underline{\operatorname{Map}}(y(\Delta_{I}^{\bullet}),\mathcal{F})\in\operatorname{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{T}[\mathcal{A}^{-1}]).

We let
\operatorname{Sing}^{I}(\mathcal{F})
be the geometric realization of
\underline{\operatorname{Map}}(y(\Delta_{I}^{\bullet}),\mathcal{F})
. Using e.g.,
[
63
, A.2.9.30]
one can readily verify that it agrees with
[
22
, Definition 6.2.1]
.

Definition 6.2.2
.

The singular functor

\operatorname{Sing}^{I\cup\mathcal{A}}\colon\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{T})\to\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{T})

is given by
v^{*}\operatorname{Sing}^{I}v_{\sharp}
.

Definition 6.2.3
.

Let
f,g\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G}
be two morphisms in
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{T})
.
An
elementary
I\cup\mathcal{A}
-homotopy between
f
and
g
is a morphism

h\colon v_{\sharp}\mathcal{F}\times I\to v_{\sharp}\mathcal{G}

in
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{T}[\mathcal{A}^{-1}])
such that

h\circ i_{0}=f\text{ and }h\circ i_{1}=g.

Two morphisms are called
I\cup\mathcal{A}
-homotopic
if they can be connected by
a finite number of elementary
I\cup\mathcal{A}
-homotopies.

A morphism
f\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G}
in
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{T})
is called a

strict
I\cup\mathcal{A}
-homotopy equivalence
if there exists
a morphism
g\colon\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{F}
in
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{T})
such that the compositions
f\circ g
and
g\circ f
are both
I\cup\mathcal{A}
-homotopic to the respective identities.

Proposition 6.2.4
.

The following statements hold true.

(1)

The morphism

i_{0}\colon\operatorname{pt}\to I

is a strict
I\cup\mathcal{A}
-homotopy equivalence.

(2)

If
f
and
g
are
I\cup\mathcal{A}
-homotopic morphisms in
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{T})
,
then
\operatorname{Sing}^{I\cup\mathcal{A}}(f)
and
\operatorname{Sing}^{I\cup\mathcal{A}}(g)
are homotopic.

(3)

For every
X\in\mathbf{T}
, the morphism

\operatorname{Sing}^{I\cup\mathcal{A}}(X)\to\operatorname{Sing}^{I\cup\mathcal{A}}(X\times\square)

naturally induced by
i_{0}
is an equivalence equivalence of presheaves of Kan complexes.

(4)

For every
\mathcal{F}\in\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{T})
, the canonical morphism

\mathcal{F}\to\operatorname{Sing}^{I\cup\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{F})

is an
I\cup\mathcal{A}
-local equivalence.

Proof.

We refer to
[
22
, Propositions 6.2.3, 6.2.4, 6.2.6, Corollary 6.2.5]
.
∎

6.3.
Applications of
\operatorname{Sing}

Our theory of
\operatorname{Sing}
is mainly applied to the case when
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{cSm}/S
,
where
S
is a scheme,

I=\square
, and
\mathcal{A}
is the set of admissible blow-ups
\mathbf{Adm}
in
\mathbf{cSm}/S
.

Proposition 6.3.1
.

For every
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
,
the class of admissible blow-ups in
\mathbf{cSm}/S
admits a calculus of right fractions.

Proof.

It is obvious that the class of admissible blow-ups is closed under compositions.

The class of admissible blow-ups is closed under pullbacks,
so the class of admissible blow-ups satisfies the right Ore condition.

Suppose
f,g\colon Y\to X
and
h\colon X\to X^{\prime}
be morphisms in
\mathbf{cSm}/S
such that
hf=hg
.
Then
f
and
g
agree on the dense open subscheme
Y-\partial Y
of
Y
,
so we have
f=g
.
This shows that the class of admissible blow-ups satisfies the right cancellability condition.
∎

Proposition 6.3.2
.

For every
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
and presheaf
\mathcal{F}\in\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{cSm}/S)
,
the canonical morphism

\mathcal{F}\to\operatorname{Sing}^{\square\cup\mathbf{Adm}}(\mathcal{F})

is a
\square\cup\mathbf{Adm}
-local equivalence.

Proof.

Immediate from Proposition
6.2.4
(4).
∎

Proposition 6.3.3
.

Suppose
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
and
\mathcal{F}\in\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{cSm}/S)
is of the form
\mathop{\rm colim}\mathcal{F}_{i}
,
where each
\mathcal{F}_{i}
is representable by a smooth and proper scheme over
S
.
Then the unit map

\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\omega^{*}\omega_{\sharp}\mathcal{F}

is a
\square\cup\mathbf{Adm}
-local equivalence.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.2.1
, there is a
\square\cup\mathbf{Adm}
-local equivalence

\mathcal{F}\to v^{*}v_{\sharp}\mathcal{F}.

Use (
6.2.1
) to have an equivalence of Kan complexes

v^{*}v_{\sharp}\mathcal{F}(Y)\cong\mathop{\rm colim}_{Y^{\prime}\in\mathbf{Adm}\downarrow Y}\mathcal{F}(Y^{\prime}).

On the other hand, there is an equivalence

\omega^{*}\omega_{\sharp}\mathcal{F}(Y)\cong\mathcal{F}(Y-\partial Y).

Hence we only need to show that the map

\mathop{\rm colim}_{Y^{\prime}\in\mathbf{Adm}\downarrow Y}\mathcal{F}(Y^{\prime})\to\mathcal{F}(Y-\partial Y)

is an equivalence.
We only need to show this for each
\mathcal{F}_{i}
.
Hence we only need to show that the map

(6.3.1)

\mathop{\rm colim}_{Y^{\prime}\in\mathbf{Adm}\downarrow Y}\operatorname{Hom}(Y^{\prime},X)\to\operatorname{Hom}(Y-\partial Y,X)

is an isomorphism for every smooth and proper scheme
X
over
S
.

Suppose
f\colon Y-\partial Y\to X
is a morphism of schemes.
Let
\underline{Y^{\prime}}
be the closure of the image of the graph morphism

Y-\partial Y\to(Y-\partial Y)\times\underline{X}
in
\underline{Y}\times\underline{X}
.
By setting
Y^{\prime}:=(\underline{Y^{\prime}},Y^{\prime}-\partial Y^{\prime})
and
Y^{\prime}-\partial Y^{\prime}
as the preimage of
Y-\partial Y
in
Y^{\prime}
,
we obtain an admissible blow-up
p
participating
in a commutative diagram of fs log schemes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

This shows that (
6.3.1
) is surjective.

It remains to show that (
6.3.1
) is injective.
Suppose
Y^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime\prime}
are two admissible blow-ups of
Y
with two morphisms
f^{\prime}\colon Y^{\prime}\to X
and

f^{\prime\prime}\colon Y^{\prime\prime}\to X
such that the restrictions of
f^{\prime}
and
f^{\prime\prime}
to
Y-\partial Y
are equal.
We need to show that there exists a common admissible blow-up
Y^{\prime\prime\prime}
of
Y^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime\prime}
such that
we have the same morphism from
f^{\prime}
and
f^{\prime\prime}
by composing
Y^{\prime\prime\prime}\to Y^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime\prime\prime}\to Y^{\prime\prime}
.
A common admissible blow-up always exists since
\mathbf{Adm}\downarrow Y
admits a calculus of
right fractions by Proposition
6.3.1
, so we may assume that
Y^{\prime}=Y^{\prime\prime}
.
To conclude,
observe that
Y-\partial Y
is dense in
Y^{\prime}
and
X
is separated.
∎

Lemma 6.3.4
.

For
\mathcal{F}\in\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{cSm}/S)
, where
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
,
there is a natural isomorphism

\operatorname{Sing}^{\square\cup\mathbf{Adm}}\omega^{*}\mathcal{F}\cong\omega^{*}\operatorname{Sing}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\mathcal{F}.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{G}\in\mathcal{P}\mathrm{sh}(\mathbf{cSm}/S)
. Recall that
\operatorname{Sing}^{\square\cup\mathbf{Adm}}\mathcal{G}
is defined as the geometric realization of the simplicial presheaf

[n]\mapsto\operatorname{Sing}_{n}^{\square\cup\mathbf{Adm}}\mathcal{G}=v^{*}\operatorname{Sing}_{n}^{\square}v_{\sharp}\mathcal{G}=v^{*}\underline{\operatorname{Map}}(\square^{n},v_{\sharp}\mathcal{G}).

For every
X\in\mathbf{cSm}/S
,
we have then

(6.3.2)

\operatorname{Sing}_{n}^{\square\cup\mathbf{Adm}}\omega^{*}\mathcal{F}(X)=\mathop{\rm colim}_{Y\in\mathbf{Adm}\downarrow(X\times\square^{n})}\omega^{*}\mathcal{F}(Y)=\mathop{\rm colim}_{Y\in\mathbf{Adm}\downarrow(X\times\square^{n})}\mathcal{F}(Y-\partial Y).

If
Y\to X\times\square^{n}
is an admissible blow-up, then the morphism

Y-\partial Y\to(X-\partial X)\times\mathbb{A}^{n}

is an isomorphism of schemes.
Hence we have

(6.3.3)

\mathop{\rm colim}_{Y\in\mathbf{Adm}\downarrow X\times\square^{n}}\mathcal{F}(Y-\partial Y)\cong\mathcal{F}((X-\partial X)\times\mathbb{A}^{n})\cong\operatorname{Sing}_{n}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\mathcal{F}(X-\partial X)=\omega^{*}\operatorname{Sing}_{n}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\mathcal{F}(X).

Combine (
6.3.2
) and (
6.3.3
) to have an isomorphism of presheaves

\operatorname{Sing}_{n}^{\square\cup\mathbf{Adm}}\omega^{*}\mathcal{F}\cong\omega^{*}\operatorname{Sing}_{n}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\mathcal{F}

To conclude,
observe that the degeneracy and face maps for
\operatorname{Sing}_{n}^{\square\cup\mathbf{Adm}}\omega^{*}\mathcal{F}
and
\omega^{*}\operatorname{Sing}_{n}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\mathcal{F}

are compatible with this isomorphism.
∎

6.4.
Infinite Grassmannians and
\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}

In the following,
we review how to obtain the infinite dimensional Grassmannians from the finite dimensional ones.

Definition 6.4.1
.

Fix integers
0<r<n
. Let
{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)
denote the
(r,n)
Grassmannian, that is, the scheme representing the functor
S\mapsto{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)(S)
, where
{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)(S)
is the set of isomorphism classes of surjections
\mathcal{O}^{\oplus n}_{S}\to\mathcal{Q}
, where
\mathcal{Q}
is a locally free
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module of rank
n-r
, see
[
93
,
Tag 089R
]
.

We equip
{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)
with the trivial log structure.
In particular, for every log scheme
X
, there is a bijection

\operatorname{Hom}(X,{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n))\cong\operatorname{Hom}(\underline{X},{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)).

There are canonical morphisms

{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)\to{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n+1)\text{ and }{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)\to{\mathrm{Gr}}(r+1,n+1)

taking
V\subset\mathcal{O}^{n}
to
V\oplus\{0\}\subset\mathcal{O}^{n}\oplus\mathcal{O}
and
V\oplus\mathcal{O}\subset\mathcal{O}^{n}\oplus\mathcal{O}
,
where
\mathcal{O}
denotes the standard linear space of dimension
1
.
From these,
we can form colimits

{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,\infty):=\mathop{\rm colim}_{n\geq r}{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)\text{ and }{\mathrm{Gr}}:=\mathop{\rm colim}_{r}{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,\infty).

Observe that
{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,\infty)
and
{\mathrm{Gr}}
are filtered colimits of smooth schemes.
There is a natural morphism

{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)\times{\mathrm{Gr}}(r^{\prime},n^{\prime})\to{\mathrm{Gr}}(r+r^{\prime},n+n^{\prime})

sending
(V,W)
to
V\oplus W
.
This gives a (non-commutative) monoid structure on the coproduct
\coprod_{r=1}^{\infty}{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,\infty)

in the category of log schemes.

Proposition 6.4.2
.

If
X
is a regular affine noetherian scheme, then the canonical morphism

(6.4.1)

\operatorname{Sing}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}})(X)\to\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}(X)

is an equivalence of Kan complexes.
In particular, if
Y
is a regular noetherian scheme, then the canonical morphism

(6.4.2)

L_{Nis}\operatorname{Sing}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}})(Y)\to\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}(Y)

is an equivalence of Kan complexes.

Proof.

For (
6.4.1
), see the proof of
[
70
, Proposition 3.16, p. 141]
.
This implies that (
6.4.2
) is a
weak equivalence since
\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}
satisfies Nisnevich descent.
∎

Proposition 6.4.3
.

Suppose
S
is a regular noetherian scheme.
Then for all
X\in\mathbf{cSm}/S
, there is a canonical equivalence of spaces

\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}(X-\partial X)\simeq\operatorname{Map}_{\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\ast}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(S)}(X_{+},\omega^{*}\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}).

Proof.

For every
X\in\mathbf{cSm}/S
, we have an equivalence

\omega^{*}\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}(X)\simeq\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}(X-\partial X).

The
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance of
K
-theory implies that
\omega^{*}\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}
is
\square
-local.
If
Y\to X
is an admissible blow-up, then
Y-\partial Y\to X-\partial X
is an isomorphism of schemes.
It follows that
\omega^{*}\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}
is
\mathbf{Adm}
-local.
Furthermore,
together with Lemma
2.3.23
,
we deduce that
\omega^{*}\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}
is strict Nisnevich local since
\mathrm{K}
is Nisnevich local.
In conclusion,
\omega^{*}\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}
is
(sNis,\square\cup\mathbf{Adm})
-local.
This gives the desired equivalence.
∎

The following is the most important result in this subsection,
which shows that the unstable
K
-theory spectrum
\omega^{*}\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}
admits a geometric model
in our logarithmic setting.

Theorem 6.4.4
.

Suppose
S
is a regular scheme.
Then there is a canonical equivalence

(6.4.3)

\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}}\to\omega^{*}\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}

in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\ast}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(S)
.

Proof.

Due to Proposition
6.3.3
, there is a
\square\cup\mathbf{Adm}
-local equivalence

(6.4.4)

\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}}\simeq\omega^{*}(\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}}).

Apply Proposition
6.3.2
to have a
\square\cup\mathbf{Adm}
-local equivalence

(6.4.5)

\omega^{*}(\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}})\simeq\operatorname{Sing}^{\square\cup\mathbf{Adm}}\omega^{*}(\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}}).

Furthermore,
Lemma
6.3.4
gives an equivalence

(6.4.6)

\operatorname{Sing}^{\square\cup\mathbf{Adm}}\omega^{*}(\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}})\cong\omega^{*}\operatorname{Sing}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}}).

Next we combine (
6.4.4
), (
6.4.5
), and (
6.4.6
)
to deduce the
(sNis,\square\cup\mathbf{Adm})
-local equivalence

(6.4.7)

\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}}\simeq L_{sNis}\omega^{*}\operatorname{Sing}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}}).

There is a canonical morphism of presheaves

(6.4.8)

\omega^{*}\operatorname{Sing}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}})\to\omega^{*}L_{Nis}\operatorname{Sing}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}}).

If
x
is a point of
X\in\mathbf{cSm}/S
,
then there exists an open affine neighborhood
U
of
x
in
X
such that the restriction
\partial U

of the Cartier divisor
\partial X
to
U
is principal.
We note that
U-\partial U
is affine.
By Proposition
6.4.2
, the morphism of Kan complexes

\omega^{*}\operatorname{Sing}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}})(U)\to\omega^{*}L_{Nis}\operatorname{Sing}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}})(U)

obtained from (
6.4.8
) becomes
\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}(U-\partial U)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}(U-\partial U)
.
This means that
L_{sNis}
turns (
6.4.8
) into a pointwise weak equivalence.
Together with (
6.4.2
) and (
6.4.7
), we obtain the desired equivalence.
∎

6.5.
The logarithmic
K
-theory spectrum

Next we explain how to construct a
(2,1)
-periodic log motivic spectrum from a
pointed log space satisfying the following condition.

Definition 6.5.1
.

A pointed log motivic space
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\ast}(S)
satisfies
the
loops
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle formula

if there exists an equivalence

\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\colon\mathcal{F}\to\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\mathcal{F}

in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\ast}(S)
.

Construction 6.5.2
.

If a pointed log motivic space
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\ast}(S)
satisfies the loops
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle formula,
then by adjunction we have a morphism

\beta_{\mathcal{F}}\colon\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{F}.

We use
\beta_{\mathcal{F}}
as the bonding maps in the log
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-spectrum

\mathrm{Bott}(\mathcal{F}):=(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F},\ldots)\in\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(S).

This is called
the logarithmic motivic Bott spectrum of

\mathcal{F}
.
By construction,
there are canonical equivalences in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(S)

(6.5.1)

\mathrm{Bott}(\mathcal{F})\simeq\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\mathrm{Bott}(\mathcal{F})

and

(6.5.2)

\Omega_{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}}^{\infty}\mathrm{Bott}(\mathcal{F})\simeq(\mathcal{F},\Omega_{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}}\mathcal{F},\Omega_{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}}^{2}\mathcal{F},\ldots).

The bonding maps in (
6.5.2
) are induced by
\mathcal{F}\to\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\mathcal{F}\simeq\Omega_{S^{1}}\Omega_{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}}\mathcal{F}
.

Construction 6.5.3
.

If a pointed log motivic space
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\ast}(S)
satisfies the loops
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle formula,
then we can view
\mathrm{Bott}(\mathcal{F})
as a
(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{\wedge 2}
-spectrum

(6.5.3)

(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F},\ldots)

with bonding maps given by the composite
\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{2}\mathcal{F}\to\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{F}
.
We can also view
\mathrm{Bott}(\mathcal{F})\wedge\mathrm{Bott}(\mathcal{F})
as a
(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{\wedge 2}
-spectrum

(6.5.4)

(\mathcal{F}\wedge\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}\wedge\mathcal{F},\ldots),

where the bonding maps are given by

\mathcal{F}\wedge\mathcal{F}\wedge\mathbb{P}^{1}\wedge\mathbb{P}^{1}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathcal{F}\wedge\mathbb{P}^{1}\wedge\mathcal{F}\wedge\mathbb{P}^{1}\xrightarrow{\beta_{\mathcal{F}}\wedge\beta_{\mathcal{F}}}\mathcal{F}\wedge\mathcal{F}.

Suppose
\mathcal{F}
is a homotopy commutative monoid in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\ast}(S)
with multiplication

\mu\colon\mathcal{F}\wedge\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{F}
such that the diagram

(6.5.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\wedge\mathcal{F}\wedge\mathbb{P}^{1}\wedge\mathbb{P}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\wedge\mathbb{P}^{1}\wedge\mathcal{F}\wedge\mathbb{P}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\wedge\mathcal{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\wedge\mathbb{P}^{1}\wedge\mathbb{P}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{\mathcal{F}}\wedge\beta_{\mathcal{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{\mathcal{F}}^{2}}

commutes.
Then, since the bonding maps are compatible,
\mu
induces a map

\mathrm{Bott}(\mathcal{F})\wedge\mathrm{Bott}(\mathcal{F})\to\mathrm{Bott}(\mathcal{F})

Furthermore,
the assumptions on
\mathcal{F}
ensures
\mathrm{Bott}(\mathcal{F})
is a homotopy commutative monoid in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(S)
.

The composite of the two maps in the upper horizontal row of (
6.5.5
) is
equivalent to the composite

(6.5.6)

\mathcal{F}\wedge\mathcal{F}\wedge\mathbb{P}^{1}\wedge\mathbb{P}^{1}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\wedge\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}}\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\mathcal{F}\wedge\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\mathcal{F}\wedge\mathbb{P}^{1}\wedge\mathbb{P}^{1}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\mathcal{F}\wedge\mathbb{P}^{1}\wedge\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\mathcal{F}\wedge\mathbb{P}^{1}\xrightarrow{\gamma\wedge\gamma}\mathcal{F}\wedge\mathcal{F},

where
\gamma\colon\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\mathcal{F}\wedge\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathcal{F}
is the canonical map.
The adjoint of the composite of the two right-most morphisms in (
6.5.6
) takes the form

\nu\colon\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\mathcal{F}\wedge\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\mathcal{F}\to\Omega_{(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{\wedge 2}}(\mathcal{F}\wedge\mathcal{F}).

To check that (
6.5.5
) commutes, it suffices to check that the diagram

(6.5.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\wedge\mathcal{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\mathcal{F}\wedge\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\mathcal{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{\wedge 2}}(\mathcal{F}\wedge\mathcal{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{\wedge 2}}(\mathcal{F})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}\wedge\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\mathcal{F}}^{2}}

commutes by adjunction.

Remark 6.5.4
.

The above discussions on the loops
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle formula and logarithmic motivic Bott spectrum can be adapted in the setting of
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\ast}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(S)
and
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(S)
.

Proposition 6.5.5
.

Suppose
S
is a regular scheme.
Then
\omega^{*}\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}\in\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\ast}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(S)
satisfies the loops
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle formula.
A similar result holds for
\omega^{*}\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}\in\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\ast}(S)
too.

Proof.

We focus on the case of
\omega^{*}\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}\in\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\ast}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(S)
since the proofs are similar.
By the projective bundle formula for
K
-theory of schemes, we have an equivalence of presheaves of spaces

(6.5.8)

\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}\simeq\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}.

For every
X\in\mathbf{lSm}/S
, we have a canonical isomorphism of schemes

(X\times\mathbb{P}^{1})-\partial(X\times\mathbb{P}^{1})\cong(X-\partial X)\times\mathbb{P}^{1}.

This implies that there is a natural equivalence

(6.5.9)

\omega^{*}\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\simeq\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\omega^{*}.

Apply
\omega^{*}
to (
6.5.8
), and use (
6.5.9
) to obtain the desired equivalence.
∎

Definition 6.5.6
.

Let
S
be a regular scheme.
Proposition
6.5.5
shows that

\omega^{*}\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}\in\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\ast}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(S)
satisfies the loops
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle formula.
With reference to Construction
6.5.2
,
we define the
logarithmic
K
-theory spectrum of
S
to be the
Bott periodic
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-spectrum

(6.5.10)

\mathbf{logKGL}:=\mathrm{Bott}(\omega^{*}\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K})=(\omega^{*}\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K},\omega^{*}\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K},\ldots)\in\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(S).

By Theorem
6.4.4
,
we have an equivalence in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(S)

(6.5.11)

\mathbf{logKGL}\simeq(\mathbb{L}_{sNis,\square\cup\mathbf{Adm}}\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}},\mathbb{L}_{sNis,\square\cup\mathbf{Adm}}\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}},\ldots).

We also have the logarithmic
K
-theory spectrum

(6.5.12)

\mathbf{logKGL}:=\mathrm{Bott}(\omega^{*}\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K})\in\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(S).

Using the functor
\psi^{*}\colon\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(S)\to\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(S)
in
4.0.12
,
we can relate (
6.5.10
) and (
6.5.12
) by the equivalence

(6.5.13)

\psi^{*}\mathbf{logKGL}\simeq\mathbf{logKGL}

since the triangle (
4.0.8
) commutes.

Theorem 6.5.7
.

Suppose
S
is a regular scheme.
For all
X\in\mathbf{cSm}/S
and integer
d\in\mathbb{Z}
, there are canonical equivalences of spectra

(6.5.14)

\mathrm{K}(X-\partial X)\simeq\operatorname{map}_{\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(S)}(\Sigma_{S^{1}}^{\infty}X_{+},\Omega_{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}}^{\infty}\mathbf{logKGL})

and

(6.5.15)

\mathrm{K}(X-\partial X)\simeq\operatorname{map}_{\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(S)}(\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{d}\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{\infty}X_{+},\mathbf{logKGL}).

Similar results hold for
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}(S)
and
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(S)
too.

Proof.

We focus on the cases of
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(S)
and
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(S)
since the proofs are similar.
Due to (
6.5.1
), we may assume
d=0
.
Then, by adjunction, (
6.5.15
) is a consequence of (
6.5.14
).

By (
6.5.2
), there is an equivalence in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(S)

(6.5.16)

\Omega_{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}}^{\infty}\mathbf{logKGL}\simeq(\omega^{*}\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K},\Omega_{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}}\omega^{*}\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K},\ldots).

This gives an equivalence of spectra

(6.5.17)

\begin{split}&\operatorname{map}_{\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(k)}(\Sigma_{S^{1}}^{\infty}X_{+},\Omega_{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}}^{\infty}\mathbf{logKGL})\\
\simeq&(\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}(X-\partial X),\Omega_{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}(X-\partial X),\ldots).\end{split}

Compare with (
6.1.3
) to conclude.
∎

Proposition 6.5.8
.

The motivic space
\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}}
is a homotopy commutative monoid in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\ast}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(S)
. If
S
is a regular scheme,

\mathbf{logKGL}
is a homotopy commutative monoid in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(S)
.

Proof.

The morphism
{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)\times{\mathrm{Gr}}(s,l)\to{\mathrm{Gr}}(r+s,n+l)
sending any pair
(V\in\mathcal{O}^{n},W\in\mathcal{O}^{l})
to
V\oplus W
naturally induces a morphism
\mu\colon(\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}})\wedge(\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}})\to\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}}
.
Together with Theorem
6.4.4
, we have a map
\mu^{\prime}\colon(\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}})\wedge(\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}})\to\omega^{*}\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}
.
The square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}})\wedge(\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}})\wedge(\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}})\wedge(\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}})\wedge(\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega^{*}\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu\wedge\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}\wedge\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

commutes since the corresponding square removing
\omega^{*}
in the lower right corner commutes in
\mathcal{H}_{\ast}(k)
.
This shows that
\mu
satisfies the associative axiom.
One can similarly show that
\mu
satisfies the unital and commutative axioms, and hence
\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}}
is a homotopy commutative monoid in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{H}_{\ast}^{\mathbf{Adm}}(S)
.

It remains to check that (
6.5.5
) commutes for
\omega^{*}\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}
.
In other words, we need to show that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}})\wedge(\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}})\wedge\mathbb{P}^{1}\wedge\mathbb{P}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}})\wedge\mathbb{P}^{1}\wedge(\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}})\wedge\mathbb{P}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}})\wedge(\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathrm{Gr}})\wedge\mathbb{P}^{1}\wedge\mathbb{P}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega^{*}\Omega_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{K}}

commutes.
This follows from the commutativity of the corresponding square in
\mathcal{H}_{\ast}(k)
, where
\omega^{*}
in the lower right corner is removed.
∎

Definition 6.5.9
.

Suppose
S
is a regular scheme and
f\colon X\to S
is a morphism in
\mathbf{lSch}
.
The
logarithmic
K
-theory of
S

is the mapping spectrum

\mathrm{logK}(X):=\operatorname{map}_{\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(X)}(\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{\infty}X_{+},f^{*}\mathbf{logKGL}).

For every integer
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we set

\mathrm{logK}_{n}(X):=\pi_{n}\mathrm{logK}(X).

If
f
is log smooth,
then we have equivalences of spectra

(6.5.18)

\mathrm{logK}(X)\simeq\operatorname{map}_{\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(S)}(\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{\infty}X_{+},\mathbf{logKGL})\simeq\mathrm{K}(X-\partial X),

where the right equivalence is due to
Theorem
6.5.7
.
In particular, if
S\in\mathbf{Sm}/k
, then we have an equivalence of spectra

\mathrm{logK}(S)\simeq\mathrm{K}(S).

One may ask if this is true in a more general situation.

Conjecture 6.5.10
.

For every
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
there exists an equivalence of spectra

\mathrm{logK}(S)\simeq\mathrm{K}(S).

Remark 6.5.11
.

There exist examples of non-regular schemes
S
and
X\in\mathbf{lSm}/S
such that

\mathrm{logK}(X)\not\simeq\mathrm{K}(X-\partial X).

Indeed,
the left-hand side is
\square
-invariant, but the right-hand side is not.

Remark 6.5.12
.

Niziol defined
K
-groups
\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{Niz}}_{n}(X_{\tau})
for various topologies
\tau

and fs log schemes
X
in
[
77
]
.
Some of these
K
-groups are computed when
X
is regular and
\partial X

is a strict normal crossing divisor in
[
77
, Corollary 4.17]
.
In particular, if
k
is a field,
then for every integer
n\geq 0
there are isomorphisms of abelian groups

\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{Niz}}_{n}(\square_{k{\acute{e}t}})\cong\mathrm{K}_{n}(k)\otimes((\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})^{\prime}-0)\text{ and }\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{Niz}}_{n}(\square_{kfl})\cong\mathrm{K}_{n}(k)\otimes(\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}-0),

where,
if
k
has characteristic
p
, we set

(\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})^{\prime}:=\oplus_{\ell\neq p}\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}/\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}.

Hence the groups
\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{Niz}}_{n}(\square_{k{\acute{e}t}})
and
\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{Niz}}_{n}(\square_{kfl})
are non-isomorphic to
\mathrm{K}_{n}(k)
.

On the other hand,
suppose
X
is an fs log scheme log smooth over a regular scheme.
Due to (
6.5.18
) and
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance of
K
-theory for regular schemes,
there are isomorphisms of abelian groups

\mathrm{logK}_{n}(X\times\square)\cong\mathrm{K}_{n}(X\times\mathbb{A}^{1})\cong\mathrm{K}_{n}(X).

Hence our logarithmic
K
-group
\mathrm{K}_{n}^{log}(X)
is non-isomorphic to Niziol’s
K
-groups

\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{Niz}}_{n}(X_{k{\acute{e}t}})
and
\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{Niz}}_{n}(X_{kfl})
.

7.
Oriented cohomology theories

Orientations in
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy theory can be used to give a systematic proof of the
projective bundle formula for
various cohomology theories including homotopy algebraic
K
-theory and motivic cohomology.
Furthermore, one can associate Chern and Thom classes as in the classical intersection theory.
We refer to
[
97
]
,
[
80
]
, and
[
32
]
for these developments.

The purpose of this section is to prove various analogous results in our logarithmic setting, without
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance. As discussed in the introduction, the scope of applications of these results goes beyond the logarithmic world, since it gives automatically results for virtually all known non-
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy invariant cohomology theories (for example: crystalline, Hodge, prismatic and syntomic cohomology). In presence of an orientation, the Thom space term in the Gysin sequence of Remark
3.2.22
can be trivialized in light of the Thom isomorphism theorem
7.3.8
below.

In this section, we also present a complete computation of the cohomology the Grassmannians
{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)
: our proof uses in essential way the
(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})
-invariance of any cohomology theory representable in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}
, and once again does not follow from the analogous computation in the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-setting.

We begin by defining precisely what we mean by cohomology theory associated with a homotopy commutative monoid
\mathbf{E}
.

Definition 7.0.1
.

Suppose
S\in\mathbf{lSch}
and let
\mathbf{E}
be a homotopy commutative monoid in
\mathbf{logSH}(S)
.
For integers
p
and
q
,
we define the
\mathbf{E}
-cohomology

of
X\in\mathbf{lSm}/S
by

(7.0.1)

\mathbf{E}^{p,q}(X):=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{logSH}(S)}(\Sigma_{T}^{\infty}X_{+},\Sigma^{p,q}\mathbf{E}).

For any morphism
Y\to X
in
\mathbf{lSm}/S
, we define

\mathbf{E}^{p,q}(X/Y):=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{logSH}(S)}(\Sigma_{T}^{\infty}X/Y,\Sigma^{p,q}\mathbf{E}).

We set
\mathbf{E}^{p,q}:=\mathbf{E}^{p,q}(\operatorname{pt})
and
\mathbf{E}^{**}(X):=\bigoplus_{p,q\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbf{E}^{p,q}(X)
for abbreviation.

Remark 7.0.2
.

For
X,X^{\prime}\in\mathbf{lSm}/S
and integers
p
,
q
,
r
, and
s
, any two maps
u\colon\Sigma_{T}^{\infty}X_{+}\to\Sigma^{p,q}\mathbf{E}
and
v\colon\Sigma_{T}^{\infty}X_{+}^{\prime}\to\Sigma^{r,s}\mathbf{E}

give

\Sigma_{T}^{\infty}(X\times X^{\prime})_{+}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\Sigma_{T}^{\infty}X_{+}\wedge\Sigma_{T}^{\infty}X_{+}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{u\wedge v}\Sigma^{p,q}\mathbf{E}\wedge\Sigma^{r,s}\mathbf{E}\to\Sigma^{p+r,q+s}\mathbf{E}.

From this, we have a homomorphism

(7.0.2)

\mathbf{E}^{p,q}(X)\times\mathbf{E}^{r,s}(X^{\prime})\to\mathbf{E}^{p+r,q+s}(X\times X^{\prime}).

When a morphism
\alpha\colon X^{\prime}\to X\times X^{\prime}
is given, compose (
7.0.2
) with
\alpha^{*}
to obtain the
cup product

(7.0.3)

\smile\colon\mathbf{E}^{p,q}(X)\times\mathbf{E}^{r,s}(X^{\prime})\to\mathbf{E}^{p+r,q+s}(X^{\prime}).

We are particularly interested in the case when
X=X^{\prime}
and
\alpha
is the diagonal morphism.
We similarly have the cup product for
\mathbf{E}^{**}(X/Y)
for every morphism
Y\to X
in
\mathbf{lSm}/S
by considering the diagonal map
X/Y\to X/Y\wedge X/Y
.

This cup product satisfies the axioms of rings since
\mathbf{E}
satisfies similar axioms, so
\mathbf{E}^{**}(X)
and
\mathbf{E}^{**}(X/Y)
are graded rings.
Let
\mathbf{1}
denote the unit in these rings.
Since (
7.0.2
) is functorial in
X
, there is a naturally induced graded ring homomorphism

f^{*}\colon\mathbf{E}^{**}(X)\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(Y)

for every morphism
f\colon Y\to X
in
\mathbf{logH}(S)
.
We have a similar homomorphism for a map
X^{\prime}/Y^{\prime}\to X/Y
in
\mathbf{logH}_{\ast}(S)
.

7.1.
Orientations

Throughout this subsection, we fix
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
and a homotopy commutative monoid
\mathbf{E}
in
\mathbf{logSH}(S)
.

Definition 7.1.1
.

There are closed immersions

\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}\to\cdots\to\mathbb{P}^{n}\to\cdots,

where
\mathbb{P}^{n}\to\mathbb{P}^{n+1}
maps
[x_{0}:\cdots:x_{n}]
to
[x_{0}:\cdots:x_{n}:0]
.
Hence we can regard
\mathbb{P}^{1}
as a closed subscheme of
\mathbb{P}^{\infty}
.
Furthermore, we can let the distinguished points of
\mathbb{P}^{n}
and
\mathbb{P}^{\infty}
be
1\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
A
Chern orientation
(or simply
orientation
) of
\mathbf{E}
is a class
c_{\infty}\in\mathbf{E}^{2,1}(\mathbb{P}^{\infty}/\operatorname{pt})
whose restriction to
\mathbb{P}^{1}/\operatorname{pt}
is the class of
\mathbf{E}^{2,1}(\mathbb{P}^{1}/\operatorname{pt})
given by

(\mathbb{P}^{1}/\operatorname{pt})\wedge\mathbf{1}\colon\Sigma_{T}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}^{1}/\operatorname{pt}\to(\mathbb{P}^{1}/\operatorname{pt})\wedge\mathbf{E}=\Sigma^{2,1}\mathbf{E}.

For abbreviation, we set
\Sigma^{2,1}\mathbf{1}:=(\mathbb{P}^{1}/\operatorname{pt})\wedge\mathbf{1}
.

Remark 7.1.2
.

One may also define a Chern orientation of
\mathbf{E}
as a class
c_{\infty}
whose restriction to
\mathbb{P}^{1}/\operatorname{pt}
is
-\Sigma^{2,1}\mathbf{1}
, see
[
80
, Definition 1.2]
for example.
There is a one-to-one correspondence between this definition and ours, which sends
c_{\infty}
to
-c_{\infty}
.

Definition 7.1.3
.

A Chern orientation
c_{\infty}
can be expressed as a morphism
\Sigma_{T}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}^{\infty}/\operatorname{pt}\to\Sigma^{2,1}\mathbf{E}
in
\mathbf{logSH}(S)
.
Hence for every
X\in\mathbf{lSm}/S
, there are canonical maps

(7.1.1)

\begin{split}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{logSH}_{S^{1}}(S)}(\Sigma_{S^{1}}^{\infty}X_{+},\Sigma_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}^{\infty}/\operatorname{pt})&\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{logSH}(S)}(\Sigma_{T}^{\infty}X_{+},\Sigma_{T}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}^{\infty}/\operatorname{pt})\\
&\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{logSH}(S)}(\Sigma_{T}^{\infty}X_{+},\Sigma^{2,1}\mathbf{E})=\mathbf{E}^{2,1}(X).\end{split}

We have maps

(7.1.2)

\begin{split}\operatorname{logPic}(X)=H_{sNis}^{1}(X,\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}})\to&\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{logSH}_{S^{1}}(S)}(\Sigma_{S^{1}}^{\infty}X_{+},\Sigma_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{B}\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{log}}/\operatorname{pt})\\
\cong&\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{logSH}_{S^{1}}(S)}(\Sigma_{S^{1}}^{\infty}X_{+},\Sigma_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}^{\infty}/\operatorname{pt}),\end{split}

where the last isomorphism is due to Theorem
5.2.10
.
Combine with (
7.1.1
) to have a canonical map

c_{1}\colon\operatorname{logPic}(X)\to\mathbf{E}^{2,1}(X),

which we call the
first Chern class
.
There is a canonical homomorphism
\operatorname{Pic}(\underline{X})=\operatorname{logPic}(\underline{X})\to\operatorname{logPic}(X)
.
By composition we obtain the canonical map

c_{1}\colon\operatorname{Pic}(\underline{X})\to\mathbf{E}^{2,1}(X).

In particular, since every line bundle
\mathcal{L}
determines a class of
\operatorname{Pic}(\underline{X})
, we can define
c_{1}(\mathcal{L})\in\mathbf{E}^{2,1}(X)
.

The map (
7.1.2
) sends the class
\mathcal{O}(-1)\in\operatorname{Pic}(\mathbb{P}^{1})
to the class

\alpha\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{logSH}_{S^{1}}(S)}(\Sigma_{S^{1}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{P}^{1})_{+},\Sigma_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}^{\infty}/\operatorname{pt})

induced by the inclusion
\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{\infty}
.
The map (
7.1.1
) sends the class
\beta
to the restriction of
c_{\infty}
to
\mathbb{P}^{1}/\operatorname{pt}
.
Hence we have the formula

(7.1.3)

c_{1}(\mathcal{O}(-1))=\Sigma^{2,1}\mathbf{1}.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{1}
be the tautological bundle on
\mathbb{P}^{\infty}
.
The map (
7.1.2
) sends the class
\mathcal{T}_{1}\in\operatorname{Pic}(\mathbb{P}^{\infty})
to the class

\beta\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{logSH}_{S^{1}}(S)}(\Sigma_{S^{1}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{P}^{\infty})_{+},\Sigma_{S^{1}}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}^{\infty}/\operatorname{pt})

induced by the quotient map
(\mathbb{P}^{\infty})_{+}\to\mathbb{P}^{\infty}/\operatorname{pt}
.
The map (
7.1.1
) sends the class
\alpha
to
c_{\infty}
.
Hence we have the formula

(7.1.4)

c_{1}(\mathcal{T}_{1})=c_{\infty}.

If
f\colon Y\to X
is a morphism in
\mathbf{lSm}/S
, then there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{logPic}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{2,1}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{logPic}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{2,1}(Y).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}

In particular, for every line bundle
\mathcal{L}
over
X
, we have the formula

(7.1.5)

f^{*}(c_{1}(\mathcal{L}))=c_{1}(f^{*}(\mathcal{L})).

Lemma 7.1.4
.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{1,n}
be the tautological bundle on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
.
Then
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}_{1,n}\oplus\mathcal{O})
is isomorphic to the blow-up of
\mathbb{P}^{n+1}
along a point, and
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}_{1,n})
is the exceptional divisor on
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}_{1,n}\oplus\mathcal{O})
.

Proof.

The scheme
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}_{1,n}\oplus\mathcal{O})
admits a Zariski cover consisting of the spectra of

\mathbb{Z}[x_{0}/x_{i},\ldots,x_{n}/x_{i},x_{i}]\text{ and }\mathbb{Z}[x_{0}/x_{i},\ldots,x_{n}/x_{i},1/x_{i}]

for
0\leq i\leq n
.
The divisor
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}_{1,n})
is given locally by the divisor
x_{0}/x_{i}=0
for
1\leq i\leq n
.
Let
O
be the point
[0:\cdots:0:1]
in
\mathbb{P}^{n+1}
.
The scheme
{\mathrm{Bl}}_{O}\mathbb{P}^{n+1}
admits a Zariski cover consisting of the spectra of

\mathbb{Z}[x_{1}/x_{i},\ldots,x_{n+1}/x_{i},x_{i}]\text{ and }\mathbb{Z}[x_{1}/x_{i},\ldots,x_{n+1}/x_{i},1/x_{i}]

for
1\leq i\leq n+1
.
By identifying
x_{n+1}
with
x_{0}
, we have the required claim.
∎

Lemma 7.1.5
.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{1}
be the tautological bundle on
\mathbb{P}^{\infty}
, and let
z\colon\mathbb{P}^{\infty}\to\mathcal{T}_{1}
be the zero section.
Then there is an exact sequence

(7.1.6)

0\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(\operatorname{pt})\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}^{\infty})\xrightarrow{z^{*}}\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{T}_{1}))\to 0.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{1,n}
be the tautological bundle on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
.
From Theorem
3.3.5
and Lemma
7.1.4
, we have an isomorphism

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}_{1,n}\oplus\mathcal{O})/\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}_{1,n})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbb{P}^{n+1}/\operatorname{pt}

in
\mathbf{logSH}(S)
.
If we compose with the zero section
(\mathbb{P}^{n})_{+}\to\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}_{1,n}\oplus\mathcal{O})/\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}_{1,n})
, then
(\mathbb{P}^{n})_{+}\to\mathbb{P}^{n+1}/\operatorname{pt}
factors through
(\mathbb{P}^{n+1})_{+}
.
Take colimits to have morphisms

(\mathbb{P}^{\infty})_{+}\to\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{O})/\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}_{1})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbb{P}^{\infty}/\operatorname{pt}

whose composite morphism is equal to the quotient map in
\mathbf{logSH}(S)
.
Proposition
3.2.19
finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma 7.1.6
.

Let
\overline{i}_{1},\ldots,\overline{i}_{n}\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{n}
be the closed immersions sending
[a_{0}:a_{1}]
to
[a_{0}:0:\cdots:0:a_{1}]
,
\ldots
,
[0:\cdots:0:a_{0}:a_{1}]
.
Then
\overline{i}_{1},\ldots,\overline{i}_{n}
are all equal in
\mathbf{logSH}_{S^{1}}(S)
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality it suffices to show that
\overline{i}_{1}
and
\overline{i}_{2}
are equal in
\mathbf{logSH}_{S^{1}}(S)
.
Both
\overline{i}_{1}
and
\overline{i}_{2}
factors through the closed immersion
\mathbb{P}^{2}\to\mathbb{P}^{n}
sending
[a_{0}:a_{1}:a_{2}]
to
[a_{0}:a_{1}:0:\cdots:0:a_{2}]
, so we only need to consider the case
n=2
.
Let
\overline{i}\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the closed immersion sending
[a_{0}:a_{1}]
to
[a_{0}:a_{0}:a_{1}]
.
We will show that
\overline{i}_{1}
and
\overline{i}
are equal in
\mathbf{logSH}_{S^{1}}(S)
.

There is a morphism

h\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}

sending
([a_{0}:a_{1}],t)
to
[a_{0}:ta_{0}:a_{1}]
.
This naturally induces a ring homomorphism

u\colon\mathbb{Z}(x,y)\to\mathbb{Z}(x,t)

sending
x
to
x
and
y
to
xt
.
The scheme
\mathbb{P}^{2}
has a Zariski cover consisting of the spectra of

A_{0}:=\mathbb{Z}[x,y],\;A_{1}:=\mathbb{Z}[1/x,y/x],\;A_{2}:=\mathbb{Z}[1/y,x/y].

The blow-up
{\mathrm{Bl}}_{0\times\infty}(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})
has a Zariski cover consisting of the spectra of

\displaystyle B_{1}:=\mathbb{Z}[x,t],\;B_{2}:=\mathbb{Z}[1/x,t],\;B_{3}:=\mathbb{Z}[xt,1/t],
