\displaystyle B_{4}:=\mathbb{Z}[x,1/(xt)],\;B_{5}:=\mathbb{Z}[1/x,1/t].

We have inclusions

u(A_{0})\subset B_{1},\;u(A_{1})\subset B_{2},\;u(A_{0})\subset B_{3},\;u(A_{2})\subset B_{4},\;u(A_{2})\subset B_{5}.

From the above, we deduce that
h
extends to a morphism

g\colon{\mathrm{Bl}}_{0\times\infty}(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})\to\mathbb{P}^{2}.

Let
X
be the fs log scheme whose underlying scheme is
{\mathrm{Bl}}_{0\times\infty}(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})
and whose log structure is the compactifying log structure associated with the open immersion
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\to{\mathrm{Bl}}_{0\times\infty}(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})
.
From
g
and
h
, we can form a morphism

f\colon X\to\mathbb{P}^{2}.

There is also an admissible blow-up
p\colon X\to\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\square
along a smooth center.
The zero section and one section
\mathbb{P}^{1}\rightrightarrows\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\square
can be lifted to closed immersions
s_{0},s_{1}\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\rightrightarrows X
.
From Lemma
3.2.12
, we deduce
\overline{i}_{1}=\overline{i}_{2}
in
\mathbf{logSH}_{S^{1}}(S)
.

By permuting coordinates, we can deduce
\overline{i}_{2}=\overline{i}
too.
Hence we have
\overline{i}_{1}=\overline{i}_{2}
.
∎

Lemma 7.1.7
.

We set
X:={\mathrm{Bl}}_{[0:0:1]}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
, which has a Zariski cover consisting of the spectra of

\mathbb{Z}[x/y,y],\;\mathbb{Z}[y/x,x],\;\mathbb{Z}[x/y,1/y],\;\mathbb{Z}[y/x,1/x].

Let
i_{1},i_{2}\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\rightrightarrows X
be the strict transforms of the closed immersions
\mathbb{P}^{1}\rightrightarrows\mathbb{P}^{2}
sending
[x_{0}:x_{1}]
to
[0:x_{0}:x_{1}]
and
[x_{0}:x_{1}:0]
.
Let
i_{3}\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\to X
be the closed immersion from the exceptional divisor given by the homomorphisms

\mathbb{Z}[x/y,y]\to\mathbb{Z}[t],\;x/y\mapsto t,\;y\mapsto 0

and

\mathbb{Z}[y/x,x]\to\mathbb{Z}[1/t],\;y/x\mapsto 1/t,\;x\mapsto 0.

If a class
a\in\mathbf{E}^{**}(X)
satisfies
i_{2}^{*}a=0
, then we have the formula

i_{1}^{*}a=-i_{3}^{*}a.

Proof.

There is a commutative diagram

(7.1.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pt}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{3}}

where
q
is given by the homomorphisms

\mathbb{Z}[t]\to\mathbb{Z}[x/y,y],\mathbb{Z}[x/y,1/y],\;t\mapsto x/y

and

\mathbb{Z}[1/t]\to\mathbb{Z}[y/x,x],\mathbb{Z}[y/x,1/x],\;1/t\mapsto y/x.

Let
p\colon X\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the projection.
Since
qi_{3}=\operatorname{id}
, we have
i_{3}^{*}(a-q^{*}i_{3}^{*}a)=0
.
From Proposition
3.3.5
, we have

(7.1.8)

a-q^{*}i_{3}^{*}a=p^{*}b

for some
b\in\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
.
Permute coordinates in Lemma
7.1.6
to have
i_{1}^{*}p^{*}b=i_{2}^{*}p^{*}b
.
Together with (
7.1.8
), we have the formula

(7.1.9)

i_{1}^{*}a-i_{1}^{*}q^{*}i_{3}^{*}a=i_{2}^{*}a-i_{2}^{*}q^{*}i_{3}^{*}a.

Since
i_{2}^{*}a=0
, we have
i_{1}^{*}q^{*}i_{3}^{*}a=0
from (
7.1.7
).
Combined with (
7.1.9
), we have

i_{1}^{*}a=-i_{2}^{*}q^{*}i_{3}^{*}a.

This implies the claimed formula since
qi_{2}=\operatorname{id}
.
∎

Definition 7.1.8
.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{1}
be the tautological bundle on
\mathbb{P}^{\infty}
.
A
Thom orientation
of
\mathbf{E}
is a class
t_{\infty}\in\mathbf{E}^{2,1}(\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{T}_{1}))
such that the restriction of
t_{\infty}
to the distinguished point of
\mathbb{P}^{\infty}
is equal to the class of
\mathbf{E}^{2,1}(T)
given by

-T\wedge\mathbf{1}\colon T\to T\wedge\mathbf{E}=\Sigma^{2,1}\mathbf{E}.

From (
7.1.6
), we have an isomorphism of graded rings

(7.1.10)

z^{*}\colon\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}^{\infty}/\operatorname{pt})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{T}_{1})).

Proposition 7.1.9
.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{1}
be the tautological bundle on
\mathbb{P}^{\infty}
.
If
t_{\infty}\in\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{T}_{1}))
is a class, then
t_{\infty}
is a Thom orientation of
\mathbf{E}
if and only if
z^{*}t_{\infty}\in\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}^{\infty}/\operatorname{pt})
is a Chern orientation of
\mathbf{E}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{1,1}
be the tautological bundle on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
There is a commutative diagram with cartesian squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pt}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}_{1,1}\oplus\mathcal{O})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{O})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pt}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{\infty},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}}

where
i_{0}
is the zero section, and
p
and
q
are the projections.
The restriction of
t_{\infty}
to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
gives a class
t_{1}\in\mathbf{E}^{2,1}(\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{T}_{1,1}))
.
Let
\overline{t}_{1}
be the image of
t_{1}
in
\mathbf{E}^{2,1}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}_{1,1}\oplus\mathcal{O}))
, whose restriction to
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}_{1,1})
is zero.
The blow-down of
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}_{1,1}\oplus\mathcal{O})
along
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}_{1,1})
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{2}
due to Lemma
7.1.4
.
Hence we can apply Lemma
7.1.7
to our situation, and deduce the formula

(7.1.11)

u^{*}\overline{t}_{1}=-v^{*}\overline{t}_{1}.

The class
t_{\infty}
is a Thom orientation if and only if the restriction of
-v^{*}\overline{t}_{1}
to
\mathbb{P}^{1}/\operatorname{pt}
is equal to
\mathbb{P}^{1}/\operatorname{pt}\wedge\mathbf{1}
.
The class
z^{*}t_{\infty}
is a Chern orientation if and only if the restriction of
u^{*}\overline{t}_{1}
to
\mathbb{P}^{1}/\operatorname{pt}
is equal to
\mathbb{P}^{1}/\operatorname{pt}\wedge\mathbf{1}
.
From (
7.1.11
), we deduce that these are equivalent.
∎

Definition 7.1.10
.

Let
t_{\infty}
be a Thom orientation.
The above
z^{*}t_{\infty}
is called the
Chern orientation associated with
t_{\infty}
.

Proposition 7.1.11
.

There is a one-to-one correspondence between Chern orientations of
\mathbf{E}
and Thom orientations of
\mathbf{E}
.

Proof.

Combine Lemma
7.1.5
and Proposition
7.1.9
.
∎

7.2.
Cohomology of projective spaces

Throughout this subsection, we fix
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
and a homotopy commutative monoid
\mathbf{E}
in
\mathbf{logSH}(S)
.
In this subsection, we will present several results about projective spaces in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}(S)
(or for simplicity in the homotopy category
\mathbf{logSH}_{S^{1}}(S)
).
Our approach culminates with a proof of the projective bundle formula.
The following theorem is a fundamental property of our motivic category.

Proposition 7.2.1
.

In
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}(S)
, there is an equivalence

(7.2.1)

\mathbb{P}^{n}/\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\cong(\mathbb{P}^{1}/\operatorname{pt})^{\wedge n}.

Proof.

In
\mathbf{SH}_{S^{1}}(k)
, there are isomorphisms

\mathbb{P}^{n}/\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\xleftarrow{\cong}\mathbb{P}^{n}/(\mathbb{P}^{n}-O)\xrightarrow{\cong}(\mathbb{P}^{1}/\mathbb{A}^{1})^{\wedge n}\xleftarrow{\cong}(\mathbb{P}^{1}/\operatorname{pt})^{\wedge n},

where
O:=[0:\cdots:0:1]\in\mathbb{P}^{n}
.
We need to “compactify” these.
We can replace
\mathbb{A}^{1}
by
\square
and
\mathbb{P}^{n}-O
by
({\mathrm{Bl}}_{O}(\mathbb{P}^{n}),E)
, where
E
is the exceptional divisor.
Since
({\mathrm{Bl}}_{O}(\mathbb{P}^{n}),E)
is a
\square
-bundle on
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
by
[
22
, Lemma 7.1.7]
, the projection
({\mathrm{Bl}}_{O}(\mathbb{P}^{n}),E)\to\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
is an equivalence in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}(S)
.
Hence it remains to construct the second morphism in

\mathbb{P}^{n}/\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\xleftarrow{\cong}\mathbb{P}^{n}/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{O}(\mathbb{P}^{n}),E)\to(\mathbb{P}^{1}/\square)^{\wedge n}\xleftarrow{\cong}(\mathbb{P}^{1}/\operatorname{pt})^{\wedge n}

and to show that it is an equivalence in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}(S)
.

Let
\overline{H}_{i}
be the divisor
x_{i}=0
on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
for
1\leq i\leq n
,
and set

\overline{\mathcal{V}}:=\mathrm{horn}(\mathbb{P}^{n},\overline{H}_{1}+\cdots+\overline{H}_{n}).

Proposition
3.2.5
gives an equivalence

(7.2.2)

({\mathrm{Bl}}_{O}\mathbb{P}^{n},E)\xleftarrow{\simeq}\mathop{\rm colim}(\overline{\mathcal{V}})

in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}_{S^{1}}(S)
, which induces an equivalence

(7.2.3)

\mathbb{P}^{n}/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{O}\mathbb{P}^{n},E)\xleftarrow{\simeq}\mathbb{P}^{n}/\mathop{\rm colim}(\overline{\mathcal{V}})

in
\mathbf{logSH}_{S^{1}}(S)
.
From (
A.0.1
) and Proposition
A.0.8
, we have an equivalence

(7.2.4)

\mathbb{P}^{n}/\mathop{\rm colim}(\overline{\mathcal{V}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}(\mathbb{P}^{1}/\square)^{\wedge n}

in
\mathbf{logSH}_{S^{1}}(S)
.
Combine (
7.2.3
) and (
7.2.4
) to finish the construction.
∎

Morel proved the following result in
[
68
]
for
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy theory.

Lemma 7.2.2
.

There is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{n}/\operatorname{pt}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{P}^{n}/\operatorname{pt})^{\wedge n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{n}/\mathbb{P}^{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{P}^{1}/\operatorname{pt})^{\wedge n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{i}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\overline{i}_{1}}

in
\mathbf{logSH}_{S^{1}}(S)
, where the left vertical map is the obvious one,
the lower horizontal map is (
7.2.1
),
\Delta
is the diagonal morphism,
and
\overline{i}_{1}\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{n}
is the closed immersion sending
[a_{0}:a_{1}]
to
[a_{0}:0:\cdots:0:a_{1}]
.

Proof.

We keep using the same notation as in the proof of Proposition
7.2.1
.
Let
\overline{i}_{1},\ldots,\overline{i}_{n}\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{n}
be the closed immersions in Lemma
7.1.6
, which are all equal in
\mathbf{logSH}_{S^{1}}(S)
.
It suffices to show that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{n}/\operatorname{pt}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{P}^{n}/\operatorname{pt})^{\wedge n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{n}/\mathbb{P}^{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{P}^{1}/\operatorname{pt})^{\wedge n},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{i}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\overline{i}_{n}}

in
\mathbf{logSH}_{S^{1}}(S)
commutes.
For every
i
, there is an isomorphism of fs log schemes
\overline{V}_{i}:=(\mathbb{P}^{n},\overline{H}_{i})\cong(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
.
This means
\mathbb{P}^{n}/\operatorname{pt}\cong\mathbb{P}^{n}/\overline{V_{i}}
in
\mathbf{logSH}_{S^{1}}(S)
.
Hence the obvious map

(\mathbb{P}^{n}/\operatorname{pt})^{\wedge n}\to(\mathbb{P}^{n}/\overline{V}_{1})\wedge\cdots\wedge(\mathbb{P}^{n}/\overline{V}_{n})

is an isomorphism in
\mathbf{logSH}_{S^{1}}(S)
.
Moreover,
by strict Nisnevich descent, there is an isomorphism

\mathbb{P}^{1}/\square\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{N}}

in
\mathbf{logSH}_{S^{1}}(S)
.
From the proof of Proposition
7.2.1
, we see that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{n}/\operatorname{pt}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{n}/\mathbb{P}^{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{n}/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{O}(\mathbb{P}^{n}),E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{n}/\mathop{\rm colim}(\overline{\mathcal{V}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

in
\mathbf{logSH}_{S^{1}}(S)
commutes, where the maps from
\mathbb{P}^{n}/\operatorname{pt}
are the obvious ones.
Hence it suffices to show that the diagram

(7.2.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{n}/\operatorname{pt}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{P}^{n}/\overline{V}_{1})\wedge\cdots\wedge(\mathbb{P}^{n}/\overline{V}_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{n}/\mathop{\rm colim}(\overline{\mathcal{V}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{P}^{1}/\square)^{\wedge n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{N}})^{\wedge n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge i_{n}}

in
\mathbf{logSH}_{S^{1}}(S)
commutes, where
i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}
are the restrictions of
\overline{i}_{1},\ldots,\overline{i}_{n}
.

Let
q\colon\mathbb{A}^{n}\to(\mathbb{P}^{n})^{n}
be the morphism given by

(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\mapsto([a_{1}:0:\cdots:0:1],\ldots,[0:\cdots:0:a_{n}:1]).

Form the cubical horn
\mathcal{Z}
from
(\mathbb{P}^{n}/\overline{V}_{1})\wedge\cdots\wedge(\mathbb{P}^{n}/\overline{V}_{n})
using Proposition
A.0.8
.
Then there is a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{P}^{n})^{n}/\mathop{\rm colim}(\mathcal{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{P}^{n}/\overline{V}_{1})\wedge\cdots\wedge(\mathbb{P}^{n}/\overline{V}_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{n}/\mathop{\rm colim}(\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{N}})^{\wedge n}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge i_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

There is a morphism

\mathbb{A}^{n}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\to(\mathbb{P}^{n})^{n}

sending
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},t)
to

([a_{1}:ta_{2}:\ldots:ta_{n}:1],\ldots,[ta_{1}:\ldots:ta_{n-1}:a_{n}:1]).

Let
\Delta
be the fan associated with the toric variety
(\mathbb{P}^{n})^{n}
.
This morphism is a morphism associated with a morphism of fans
\mathbb{N}^{n}\times\mathbb{N}^{1}\to\Delta
whose morphism of lattices
h\colon\mathbb{Z}^{n}\times\mathbb{Z}\to(\mathbb{Z}^{n})^{r}
sends
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},t)
to

((a_{1},t+a_{2},\ldots,t+a_{n}),\ldots,(a_{1}+t,\ldots,a_{n-1}+t,a_{n})).

Let
\Theta
be the fan associated with the toric variety
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
According to toric resolution of singularities
[
31
, Theorem 11.1.9]
,
there is a morphism of fans
u\colon\Sigma\to\Delta
such that
\Sigma
is a refinement of
\mathbb{N}^{n}\times\Theta

and the morphism of lattices associated with
u
is
h
.
Hence we have a commutative diagram

(7.2.6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathbb{A}}_{\Sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{P}^{n})^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{n}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{n}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},}

where
\underline{\mathbb{A}}_{\Sigma}
denotes the toric variety associated with
\Sigma
.

We set
V_{i}:=(\mathbb{A}^{n},H_{i})\simeq\overline{V}_{i}\times_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}\mathbb{A}^{n}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
.
Let
X^{\prime}
(resp.
V_{i}^{\prime}
) be the fs log scheme whose underlying scheme is
\underline{\mathbb{A}}_{\Sigma}
,
and whose log structure is associated with the open immersion
\mathbb{A}^{n}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\to\underline{\mathbb{A}}_{\Sigma}

(resp.
(V_{i}-\partial V_{i})\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\to\underline{\mathbb{A}}_{\Sigma}
),
and let
H_{i}^{\prime}
be the strict transform of
H_{i}
in
\underline{\mathbb{A}}_{\Sigma}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
.
The morphisms
X^{\prime}\to\mathbb{A}^{n}\times\square
and
V_{i}^{\prime}\to V_{i}\times\square
for all
1\leq i\leq n

are admissible blow-ups along smooth centers.
Hence the maps

\mathop{\rm colim}(\mathcal{V}^{\prime})\to\mathop{\rm colim}(\mathcal{V})\times\square\text{ and }X^{\prime}\to\mathbb{A}^{n}\times\square

are isomorphisms in
\mathbf{logSH}_{S^{1}}(S)
,
where

\mathcal{V}:=\mathrm{horn}(\mathbb{A}^{n},H_{1}+\cdots+H_{n})\text{ and }\mathcal{V}^{\prime}:=\mathrm{horn}(X,H_{1}^{\prime}+\cdots+H_{n}^{\prime}).

It follows that the map

(7.2.7)

X^{\prime}/\mathop{\rm colim}(\mathcal{V}^{\prime})\to(\mathbb{A}^{n}/\mathop{\rm colim}(\mathcal{V}))\times\square

is an isomorphism in
\mathbf{logSH}_{S^{1}}(S)
.

By considering the suitable compactifying log structures for the schemes in (
7.2.6
), we obtain the map

X^{\prime}/\mathop{\rm colim}(\mathcal{V}^{\prime})\to(\mathbb{P}^{n})^{n}/\mathop{\rm colim}(\mathcal{Z}).

Let
q^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{A}^{n}\to(\mathbb{P}^{n})^{n}
be the restriction of the diagonal map.
The restriction of
X^{\prime}\to(\mathbb{P}^{n})^{n}
at
t=0
and
t=1
are exactly
q
and
q^{\prime}
.
It follows that the two naturally induced maps

q,q^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{A}^{n}/\mathop{\rm colim}(\mathcal{V})\rightrightarrows(\mathbb{P}^{n})^{n}/\mathop{\rm colim}(\mathcal{Z})

are equal in
\mathbf{logSH}_{S^{1}}(S)
since we can apply the argument in Lemma
3.2.12
to the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{n}/\mathop{\rm colim}(\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}/\mathop{\rm colim}(\mathcal{V}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{P}^{n})^{n}/\mathop{\rm colim}(\mathcal{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{A}^{n}/\mathop{\rm colim}(\mathcal{V}))\times\square,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{0}^{\prime}}

where
u_{0}^{\prime}
and
u_{1}^{\prime}
are the lifts of the zero section and one section.

Finally,
by combining the commutative diagrams (
7.2.6
) and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{n}/\operatorname{pt}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{P}^{n})^{n}/\mathop{\rm colim}(\mathcal{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{n}/\mathop{\rm colim}(\overline{\mathcal{V}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{n}/\mathop{\rm colim}(\mathcal{V})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

in
\mathbf{logSH}_{S^{1}}(S)
, we deduce that (
7.2.5
) commutes.
∎

Lemma 7.2.3
.

Suppose
\mathbf{E}
is oriented.
Let
i\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{n}
be the closed immersion sending
[x_{0}:x_{1}]
to
[x_{0}:x_{1},0,\ldots,0]
, let
p\colon\mathbb{P}^{n}\to\operatorname{pt}
and
q\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\operatorname{pt}
be the structure morphisms, and let
\mathcal{T}
be the tautological bundle on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
.
Then the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{*-2,*-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}^{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}^{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}}

commutes, where the right vertical isomorphism sends
x\in\mathbf{E}^{*-2,*-1}
to
q^{*}(x)\smile\Sigma^{2,1}\mathbf{1}
, and
\psi_{n}
sends
x\in\mathbf{E}^{*-2,*-1}
to
p^{*}(x)\smile c_{1}(\mathcal{T})
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be the tautological bundle on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
From (
7.1.5
), we have the formula

i^{*}\psi_{n}(x)=q^{*}(x)\smile c_{1}(\mathcal{L}).

To conclude the proof, we appeal to (
7.1.3
).
∎

Construction 7.2.4
.

Suppose
\mathbf{E}
is oriented and
\mathcal{E}\to X
is a rank
d+1
vector bundle with
X\in\mathbf{lSm}/S
.
Let
\mathcal{L}
be the tautological line bundle on
\mathcal{E}
.
There is a map

(7.2.8)

\rho_{\mathcal{E}}\colon\bigoplus_{i=0}^{d}\mathbf{E}^{*-i,*-2i}(X)\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}))

sending
(x_{0},\ldots,x_{d})
to
\sum_{i=0}^{d}p^{*}(x_{i})\smile c_{1}(\mathcal{L})\smile\cdots\smile c_{1}(\mathcal{L})

(
i
copies of
c_{1}(\mathcal{L})
).
For abbreviation, we often omit the symbol
\smile
, and then write the expression above as

\sum_{i=0}^{d}p^{*}(x_{i})c_{1}(\mathcal{L})^{i}.

If
U
is an open subscheme of
X
with
\mathcal{E}_{U}:=\mathcal{E}\times_{X}U
,
we consider the projection
q\colon\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{E}_{U}\to X/U
and the morphism

\Delta_{i}\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})/\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{U})\to\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})^{i+1}/(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{U})\times\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})^{i})\cong\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})/\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{U})\times\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})^{i}

naturally induced by the diagonal morphism
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})\to\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})^{i+1}
for every integer
i\geq 1
.
We have homomorphisms

(7.2.9)

\begin{split}\mathbf{E}^{*-2i,*-i}(X/U)&\xrightarrow{q^{*}}\mathbf{E}^{*-2i,*-i}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})/\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{U}))\\
&\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})/\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{U})\times\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})^{i})\xrightarrow{\Delta_{i}^{*}}\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})/\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{U}))\end{split}

where the second homomorphism is constructed from (
7.0.2
) and
c_{1}(\mathcal{L})\in\mathbf{E}^{2,1}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}))
.
Collect (
7.2.9
) for every
i=0,\ldots,d
to form the homomorphism

\rho_{\mathcal{E},U}\colon\bigoplus_{i=0}^{d}\mathbf{E}^{*-2i,*-i}(X/U)\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})/\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{U})).

Lemma 7.2.5
.

Suppose
\mathbf{E}
is oriented.
With the above notation, the diagram

(7.2.10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus_{i=0}^{d}\mathbf{E}^{*-2i,*-i}(X/U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus_{i=0}^{d}\mathbf{E}^{*-2i,*-i}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus_{i=0}^{d}\mathbf{E}^{*-2i,*-i}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus_{i=0}^{d}\mathbf{E}^{*-2i+1,*-i}(X/U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})/\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{U}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{U}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{*+1,*}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})/\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{U}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\mathcal{E},U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\mathcal{E}_{U}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\mathcal{E},U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}

commutes.

Proof.

The left and middle squares of (
7.2.10
) commute by the functoriality of the construction.
Let
j\colon U\to X
be the open immersion, and
\Delta_{i,U}\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{U})\to\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{U})\times\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})^{i}
be the restriction of the diagonal map for every integer
i\geq 1
.
There is a commutative diagram

(7.2.11)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{*-2i,*-i}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{U})\times\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})^{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{U}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{*-2i+1,*-i}(X/U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{*+1,*}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})/\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{U})\times\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})^{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{*+1*}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})/\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{U})),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{i,U}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{i}^{*}}

where the lower row of (
7.2.11
) comes from (
7.2.9
), and the upper left horizontal homomorphism of (
7.2.11
) is constructed from
j^{*}\colon\mathbf{E}^{*-2i,*-i}(U)\to\mathbf{E}^{*-2i,*-i}(X)
, (
7.0.2
), and
c_{1}(\mathcal{L})\in\mathbf{E}^{2,1}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}))
.
We set
\mathcal{L}_{U}:=j^{*}(\mathcal{L})
, which is the tautological bundle on
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{U})
.
Since
j^{*}c_{1}(\mathcal{L})=c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{U})
by (
7.1.5
),
the collection of the composite of the homomorphisms in the upper row of (
7.2.11
) for
i=0,\ldots,d
constructs
\rho_{\mathcal{E}_{U}}
.
This shows that the right square of (
7.2.10
) commutes.
∎

Theorem 7.2.6

(Projective bundle theorem)
.

Suppose that
\mathbf{E}
is oriented.
With the above notation, the map (
7.2.8
) is an isomorphism.

Proof.

There exists a Zariski cover
\{U_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
of
X
such that
\mathcal{E}\times_{X}U_{i}
is a trivial bundle on
U_{i}
for every
1\leq i\leq n
.
We proceed by induction on
n
.
The case
n=1
is done in Proposition
7.2.7
below.
Suppose that the claim holds for
n-1
.
We set
U:=U_{1}\cup\cdots\cup U_{n-1}
.
By induction, the map
\rho_{\mathcal{E}_{U}}
in Lemma
7.2.5
is an isomorphism.
Therefore, by the five lemma, it suffices to show that the map
\rho_{\mathcal{E},U}
in
Lemma
7.2.5
is an isomorphism.
By excision, it suffices to show that the map

\oplus_{i=0}^{d}\mathbf{E}^{*-2i,*-i}(U_{n}/U_{n}\cap U)\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{U_{n}})/\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{U_{n}\cap U}))

is an isomorphism, where
\mathcal{E}_{U_{n}}:=\mathcal{E}\times_{X}U_{n}
and
\mathcal{E}_{U_{n}\cap U}:=\mathcal{E}\times_{X}(U_{n}\cap U)
.
Again by the five lemma and Lemma
7.2.5
, it suffices to show that the maps

\oplus_{i=0}^{d}\mathbf{E}^{*-2i,*-i}(V)\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{V}))

are isomorphisms for
V=U_{n}
and
V=U_{n}\cap U
.
This follows from the induction hypothesis.
∎

Proposition 7.2.7
.

Suppose that
\mathbf{E}
is oriented.
Then the map

\rho_{d}\colon\bigoplus_{i=0}^{d}\mathbf{E}^{*-2i,*-i}(X)\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(X\times\mathbb{P}^{d})

is an isomorphism for every
X\in\mathbf{lSm}/S
, where
\rho_{d}:=\rho_{\mathcal{E}}
, and
\mathcal{E}
is the rank
d+1
trivial bundle on
X
.

Proof.

We only deal with the case where
X=\operatorname{pt}
since the general case follows in a similar way.
It suffices to show that the map

\widetilde{\rho}_{d}\colon\bigoplus_{i=1}^{d}\mathbf{E}^{*-2i,*-i}\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}^{d}/\operatorname{pt})

naturally induced by
\rho_{d}
is an isomorphism.
Let
i\colon\mathbb{P}^{d-1}\to\mathbb{P}^{d}
be the closed immersion sending
[a_{0}:\cdots:a_{d-1}]
to
[a_{0}:\cdots:a_{d-1}:0]
.
There is a naturally induced diagram

(7.2.12)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{*-2d,*-d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus_{i=1}^{d}\mathbf{E}^{*-2i,*-i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus_{i=1}^{d-1}\mathbf{E}^{*-2i,*-i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}^{d}/\mathbb{P}^{d-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}^{d}/\operatorname{pt})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}^{d-1}/\operatorname{pt})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\rho}_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\rho}_{d-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}}

with exact rows, where
q
and
r
are the obvious inclusion and projection maps.

We will show that (
7.2.12
) commutes.
Let
\mathcal{L}_{d}
be the tautological line bundle on
\mathbb{P}^{d}
.
To show the square (b) commutes, let
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})
be an element of
\oplus_{i=1}^{d}\mathbf{E}^{*-2i,*-i}
.
We need to show the formula

i^{*}\big(\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{d})^{i}\big)=\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}x_{i}c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{d-1})^{i}.

Since
i^{*}(\mathcal{L}_{d})\cong\mathcal{L}_{d-1}
, we are reduced to showing the vanishing

(7.2.13)

c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{d-1})^{d}=0.

For every integer
n\geq 1
, let
\psi_{n}\colon\mathbf{E}^{*-2,*-1}\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}^{n}/\operatorname{pt})
be the homomorphism in Lemma
7.2.3
.
There is a diagram

(7.2.14)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{*-2d,*-d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{*-2d,*-d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}((\mathbb{P}^{1}/\operatorname{pt})^{\wedge d})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}((\mathbb{P}^{d}/\operatorname{pt})^{\wedge d})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}((\mathbb{P}^{d-1}/\operatorname{pt})^{\wedge d})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}^{d}/\mathbb{P}^{d-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}^{d}/\operatorname{pt})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}^{d-1}/\operatorname{pt}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{d}^{\wedge d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{d-1}^{\wedge d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(c)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{*}}

where the homomorphisms denoted by
\Delta^{*}
are naturally induced by the diagonal morphisms.
By Lemmas
7.2.2
and
7.2.3
,
(c)
and
(d)
commutes.
Apply the relation (
7.1.5
) to the inclusion
i\colon\mathbb{P}^{d-1}\to\mathbb{P}^{d}
to deduce that
(e)
commutes too.
It is trivial that the square
(f)
commutes.
The composite
\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}^{d}/\mathbb{P}^{d-1})\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}^{d-1}/\operatorname{pt})
is zero,
so from the commutativity of (
7.2.14
) we deduce
c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{d-1})^{d}=0
.
It follows that
(b)
commutes.
The commutativity of
(c)
and
(d)
implies the commutativity of
(a)
.

We proceed by induction on
d
to show that
\widetilde{\rho}_{d}
is an isomorphism for every integer
d\geq 0
.
The claim is trivial if
d=0
.
In the following we assume
d>0
.
If
\widetilde{\rho}_{d-1}
is an isomorphism, then
i^{*}
is surjective.
From the long exact sequence

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}^{*-1,*}(\mathbb{P}^{d}/\operatorname{pt})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{i^{*}}\mathbf{E}^{*-1,*}(\mathbb{P}^{d-1}/\operatorname{pt})\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}^{d}/\mathbb{P}^{d-1})

\displaystyle\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}^{d}/\operatorname{pt})\xrightarrow{i^{*}}\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}^{d-1}/\operatorname{pt}),

we see that the sequence

0\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}^{d}/\mathbb{P}^{d-1})\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}^{d}/\operatorname{pt})\xrightarrow{i^{*}}\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}^{d-1}/\operatorname{pt})\to 0

is exact.
Combine this with (
7.2.12
) and apply the five lemma to deduce that
\widetilde{\rho}_{d}
is an isomorphism too.
∎

Proposition 7.2.8
.

Suppose
\mathbf{E}
is oriented and
\mathcal{E}\to X
is a vector bundle in
\mathbf{SmlSm}/S
.
Then there is an exact sequence

(7.2.15)

0\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}))\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{O}))\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}))\to 0.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.2.19
, it suffices to show that
\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{O}))\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}))
is surjective.
This follows from Theorem
7.2.6
.
∎

As in
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy theory, we have the following consequence of the projective bundle theorem.

Proposition 7.2.9
.

Suppose
\mathbf{E}
is oriented.
For all
x\in\mathbf{E}^{m,p}(X)
and
y\in\mathbf{E}^{n,q}(X)
with
X\in\mathbf{lSm}/S
and
m,n,p,q\in\mathbb{Z}
, there is the formula

x\smile y=(-1)^{mn}y\smile x

in
\mathbf{E}^{m+n,p+q}(X)
.

Proof.

Let
\eta\colon T\wedge T\to T\wedge T
be the permutation map, which naturally induces a map

\eta\colon\mathbf{E}^{**}(X)\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(X)

for all
X\in\mathbf{lSm}/S
.
In general, we have the formula

(7.2.16)

x\smile y=(-1)^{mn}\eta^{pq}y\smile x.

Hence, it remains to show
\eta=1
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be the tautological bundle on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, and we set
\lambda:=c_{1}(\mathcal{T})
.
When
x=y=\lambda
in (
7.2.16
), we have
\lambda^{2}=\eta\lambda^{2}
.
Together with Theorem
7.2.6
, we have
1=\eta
.
∎

7.3.
Chern classes

Throughout this subsection, we fix
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
and an oriented homotopy commutative monoid
\mathbf{E}
in
\mathbf{logSH}(S)
.

Definition 7.3.1
.

Suppose
\mathcal{E}\to X
is a rank
d
vector bundle with
X\in\mathbf{lSm}/S
.
To define higher Chern classes of
\mathcal{E}
, we imitate Grothendieck’s approach as follows.
Let
p\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})\to X
be the canonical projection,
and let
\mathcal{T}
be the tautological line bundle on
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})
.
For simplicity of notation, we set
\lambda:=c_{1}(\mathcal{T})
.
Owing to Theorem
7.2.6
,
there exist unique classes
c_{i}(\mathcal{E})\in\mathbf{E}^{2i,i}(X)
for all
0\leq i\leq d
such that
c_{0}(\mathcal{E})=1
and

(7.3.1)

\sum_{i=0}^{d}p^{*}(c_{i}(\mathcal{E}))(-\lambda)^{d-i}=0.

The class
c_{i}(\mathcal{E})
is called the
i
-th Chern class

of
\mathcal{E}
.
We set
c_{i}(\mathcal{E}):=0
if
i\geq d+1
.

Remark 7.3.2
.

Suppose
\mathcal{E}\to X
is a line bundle in
\mathbf{lSm}/S
.
Then
\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{E})\cong X
, and (
7.3.1
) becomes

c_{1}(\mathcal{E})-c_{1}(\mathcal{E})=0.

Here, the left
c_{1}(\mathcal{E})
is defined in Definition
7.3.1
, and the right
c_{1}(\mathcal{E})
is defined in Definition
7.1.1
.
Hence, these two definitions of the first Chern class of
\mathcal{E}
agree.

Proposition 7.3.3
.

Suppose
\mathcal{E}\to X
is a trivial line bundle in
\mathbf{lSm}/S
.
Then
c_{i}(\mathcal{E})=0
for all integers
i\geq 1
.

Proof.

Combine (
7.2.13
) and (
7.3.1
).
∎

Proposition 7.3.4
.

Suppose
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
is a morphism in
\mathbf{lSm}/S
,
and
\mathcal{E}\to X
is a rank
d
vector bundle.
Then, for every integer
i\geq 0
, we have the formula

f^{*}(c_{i}(\mathcal{E}))=c_{i}(f^{*}\mathcal{E}).

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
be the tautological line bundle of
\mathbb{P}(f^{*}\mathcal{E})
, and we set
\lambda^{\prime}:=c_{1}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime*})
.
Apply
f^{*}
to (
7.3.1
) and use (
7.1.5
) to have the formula

\sum_{i=0}^{d}p^{\prime*}(f^{*}(c_{i}(\mathcal{E})))(-\lambda^{\prime})^{d-i}=0,

where
p^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{P}(f^{*}\mathcal{E})\to X^{\prime}
is the projection.
To conclude, compare this with (
7.3.1
) for the vector bundle
f^{*}\mathcal{E}\to X^{\prime}
.
∎

Proposition 7.3.5

(Splitting principle)
.

If
\mathcal{E}\to X
is a rank
d
vector bundle with
X\in\mathbf{lSm}/S
, then there exists a strict proper smooth morphism
f\colon Y\to X
such that
f^{*}\colon\mathbf{E}^{**}(X)\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(Y)
is a monomorphism and
f^{*}\mathcal{E}
admits a filtration of subbundles

0=\mathcal{E}_{0}\subset\mathcal{E}_{1}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{E}_{d}=\mathcal{E}

such that the quotient bundle
\mathcal{E}_{i}/\mathcal{E}_{i-1}
is a line bundle for every
1\leq i\leq d
.

Proof.

As in intersection theory or
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy theory, let
Y
be the complete flag variety associated with
\mathcal{E}
.
If
f
is the projection
Y\to X
, then it is well known that
f^{*}\mathcal{E}
satisfies the required condition.
Since
f
can be factored as a tower of projective bundles, Theorem
7.2.6
shows that
f^{*}
is a monomorphism.
∎

Definition 7.3.6
.

Suppose
\mathcal{E}\to X
is a rank
d
vector bundle with
X\in\mathbf{lSm}/S
.
Let
p\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})\to X
and
p^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{O})\to X
be the canonical projections, and let
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
be the tautological line bundles on
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})
and
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{O})
for simplicity of notation.
We set
\lambda:=c_{1}(\mathcal{T})
and
\lambda^{\prime}:=c_{1}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})
.
Consider the class

(7.3.2)

\overline{t}(\mathcal{E}):=\sum_{i=0}^{d}p^{\prime*}(c_{i}(\mathcal{E}))(-\lambda^{\prime})^{d-i}\in\mathbf{E}^{2d,d}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{O})).

Due to (
7.1.5
) and (
7.3.1
), the restriction of
\overline{t}(\mathcal{E})
to
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})
is
0
.
From (
7.2.15
), we have a unique class

t(\mathcal{E})\in\mathbf{E}^{2d,d}(\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}))

mapping to
\overline{t}(\mathcal{E})
, which we call the
Thom class
of
\mathcal{E}
.

Proposition 7.3.7
.

Let
t_{\infty}\in\mathbf{E}^{2,1}(\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{T}_{1}))
be a Thom orientation, where
\mathcal{T}_{1}
is the tautological line bundle on
\mathbb{P}^{\infty}
.
If
c_{\infty}
is the Chern orientation associated with
t_{\infty}
, then the Thom class
t(\mathcal{T}_{1})
obtained from
c_{\infty}
coincides with
t_{\infty}
.

Proof.

Consider the isomorphism
z^{*}
given in (
7.1.10
).
We need to show
z^{*}t_{\infty}=z^{*}t(\mathcal{T}_{1})
.
The left-hand side equals
c_{\infty}
.
On the other hand,
from (
7.3.2
),
we have the formula
z^{*}t(\mathcal{T}_{1})=c_{1}(\mathcal{T}_{1})
.
An appeal to (
7.1.4
) finishes the proof.
∎

Suppose
X\in\mathbf{lSm}/S
and
\mathcal{E}\to X
is a rank
d
vector bundle, and let
E
be the exceptional divisor on
{\mathrm{Bl}}_{X}\mathcal{E}
.
Using the graph morphisms of the two projections
\mathcal{E}\to X
and
({\mathrm{Bl}}_{X}\mathcal{E},E)\to X
,
we obtain a morphism

(7.3.3)

\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E})\to\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E})\wedge X_{+}.

We use the latter map to define the cup product

\smile\colon\mathbf{E}^{**}(X)\times\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}))\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}))

as in (
7.0.3
).

Theorem 7.3.8

(Thom isomorphism)
.

For every rank
d
vector bundle
p\colon\mathcal{E}\to X
with
X\in\mathbf{lSm}/S
,
the cup product homomorphism

(7.3.4)

-\smile t(\mathcal{E})\colon\mathbf{E}^{**}(X)\to\mathbf{E}^{*+2d,*+d}(\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}))

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Combine Theorem
7.2.6
and (
7.2.15
) to have a commutative diagram with exact rows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus_{i=0}^{d}\mathbf{E}^{*+2i,*+i}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus_{i=1}^{d}\mathbf{E}^{*+2i,*+i}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{*+2d,*+d}(\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{*+2d,*+d}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{O}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{*+2d,*+d}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\smile t(\mathcal{E})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

The five lemma finishes the proof.
∎

Proposition 7.3.9
.

Suppose
X\in\mathbf{lSm}/S
and
p\colon\mathcal{E}\to X
is a rank
d
vector bundle.
For all integers
p
and
q
, there is a canonical equivalence of spectra

(7.3.5)

\operatorname{map}(\Sigma_{T}^{\infty}X_{+},\Sigma^{p,q}\mathbf{E})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\operatorname{map}(\Sigma_{T}^{\infty}\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}),\Sigma^{p+2d,q+d}\mathbf{E}).

Proof.

The Thom class
t(\mathcal{E})
gives a map
\mathbf{1}\to\operatorname{map}(\Sigma_{T}^{\infty}\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}),\Sigma^{2d,d}\mathbf{E})
,
where
\mathbf{1}
denotes the sphere spectrum.
We have the maps of spectra

\displaystyle\operatorname{map}(\Sigma_{T}^{\infty}X_{+},\Sigma^{p,q}\mathbf{E})\wedge\operatorname{map}(\Sigma_{T}^{\infty}\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}),\Sigma^{2d,d}\mathbf{E})

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\operatorname{map}(\Sigma_{T}^{\infty}X_{+}\wedge\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}),\Sigma^{p+2d,q+d}\mathbf{E}\wedge\mathbf{E})

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\operatorname{map}(\Sigma_{T}^{\infty}X_{+}\wedge\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}),\Sigma^{p+2d,q+d}\mathbf{E})\to\operatorname{map}(\Sigma_{T}^{\infty}\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}),\Sigma^{p+2d,q+d}\mathbf{E}),

where the second map is naturally induced by the ring structure map
\mathbf{E}\wedge\mathbf{E}\to\mathbf{E}
,
and the third map is naturally induced by (
7.3.3
).
This constructs the map in (
7.3.5
).
If we apply
\pi_{r}
to (
7.3.5
) for every integer
r
,
then we obtain an isomorphism due to Theorem
7.3.8
.
Hence (
7.3.5
) is an equivalence.
∎

Proposition 7.3.10
.

Suppose
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
is a morphism in
\mathbf{lSm}/S
and
\mathcal{E}\to X
is a vector bundle.
If
g\colon\mathrm{Th}(f^{*}\mathcal{E})\to\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E})
is the naturally induced morphism,
then for every integer
i\geq 0
we have the formula

g^{*}(t(\mathcal{E}))=t(f^{*}\mathcal{E}).

Proof.

Apply Proposition
7.3.4
to (
7.3.2
).
∎

Suppose
i\colon Z\to X
is a codimension
d
closed immersion in
\mathbf{Sm}/S
.
Combine (
3.2.32
) with Theorem
7.3.8
to have an isomorphism

(7.3.6)

\mathbf{E}^{*-2d,*-d}(Z)\cong\mathbf{E}^{**}(X/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}X,E)),

where
E
is the exceptional divisor of the blow-up
{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}X
.
Compose with the canonical homomorphism
\mathbf{E}^{**}(X/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}X,E))\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(X)
to have a homomorphism

(7.3.7)

i_{*}\colon\mathbf{E}^{*-2d,*-d}(Z)\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(X),

which we call the
Gysin map
.
From (
7.3.6
), we have the long exact sequence

(7.3.8)

\cdots\to\mathbf{E}^{*-2d,*-d}(Z)\xrightarrow{i_{*}}\mathbf{E}^{**}(X)\xrightarrow{j^{*}}\mathbf{E}^{**}({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}X,E)\to\mathbf{E}^{*-2d+1,*-d}(Z)\to\cdots,

where
j\colon({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}X,E)\to X
is the projection.

Proposition 7.3.11
.

Suppose
\mathcal{E}\to Z
is a rank
d
vector bundle in
\mathbf{lSm}/S
.
If
i\colon Z\to\mathcal{E}
is the zero section, then the isomorphism (
7.3.6
) with
X:=\mathcal{E}
coincides with the Thom isomorphism (
7.3.4
).

Proof.

Lemma
3.2.23
implies that the map

\mathfrak{p}_{\mathcal{E},Z}\colon\mathcal{E}/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}\mathcal{E},E)\to\mathrm{Th}({\mathrm{N}}_{Z}\mathcal{E})=\mathcal{E}/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}\mathcal{E},E)

is equal to the identity map
\operatorname{id}
in
\mathbf{logSH}_{S^{1}}(S)
, where
E
is the exceptional divisor.
Composing
\mathfrak{p}_{\mathcal{E},Z}
and
\operatorname{id}
with the Thom isomorphism in (
7.3.4
)
yields the claim.
∎

Suppose

(7.3.9)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

is a cartesian square in
\mathbf{Sm}/S
such that
i
and
i^{\prime}
are closed immersions of codimensions
d
and
d^{\prime}
.
Then there is a naturally induced diagram

(7.3.10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{*-2d,*-d}(Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(X/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}X,E))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{*-2d^{\prime},*-d^{\prime}}(Z^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(X^{\prime}/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z^{\prime}}X^{\prime},E^{\prime})),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{d-d^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F})\smile g^{*}(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

where
E
and
E^{\prime}
are the exceptional divisors and
\mathcal{F}:=g^{*}{\mathrm{N}}_{Z}X/{\mathrm{N}}_{Z^{\prime}}X^{\prime}
.

What we are concerned with is the commutativity of (
7.3.10
).
Use the diagram (
3.2.35
) to reduce to the case where
X={\mathrm{N}}_{Z}X
,
X^{\prime}={\mathrm{N}}_{Z^{\prime}}X^{\prime}
, and
i
and
i^{\prime}
are zero sections.
Hence to show that (
7.3.10
) commutes, it suffices to show that the naturally induced diagram

(7.3.11)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{*-2d,*-d}(Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathrm{Th}({\mathrm{N}}_{Z}X))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{*-2d^{\prime},*-d^{\prime}}(Z^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathrm{Th}({\mathrm{N}}_{Z^{\prime}}X^{\prime}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{d-d^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F})\smile g^{*}(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

commutes.
This holds if we have the formula

(7.3.12)

c_{d-d^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F})\smile\overline{t}({\mathrm{N}}_{Z^{\prime}}(X^{\prime}))=g^{\prime*}(\overline{t}({\mathrm{N}}_{Z}X)),

where
g^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{P}({\mathrm{N}}_{Z^{\prime}}X^{\prime}\oplus\mathcal{O})\to\mathbb{P}({\mathrm{N}}_{Z}X\oplus\mathcal{O})
is the naturally induced morphism.

Lemma 7.3.12
.

The diagram (
7.3.10
) commutes in the special case where
i
is a closed immersion of codimension
1
and
f=i
.

Proof.

We need to show (
7.3.12
).
Let
W
be the divisor on
X\cong\mathbb{P}({\mathrm{N}}_{Z}X\oplus\mathcal{O})
at
\infty
,
which is isomorphic to
Z
.
We have
g^{\prime*}\overline{t}({\mathrm{N}}_{Z}X)=c_{1}({\mathrm{N}}_{Z}X)-g^{\prime*}(c_{1}(\mathcal{T}))
, where
g^{\prime}
is the zero section, and
\mathcal{T}
is the tautological line bundle on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{N}_{Z}X\oplus\mathcal{O})
.
Hence, it remains to show the line bundle
g^{\prime*}(\mathcal{T})
is trivial.
According to
[
41
, Paragraph B.5.6]
, there is a section of the dual bundle
\mathcal{T}^{*}
whose zero scheme is
W
.
This implies that
g^{\prime*}(\mathcal{T}^{*})
is trivial since the intersection of
W
and the zero section is empty.
It follows that
g^{\prime*}(\mathcal{T})
is also trivial.
∎

Lemma 7.3.13
.

The diagram (
7.3.10
) commutes in the special case where
{\mathrm{N}}_{Z^{\prime}}X^{\prime}\to g^{*}{\mathrm{N}}_{Z}X
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

We need to show (
7.3.12
).
This follows from Proposition
7.3.4
and the formulation (
7.3.2
).
∎

Lemma 7.3.14
.

Let
X
be a scheme with a rank
n
vector bundle
\mathcal{E}\to X
.
If
s\colon X\to\mathcal{E}
is a global section of
\mathcal{E}
that intersects transversally with the zero section in a
smooth closed subscheme
Z
of
X
,
then there is a morphism

{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z\times\infty}(X\times\mathbb{P}^{1})\to\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{O})

extending the morphism
f\colon X\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\to\mathcal{E}
sending
(x,a)
to
as(x)
.

Proof.

Suppose
U=\operatorname{Spec}(A)
is an open affine subscheme of
X
such that
\mathcal{E}
becomes a trivial bundle
\operatorname{Spec}(A[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}])
.
If
V=\operatorname{Spec}(B)
is an open affine subscheme of
U
,
let
h\colon A\to B
be the structure homomorphism.
Then the morphism
\mathcal{E}|_{V}\to\mathcal{E}|_{U}
is given by a homomorphism
A[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]\to B[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
sending

a\mapsto h(a)
for all
a\in A
and
x_{i}\mapsto\sum_{j=1}^{n}m_{ij}x_{j}
for some
M=(m_{ij})\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(B)
.
The restriction of
f
on
U\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
(resp.
V\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
) is given by
A[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]\to A[x]

(resp.
B[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]\to B[x]
) sending
a\mapsto a
for all
a\in A
and
x_{i}\mapsto s_{i}x_{i}

for some
s_{i}\in A
(resp.
x_{i}\mapsto t_{i}x_{i}
for some
t_{i}\in B
).

There is a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[x]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B[x].}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{i}\mapsto s_{i}x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{i}\mapsto\sum_{j=1}^{n}m_{ij}x_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x\mapsto x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{i}\mapsto t_{i}x}

Since
s
is transversal with the zero section, the restriction of
{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z\times\infty}(X\times\mathbb{P}^{1})
to
U\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
V\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
have Zariski covers

\{\operatorname{Spec}(A[1/x,s_{1}x,\ldots,s_{n}x])\}\cup\{\operatorname{Spec}(A[1/(s_{i}x),s_{1}/s_{i},\ldots,s_{n}/s_{i},s_{i}]):1\leq i\leq n\}

and

\{\operatorname{Spec}(B[1/x,t_{1}x,\ldots,t_{n}x])\}\cup\{\operatorname{Spec}(B[1/(t_{i}x),t_{1}/t_{i},\ldots,t_{n}/t_{i},t_{i}]):1\leq i\leq n\}

There are naturally induced commutative diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[1/x,s_{1}x,\ldots,s_{n}x]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B[1/x,t_{1}x,\ldots,t_{n}x]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{i}\mapsto s_{i}x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{i}\mapsto t_{i}x}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[x_{1}/x_{i},\ldots,x_{n}/x_{i},x_{i}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[x,s_{1}/s_{i},\ldots,s_{n}/s_{i},s_{i}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B[x_{1}/x_{i},\ldots,x_{n}/x_{i},x_{i}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B[x,t_{1}/t_{i},\ldots,t_{n}/t_{i},t_{i}].}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{j}/x_{i}\mapsto s_{j}/s_{i},x_{i}\mapsto s_{i}x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{j}/x_{i}\mapsto t_{j}/t_{i},x_{i}\mapsto t_{i}x}

Hence we can glue the appropriate morphisms all together to produce a morphism

{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z\times\infty}(X\times\mathbb{P}^{1})\to\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{O})
,
which yields an extension of
f\colon X\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\to\mathcal{E}
.
∎

Lemma 7.3.15
.

Let
\mathcal{E}\to X
be a line bundle in
\mathbf{Sm}/S
.
If
s\colon X\to\mathcal{E}
is a global section of
\mathcal{E}
that intersects transversally
with the zero section in a smooth closed subscheme
Z
of
X
,
then we have the formula

i_{*}i^{*}a=c_{1}(\mathcal{E})\smile a

for all
a\in\mathbf{E}^{**}(X)
, where
i\colon Z\to X
is the obvious closed immersion.

Proof.

Let
s_{0}\colon X\to\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{O})
be the zero section.
There are cartesian squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{O}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{O}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{0}}

Lemma
7.3.12
implies the formula

s^{*}s_{*}a=c_{1}(\mathcal{E})\smile a.

On the other hand, Lemma
7.3.13
implies that we have

s_{0}^{*}s_{*}a=i_{*}i^{*}a.

Hence we are reduced to show the formula
s^{*}=s_{0}^{*}
.
In effect,
let
Y
be the fs log scheme whose underlying scheme is
{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z\times\infty}(X\times\mathbb{P}^{1})
and whose log structure is associated with the open immersion
X\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\to{\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z\times\infty}(X\times\mathbb{P}^{1})
.
Then
Y
is an admissible blow-up of
X\times\square
along a smooth center.
From Lemma
7.3.14
, we have a morphism

Y\to\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{O}),

and the zero section and one section
X\rightrightarrows X\times\square
have lifts to
Y
.
Lemma
3.2.12
finishes the proof.
∎

Proposition 7.3.16

(Whitney sum formula)
.

Suppose there is an exact sequence of vector bundles

0\to\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}\to 0

over
X\in\mathbf{lSm}/S
.
Then, for every integer
i\geq 0
, we have the formula

c_{i}(\mathcal{E})=\sum_{j=0}^{i}c_{j}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime})c_{i-j}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}).

Proof.

Owing to Proposition
7.3.5
, we only need to consider the case when
\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}
is a line bundle.
Let
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
be the tautological line bundles on
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})
and
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
.
We set

a:=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}p^{*}c_{i}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime})(-\lambda)^{n-1-i}\text{ and }b:=-\lambda+p^{*}c_{1}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}),

where
p\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})\to X
is the projection, and
\lambda:=c_{1}(\mathcal{T})
.
Due to Theorem
7.2.6
, it suffices to show
a\smile b=0
.

We set
\mathcal{F}:=\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime*}
and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime*}
with tautological line bundles
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
.
Observe that we have an exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{O}\to 0.

We identify
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})
with
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
and
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
with
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
,
and then there are canonical isomorphisms
\mathcal{T}\cong\mathcal{L}\otimes p^{*}\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}
and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\cong\mathcal{L}\otimes p^{\prime*}\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}
,
where
p^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime})\to X
is the projection.
According to
[
41
, Paragraph B.5.6]
, there exists a global section of
\mathcal{L}^{*}
whose zero scheme is
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
.
Together with Lemma
7.3.15
, we have the formula

(7.3.13)

i_{*}i^{*}=\tau\smile(-),

where
i\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime})\to\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})
is the closed immersion, and
\tau:=c_{1}(\mathcal{L})
.

We set

(7.3.14)

c:=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}p^{*}c_{i}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})(-\tau)^{n-1-i}\text{ and }d:=-\tau.

Since
i^{*}\mathcal{L}\cong\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
, we have
i^{*}c=0
.
Together with (
7.3.13
), we have

(7.3.15)

c\smile d=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}p^{*}c_{i}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})(-\tau)^{n-i}=0.

This implies the equality
c_{i}(\mathcal{F})=c_{i}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
for all
0\leq i\leq n-1
and the vanishing
c_{n}(\mathcal{F})=0
.
In particular,
the homomorphism
i^{*}\colon\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F}))\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime}))
is isomorphic to the quotient homomorphism

(7.3.16)

\mathbf{E}^{**}(X)[\tau]/(\tau c)\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(X)[\tau]/(c).

Since
i^{*}a=0
, we deduce that
a
is a multiple of
c
.

Let
j\colon(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}),\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}))\to\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})
be the morphism removing the log structure.
There is a canonical exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})}^{\mathrm{gp}}\to\mathcal{M}_{(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}),\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}))}^{\mathrm{gp}}\to i_{*}\mathbb{Z}\to 0.

This naturally induces an exact sequence

H_{{s\acute{e}t}}^{0}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}),\mathbb{Z})\xrightarrow{\delta}\operatorname{Pic}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}))\xrightarrow{j^{*}}\operatorname{Pic}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}),\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime})).

Since there is a global section of
\mathcal{L}
whose zero scheme is
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
, the image of
\delta
contains
\mathcal{L}
.
This implies
j^{*}(\mathcal{L})=0
, and hence
j^{*}\mathcal{T}\cong j^{*}p^{*}\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}
.
Together with Proposition
7.3.4
, we have
j^{*}c_{1}(\mathcal{T})=j^{*}p^{*}c_{1}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime})
.
In particular, we have
j^{*}b=0
.

Due to (
7.3.8
), there is an exact sequence

\mathbf{E}^{*-2,*-1}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}))\xrightarrow{i_{*}}\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}))\xrightarrow{j^{*}}\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}),\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime})).

Since
j^{*}b=0
,
b
is in the image of
i_{*}
.
From the explicit description (
7.3.16
), we see that
i^{*}
is surjective.
Together with (
7.3.13
), we deduce that everything in the image of
i_{*}
is a multiple of
\tau
.
In particular,
b
is a multiple of
d
.
We have verified before that
a
is a multiple of
c
.
Therefore, (
7.3.15
) implies
a\smile b=0
.
∎

Proposition 7.3.17
.

Suppose
p\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{O})\to X
is a projective bundle, where
\mathcal{E}\to X
is a rank
d
vector bundle with
X\in\mathbf{Sm}/S
.
If
\mathcal{T}
is the tautological line bundle on
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{O})
and
\mathcal{Q}
is the universal quotient bundle on
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{O})
that fits in an exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{T}\to p^{*}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{O})\to\mathcal{Q}\to 0,

then we have
\overline{t}(\mathcal{E})=c_{d}(\mathcal{Q})
.

Proof.

If
t
is an indeterminate variable, then Proposition
7.3.16
gives the formula

(1+c_{1}(\mathcal{T})t)(1+c_{1}(\mathcal{Q})t+\cdots+c_{d}(\mathcal{Q})t^{d})=1+c_{1}(p^{*}(\mathcal{E}))t+\cdots+c_{d}(p^{*}(\mathcal{E}))t^{d}.

It follows that
c_{d}(\mathcal{Q})
is equal to the coefficient of
t^{d}
in

(1+c_{1}(p^{*}(\mathcal{E}))t+\cdots+c_{d}(p^{*}(\mathcal{E}))t^{d})/(1+c_{1}(\mathcal{T})t),

which is precisely
\overline{t}(\mathcal{E})
.
∎

Proposition 7.3.18

(Excessive intersection formula)
.

Suppose that (
7.3.9
) is a cartesian square in
\mathbf{Sm}/S
such that
i
and
i^{\prime}

are closed immersions of codimensions
d
and
d^{\prime}
.
Then the square (
7.3.10
) commutes.

Proof.

We need to show (
7.3.12
).
Let
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
be the universal quotient bundles on
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})
and
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
.
There is a canonical exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\to g^{\prime*}\mathcal{Q}\to p^{\prime*}\mathcal{F}\to 0,

where
p^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\oplus\mathcal{O})\to Z^{\prime}
is the projection.
To conclude, apply Propositions
7.3.16
and
7.3.17
.
∎

Corollary 7.3.19

(Self-intersection formula)
.

Suppose that
i\colon Z\to X
is a closed immersion in
\mathbf{Sm}/S
of codimension
d
.
Then for all
a\in\mathbf{E}^{**}(X)
we have the formula

i^{*}i_{*}a=c_{d}({\mathrm{N}}_{Z}X)\smile a.

Proof.

This is a special case of Proposition
7.3.18
when
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
is equal to
i\colon Z\to X
.
∎

Lemma 7.3.20
.

Suppose

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

is a diagram in
\mathbf{lSm}/S
, where
\mathcal{E}
is a rank
d
vector bundle on
X
,
s_{0}
is the zero section,
i
is a closed immersion, and
j
is an open immersion.
Then the naturally induced diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{*-2d,*-d}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathcal{E}/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{X}\mathcal{E},E))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(P/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{X}P,E))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\smile t(\mathcal{E})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

commutes.

Proof.

There is a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{*-2d,*-d}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(P/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{X}P,E))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathcal{E}/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{X}\mathcal{E},E)).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\smile t(\mathcal{E})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\smile t(\mathcal{E})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{p}_{X,P}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{p}_{X,\mathcal{E}}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(c)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

The diagram
(a)
commutes by the definition of
i_{*}
, the diagram (b) commutes by Proposition
7.3.11
, and the diagram (c) commutes by (
3.2.30
).
Combine these to conclude.
∎

Proposition 7.3.21
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathbf{Sm}/S
and
\mathcal{E}\to X
and
\mathcal{F}\to Y
are vector bundles.
We consider
\mathcal{E}\times_{S}\mathcal{F}
as a vector bundle on
X\times_{S}Y
.
If
\eta\colon\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E})\wedge\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{F})\to\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}\times_{S}\mathcal{F})
is the isomorphism (
3.2.28
) in
\mathbf{logSH}(S)
, then we have the formula

t(\mathcal{E}\times_{S}\mathcal{F})=\eta^{*}(t(\mathcal{E})\smile t(\mathcal{F})).

Proof.

For simplicity of notation, we set
P:=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{O})
,
Q:=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F}\oplus\mathcal{O})
, and
R:=P\times_{S}Q
.
Let
i\colon Z:=X\times_{S}Y\to R
be the zero section.
We need to show that the top triangle in the naturally induced diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E})\wedge\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{F}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{E}\times_{S}\mathcal{F}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(P/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{X}P,E)\wedge Q/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Y}Q,F))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}(R/({\mathrm{Bl}}_{Z}R,G))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\smile t(\mathcal{E})\smile t(\mathcal{F})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\smile t(\mathcal{E}\times_{S}\mathcal{F})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\smile\overline{t}(\mathcal{E})\smile\overline{t}(\mathcal{F})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

commutes, where
E
,
F
, and
G
are the exceptional divisors.
The bottom triangle and the front square clearly commute.
The left parallelogram commutes by the constructions of
t(\mathcal{E})
and
t(\mathcal{F})
from
\overline{t}(\mathcal{E})
and
\overline{t}(\mathcal{F})
.
The right parallelogram commutes owing to Lemma
7.3.20
.
Therefore, it remains to show
i_{*}(-)=(-)\smile\overline{t}(\mathcal{E})\smile\overline{t}(\mathcal{F})
.
Since
i^{*}\colon\mathbf{E}^{**}(R)\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(X)
is surjective by Theorem
7.2.6
,
it suffices to show the formula

(7.3.17)

i_{*}i^{*}(-)=i^{*}(-)\smile\overline{t}(\mathcal{E})\smile\overline{t}(\mathcal{F}).

Let
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
be the universal quotient bundles on
P
and
Q
.
We set
\mathcal{R}:=\mathcal{P}\times_{S}\mathcal{Q}
.
The composite
p^{*}\mathcal{O}\to p^{*}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{O})\to\mathcal{P}
gives a section of
\mathcal{P}
whose zero scheme is the zero section of
P
.
Similarly,
we have a section of
\mathcal{Q}
whose zero scheme is the zero section of
Q
.
Combining these, we obtain a section
s
of
\mathcal{R}
.
This gives a cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

Here
s_{0}
denotes the zero section.
Proposition
7.3.18
yields the formula

(7.3.18)

s^{*}s_{0*}=i_{*}i^{*}.

On the other hand,
applying Proposition
7.3.18
to the cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

we deduce the formula

s^{*}s_{*}=c_{r+s}(\mathcal{R}).

Together with Propositions
7.3.16
and
7.3.17
, we have

(7.3.19)

s^{*}s_{*}=(-)\smile\overline{t}(\mathcal{E})\smile\overline{t}(\mathcal{F}).

Therefore, to deduce (
7.3.17
) from (
7.3.18
) and (
7.3.19
),
it suffices to show
s_{0}^{*}=s^{*}
.
This follows from Lemma
7.3.14
as in the proof of Lemma
7.3.15
.
∎

7.4.
Cohomology of Grassmannians

Throughout this subsection,
we fix a scheme
S\in\mathbf{Sch}
and an oriented homotopy commutative monoid
\mathbf{E}
in
\mathbf{logSH}(S)
.
Let
\mathcal{T}_{r,n}
be the tautological bundle on
{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)
,
and let
\mathcal{Q}_{r,n}
be the universal quotient bundle on
{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)
.
Recall that there is a canonical exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{T}_{r,n}\to\mathcal{O}^{r}\to\mathcal{Q}_{r,n}\to 0.

We define the ring

(7.4.1)

R_{r,n}:=\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-r}]/I_{r,n},

where
I_{r,n}
is the ideal generated by the polynomials
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}
satisfying

1+tz_{1}+\cdots+t^{n}z_{n}=(1+tx_{1}+\cdots+t^{r}x_{r})(1+ty_{1}+\cdots+t^{n-r}y_{n-r}).

It is well known that the singular cohomology ring
H^{*}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r,\mathbb{C}^{n}))
of the complex
Grassmannian manifold
{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,\mathbb{C}^{n})
is isomorphic to
R_{r,n}
.

By Proposition
7.3.16
, there is a canonical homomorphism

R_{r,n}\to\mathbf{E}^{**}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n))

sending
x_{i}
to
c_{i}(\mathcal{T}_{r,n})
and
y_{i}
to
c_{i}(\mathcal{Q}_{r,n})
.
Thus, there is a naturally induced graded ring homomorphism

\varphi_{r,n}\colon\mathbf{E}^{**}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R_{r,n}\to\mathbf{E}^{**}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)).

Here we assign the degree
(2i,i)
to
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
.

We claim that
\varphi_{r,n}
is an isomorphism.
In
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy theory,
this was shown by Naumann-Spitzweck-Østvær
[
75
, Proposition 6.1]
, using explicit
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopies. Without them, we need to produce a suitable logarithmic version of the equation in

[
75
, (18)]
. Here is where the proof significantly differs from the classical one: our new key input is Lemma
7.4.1
below.

Recall that the scheme
{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)
parameterizes the rank
r
subbundles of the rank
n
trivial bundle
\mathcal{O}^{n}
.
The formula
V\mapsto V\oplus\mathcal{O}
gives a closed immersion

(7.4.2)

i\colon{\mathrm{Gr}}(r-1,n-1)\to{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n),

and the formula
V\mapsto V\oplus 0
gives a closed immersion

(7.4.3)

u\colon{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n-1)\to{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n).

There are canonical isomorphisms of vector bundles

(7.4.4)

\begin{split}&i^{*}\mathcal{T}_{r,n}\cong\mathcal{T}_{r-1,n-1}\oplus\mathcal{O},\;i^{*}\mathcal{Q}_{r,n}\cong\mathcal{Q}_{r-1,n-1},\\
&u^{*}\mathcal{T}_{r,n}\cong\mathcal{T}_{r,n-1},\text{ and }u^{*}\mathcal{Q}_{r,n}\cong\mathcal{Q}_{r,n-1}\oplus\mathcal{O}.\end{split}

Hence there are commutative diagrams

(7.4.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R_{r,n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R_{r-1,n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r-1,n-1))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{r,n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{r-1,n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}}

and

(7.4.6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R_{r,n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R_{r,n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n-1)),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{r,n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{r,n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{*}}

where
\alpha\colon R_{r,n}\to R_{r-1,n-1}
sends
y_{i}
to
y_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq n-r
and
\beta\colon R_{r,n}\to R_{r,n-1}
sends
x_{i}
to
x_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq r
.

Lemma 7.4.1
.

Let
X
be the blow-up of
{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)
along the closed immersion
i\colon{\mathrm{Gr}}(r-1,n-1)\to{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)
in
(
7.4.2
) with exceptional divisor
E
.
Then
X
is a
\mathbb{P}^{r}
-bundle on
{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n-1)
, and
(X,E)
is a
(\mathbb{P}^{r},\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
-bundle on
{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n-1)
.

Proof.

We will use the Plücker embedding

{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)\to\mathbb{P}^{\binom{n}{r}-1}

sending
\mathrm{span}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})
to
v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{n}
.
For simplicity of notation, we set
p=\binom{n-1}{r}
and
q=\binom{n-1}{r-1}
.
We express the coordinates of
\mathbb{P}^{\binom{n}{r}-1}=\mathbb{P}^{p+q-1}
by
w_{I}
for subsets

I\subset\{1,\ldots,n\}
such that
|I|=r
.

Let
Y
denote the blow-up of
\mathbb{P}^{p+q-1}
along the closed subscheme defined
by the equations
w_{I}=0
for all
I
containing
n
.
Then
X
is the strict transform of
{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)
in
Y
.
Let
e_{1},\ldots,e_{p+q-1}
be the standard coordinates in
\mathbb{N}^{p+q-1}
.
Note that projective space
\mathbb{P}^{p+q-1}
is the toric variety associated with

\displaystyle\sigma:=\operatorname{Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{p+q-1}),

\displaystyle\operatorname{Cone}(e_{1},\cdots,e_{i-1},e_{i+1},\ldots,e_{p+q-1},-f)\;\;(1\leq i\leq p+q),

where
f:=e_{1}+\cdots+e_{p+q-1}
.
By arranging the coordinates,
we may assume that
Y
is the star subdivision relative to

\operatorname{Cone}(e_{p+1},\ldots,e_{p+q-1},-f)
.
Then
Y
is the toric variety associated with a fan
\Sigma
whose maximal cones are

\displaystyle\sigma,\;\tau_{ij}:=\operatorname{Cone}(e_{1},\cdots,e_{i-1},e_{i+1},\ldots,e_{j-1},e_{j+1},\ldots,e_{p+q-1},-e_{j},-f)

\displaystyle(1\leq i\leq p,\;p+1\leq j\leq p+q),

\displaystyle\rho_{i}:=\operatorname{Cone}(e_{1},\cdots,e_{i-1},e_{i+1},\ldots,e_{p+q-1},-e_{1}-\cdots-e_{p})\;\;(p+1\leq i\leq p+q).

On the other hand,
\mathbb{P}^{p-1}
is the toric variety associated with a fan
\Delta
whose maximal cones are

\displaystyle\sigma^{\prime}:=\operatorname{Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{p-1}),

\displaystyle\tau_{i}^{\prime}:=\operatorname{Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{i-1},e_{i+1},\ldots,e_{p-1},-e_{1}-\cdots-e_{p})\;\;(1\leq i\leq p).

Let
h\colon\mathbb{N}^{p+q-1}\to\mathbb{N}^{p-1}
denote the homomorphism sending
e_{i}
to
e_{i}
if

1\leq i\leq p-1
and to
0
if
i\geq p
.
Then
h(\sigma)\subset\sigma^{\prime}
and
h(\tau_{ij}),h(\rho_{i})\subset\tau_{i}^{\prime}
.
This means that
h
defines a morphism of fans
\Sigma\to\Delta
,
which gives a morphism of scheme
p\colon Y\to\mathbb{P}^{p-1}
.

There is a dense open subscheme
U:=\operatorname{Spec}(A)
of
{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n-1)
,
where

(7.4.7)

A:=\mathbb{Z}[a_{ij}|i\in[1,r],\;j\in[r+1,n-1]].

Similarly,
there is a dense open subscheme
V_{0}:=\operatorname{Spec}(B_{0})
of
{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)
, where

(7.4.8)

B_{0}:=\mathbb{Z}[a_{ij}|i\in[1,r],\;j\in[r+1,n]].

The morphism
p
maps
V_{0}
onto
U
.
Since
p
is proper, this implies that
p
maps
X
onto
{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n-1)
.
Let
q\colon X\to{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n-1)
be the restriction of
p
.

We claim that
q
is a
\mathbb{P}^{r}
-bundle.
Without loss of generality, it suffices to show that
q^{-1}(U)\to U
is a
\mathbb{P}^{r}
-bundle.
There are dense open subschemes
V_{s}:=\operatorname{Spec}(B_{s})
of
{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)
for
1\leq s\leq r
, where

B_{s}:=\mathbb{Z}[b_{ij}|i\in[1,r],\;j\in\{s\}\cup[r+1,n-1]].

We view
B_{s}
as a subring of
\mathbb{Z}(a_{ij}|i\in[1,r],j\in[r+1,n])
.
By applying elementary row operations, we have identifications

(7.4.9)

b_{ij}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1/a_{sn}&\text{if }(i,j)=(s,s),\\
-a_{in}/a_{sn}&\text{if }i\neq s\text{ and }j=s,\\
a_{sj}/a_{sn}&\text{if }i=s\text{ and }j\neq s,\\
a_{ij}-a_{in}a_{sj}/a_{sn}&\text{if }i,j\neq s.\end{array}\right.

There is a dense open subscheme
V_{s}^{\prime}:=\operatorname{Spec}(B_{s}^{\prime})
of
V_{s}\times_{{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)}X
, where

B_{s}^{\prime}:=\mathbb{Z}[b_{ss}]\otimes\mathbb{Z}[b_{sj}/b_{ss}|j\in[r+1,n-1]]\otimes\mathbb{Z}[b_{ij}|i\in[1,r]-\{s\},\;j\in[r+1,n-1]].

With the identifications (
7.4.9
) we have

(7.4.10)

B_{s}^{\prime}=\mathbb{Z}[1/a_{sn}]\otimes\mathbb{Z}[a_{in}/a_{sn}|i\in[1,r]-\{s\}]\otimes\mathbb{Z}[a_{ij}|i\in[1,r],\;j\in[r+1,n-1]].

Combine (
7.4.7
), (
7.4.8
), and (
7.4.10
) to deduce that the union
W
of the open subschemes
V_{0}
and
V_{s}^{\prime}
for
1\leq s\leq r
of
X
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{r}\times U
.
Hence
W
is proper over
U
, so
W=q^{-1}(U)
.
This completes the proof that
q
is a
\mathbb{P}^{r}
-bundle.

The log structure on
q^{-1}(U)\times_{X}(X,E)
is the compactifying log structure associated
with the open immersion
V_{0}\to q^{-1}(U)
.
Hence
(X,E)\to{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n-1)
is a
(\mathbb{P}^{r},\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
-bundle since
V_{0}\cong U\times\mathbb{A}^{r}
.
∎

The images of
i\colon{\mathrm{Gr}}(r-1,n-1)\to{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)
and
u\colon{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n-1)\to{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)
are disjoint.
Hence there is a naturally induced commutative diagram

(7.4.11)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Gr}}(r-1,n-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\mathrm{Bl}}_{{\mathrm{Gr}}(r-1,n-1)}{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n),E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n-1),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

where
p
and
j
are the projections, and
E
is the exceptional divisor.
From (
7.3.8
), we have an exact sequence

(7.4.12)

\begin{split}&\mathbf{E}^{*-2n+2r,*-n+r}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r-1,n-1))\xrightarrow{i_{*}}\mathbf{E}^{**}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n))\\
\xrightarrow{j^{*}}&\mathbf{E}^{**}({\mathrm{Bl}}_{{\mathrm{Gr}}(r-1,n-1)}{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n),E)\to\mathbf{E}^{*-2n+2r+1,*-n+r}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r-1,n-1)).\end{split}

Since
p
is a
(\mathbb{P}^{r},\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
-bundle,
p^{*}
is an isomorphism owing to Proposition
3.2.18
.
From
pv=\operatorname{id}
, we deduce that
v^{*}
is an isomorphism.
Hence (
7.4.12
) becomes an exact sequence

(7.4.13)

\begin{split}&\mathbf{E}^{*-2n+2r,*-n+r}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r-1,n-1))\xrightarrow{i_{*}}\mathbf{E}^{**}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n))\\
\xrightarrow{u^{*}}&\mathbf{E}^{**}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n-1))\xrightarrow{\delta}\mathbf{E}^{*-2n+2r,*-n+r+1}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r-1,n-1))\end{split}

Lemma 7.4.2
.

Let
u,u^{\prime}\colon{\mathrm{Gr}}(r-1,n)\rightrightarrows{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)
be the two closed immersions given by the inclusions

\mathcal{O}^{n-1}\rightrightarrows\mathcal{O}^{n}
sending
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1})
to
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1},0)
and

(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-2},0,x_{n-1})
.
Then
u^{*}=u^{\prime*}\colon\mathbf{E}^{**}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n))\to\mathbf{E}^{**}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n-1))
.

Proof.

Let
u^{\prime\prime}\colon{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n-1)\rightarrow{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)
be the closed immersion given by
the inclusion
\mathcal{O}^{n-1}\to\mathcal{O}^{n}

sending
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1})
to
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1},x_{n-1})
.
As in (
7.4.11
),
there is a morphism
v^{\prime\prime}\colon{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n-1)\to({\mathrm{Bl}}_{{\mathrm{Gr}}(r-1.n-1)}{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n),E)
such that
pv^{\prime\prime}=\operatorname{id}
and
u^{\prime\prime}=jv^{\prime\prime}
.
We also have
pv=\operatorname{id}
and
u=jv
.

Since
p
is a
(\mathbb{P}^{r},\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
-bundle,
p^{*}
is an isomorphism.
Hence, we get
v^{*}=v^{\prime\prime*}
.
Compose with
j^{*}
to deduce
u^{*}=u^{\prime\prime*}
.
A similar argument shows that
u^{\prime*}=u^{\prime\prime*}
.
∎

Lemma 7.4.3
.

Let
i\colon{\mathrm{Gr}}(r-1,n-1)\to{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)
be the closed immersion in (
7.4.2
).
If
\eta\colon{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)\to{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)
is the automorphism that permutes the last two coordinates,
then
i^{*}=(\eta i)^{*}\colon\mathbf{E}^{**}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n))\to\mathbf{E}^{**}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r-1,n-1))
.

Proof.

Use the duality isomorphisms
{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)\cong{\mathrm{Gr}}(n-r,n)
,
{\mathrm{Gr}}(r-1,n-1)\cong{\mathrm{Gr}}(n-r,n-1)
,
and apply Lemma
7.4.2
.
∎

Lemma 7.4.4
.

Let
i\colon{\mathrm{Gr}}(r-1,n-1)\to{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)
be the closed immersion in (
7.4.2
).
Then we have the formula

i_{*}i^{*}=(-)\smile c_{n-r}(\mathcal{Q}_{r,n}).

Proof.

There is a cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Gr}}(r-1,n-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Gr}}(r+1,n+1).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}

Here
a
and
a^{\prime}
are induced by
V\mapsto V\oplus\mathcal{O}
and
V\mapsto\eta(V\oplus\mathcal{O})
,
respectively,
where
\eta
is the automorphism that permutes the last two coordinates.
Proposition
7.3.18
gives the formula

(7.4.14)

a^{\prime*}a_{*}=i_{*}i^{*}.

The tangent bundle
{\mathrm{T}}_{{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)}
over
{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)
participates in a canonical isomorphism of vector bundles

{\mathrm{T}}_{{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)}\cong\mathcal{T}_{r,n}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{Q}_{r,n}.

Combine the exact sequence

0\to{\mathrm{T}}_{{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)}\to a^{*}{\mathrm{T}}_{{\mathrm{Gr}}(r+1,n+1)}\to{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r+1,n+1))\to 0

with (
7.4.4
) to have

(7.4.15)

{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r+1,n+1))\cong\mathcal{O}\otimes\mathcal{Q}_{r,n}\cong\mathcal{Q}_{r,n}.

There is a cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Gr}}(r+1,n+1),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

Combine Proposition
7.3.18
and (
7.4.15
) to conclude the formula

(7.4.16)

a^{*}a_{*}=(-)\smile c_{n-r}(\mathcal{Q}_{r,n}).

Since
a^{*}=a^{\prime*}
owing to Lemma
7.4.3
,
we conclude by comparing (
7.4.14
) and (
7.4.16
).
∎

For any bigraded ring
\mathbf{A}^{**}
,
we write

\mathbf{A}^{**}(m,n):=\mathbf{A}^{*+m,*+n}

to indicate the shift by
(m,n)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

Lemma 7.4.5
.

There is a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R_{r-1,n-1}(-2n+2r,-n+r)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R_{r,n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{*-2n+2r,*-n+r}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r-1,n-1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{r-1,n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{r,n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}}

Here
\gamma\colon R_{r-1,n-1}\to R_{r,n}
is the multiplication map with
y_{n-r}
.

Proof.

Combined with the commutative square (
7.4.5
), we obtain

(7.4.17)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R_{r,n}(-2n+2r,-n+r)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{*-2n+2r,*-n+r}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R_{r,n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{*-2n+2r,*-n+r}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes(\gamma\circ\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{r,n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{r,n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}i^{*}}

Since the homomorphism
\alpha\colon R_{r,n}\to R_{r-1,n-1}
is surjective, it suffices to show that the square (
7.4.17
) commutes.
This follows from Lemma
7.4.4
.
∎

Theorem 7.4.6
.

There is a naturally induced ring isomorphism

\varphi_{r,n}\colon\mathbf{E}^{**}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R_{r,n}\to\mathbf{E}^{**}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n)).

Proof.

Theorem
7.2.6
shows the claim for
n=1
.
We proceed by induction on
n>1
.
For simplicity of notation, we set
s:=n-r
.
Combine (
7.4.6
) and Lemma
7.4.5
to deduce the commutative diagram

(7.4.18)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R_{r-1,n-1}(-2s,-s)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R_{r,n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R_{r,n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{*-2s,*-s}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r-1,n-1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}^{**}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n-1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{r-1,n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{r,n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{r,n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{*}}

There is an exact sequence of singular cohomology groups of complex Grassmannian manifolds

0\to H^{*-s}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r-1,\mathbb{C}^{n-1}))\to H^{*}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r,\mathbb{C}^{n}))\to H^{*}({\mathrm{Gr}}(r,\mathbb{C}^{n-1}))\to 0,

which gives an exact sequence

0\to R_{r-1,n-1}\xrightarrow{\gamma}R_{r,n}\xrightarrow{\beta}R_{r,n-1}\to 0.

Hence the upper row in (
7.4.18
) is exact.
By induction,
\varphi_{r-1,n-1}
and
\varphi_{r,n-1}
are isomorphisms.
Since
1\otimes\beta
is surjective,
u^{*}
is surjective.
Together with the exactness of (
7.4.13
), we deduce that the lower row in (
7.4.18
) is exact.
The five lemma finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 7.4.7
.

By a similar argument, one can show that the naturally induced homomorphism

(7.4.19)

\mathbf{E}^{**}(X)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R_{r,n}\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(X\times{\mathrm{Gr}}(r,n))

is an isomorphism for every
X\in\mathbf{lSm}/k
.

Lemma 7.4.8
.

If
\mathop{\rm colim}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{F}_{i}
is a filtered colimit in
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(S)
, then there is a canonical exact sequence

0\to\mathop{\rm lim^{1}}_{i}\mathbf{E}^{*-1,*}(\mathcal{F}_{i})\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathop{\rm colim}_{i}\mathcal{F}_{i})\to\mathop{\rm lim}_{i}\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathcal{F}_{i})\to 0.

Proof.

In
\mathrm{log}\mathcal{SH}(S)
,
there is a canonical cofiber sequence

\bigoplus_{j=0}^{i-1}\mathcal{F}_{j}\xrightarrow{f_{i}}\bigoplus_{j=0}^{i}\mathcal{F}_{j}\to\mathcal{F}_{i}

for all integer
i\geq 1
,
where
f_{i}=\operatorname{id}-\mathrm{shift}
.
Take
\mathop{\rm colim}_{i}
to obtain a canonical cofiber sequence

\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{F}_{i}\to\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{F}_{i}\to\mathop{\rm colim}_{i}\mathcal{F}_{i}.

By applying
\mathbf{E}^{**}
, we obtain the desired exact sequence

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{Coker}\big(\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{E}^{*-1,*}(\mathcal{F}_{i})\to\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{E}^{*-1,*}(\mathcal{F}_{i})\big)\to\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathop{\rm colim}_{i}\mathcal{F}_{i})

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{Ker}\big(\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathcal{F}_{i})\to\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{E}^{**}(\mathcal{F}_{i})\big)\to 0.

∎

Let
\mathcal{T}_{r}
and
\mathcal{T}
be the tautological bundles on
{\mathrm{Gr}}(r)
and
{\mathrm{Gr}}
,
respectively.

Corollary 7.4.9
.

There are canonical isomorphisms

(7.4.20)
